**What's hardcoded today:**
- `RPC ERP Dataflows - Hourly Incremental`: each `RefreshDataflow` activity hardcodes `workspaceId: 0cfedeac-...` (4 times) plus each dataflow's own `dataflowId`.

**How Fabric actually supports this:** Pipelines bind to Variable Library values through a `libraryVariables` block at the pipeline's `properties` level (peer of `activities`), like:
```json
"libraryVariables": {
  "Variables_SemanticModelId": { "type": "String", "variableName": "SemanticModelId", "libraryName": "Variables" }
}
```
then consumed in activity JSON as `"@pipeline().libraryVariables.Variables_SemanticModelId"`. This is confirmed by Microsoft's docs and a couple of independent write-ups, so I'm confident in the mechanism.

**Two things I need your call on before editing:**

1. **The `groupId`/`datasetId` in the semantic model refresh already line up exactly** with `SemanticModelWorkspaceId`/`SemanticModelId` in `Prod.json` — those are a clean, correct parameterization target since they genuinely vary Dev/Test/Prod.

2. **`sqlEndpointId: 12af4556-060f-4965-9e77-dee416ea1f77`** in the pipeline does **not** match `LakehouseSqlEndpointId`'s value in `variables.json` (`4d8dd180-f17c-4252-9b47-fbe0c61d506f`), and that variable has no per-environment override in Dev/Test/Prod. Either the variable was set up with a stale/wrong value, or it refers to something other than what's currently in the pipeline. I don't want to silently pick one — can you confirm which GUID is actually correct for the Lakehouse SQL endpoint?

3. **The repeated `workspaceId: 0cfedeac-...`** on Dataflow/Pipeline activities is the workspace the ETL items themselves live in — it's constant across Dev/Test/Prod in this design (only the destination Lakehouse schema and Semantic Model vary), and it doesn't match any existing variable (`LakehouseWorkspaceId` is a different, also-constant GUID for the Lakehouse's own workspace). Fabric has no reliable "current workspace" system-variable expression for these activity fields, so the options are: (a) leave these hardcoded since they never actually change, or (b) add a new Variable Library variable for it anyway for consistency/documentation purposes. My instinct is (a) — no real reason to add indirection for a value that's genuinely constant — but wanted to check since you may have plans to eventually split environments into separate workspaces.

This looks right.  If this is going to promote to a next environment, won't we need dataflowId to be parameterized?  Or is this handled in some automated way by Fabric?

---

**Claude**

Good question — and the answer is nuanced because it depends on which of two different "environment" mechanisms you're actually promoting through.

**Fabric does not auto-remap embedded item-ID references.** Auto-bind in Fabric Deployment Pipelines only covers a narrow set of native relationships (mainly a semantic model/report's data-source binding, and a notebook's default lakehouse). It explicitly does **not** rebind GUIDs that are just embedded as activity properties in a pipeline's JSON — `dataflowId` in `RefreshDataflow`, `pipelineId` in `InvokePipeline`, etc. Per Microsoft's own docs: *"autobinding after deployment doesn't take place... you must rebind the items after deployment by changing the parameter value or using parameter rules."* So if you promote by copying these items into a separate Dev/Test/Prod workspace, `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` would come along still pointing at the *original* workspace's items unless something rewrites them.

**But — check whether that scenario actually applies to this project.** Looking at what's already in the Variable Library: `LakehouseWorkspaceId`, `LakehouseId`, and now `ETLWorkspaceId` have **no per-environment override** in Dev/Test/Prod. Only `SchemaName`, `SemanticModelWorkspaceId`, and `SemanticModelId` change per stage. That tells me the current design is: **one single set of Dataflows/Pipelines, in one workspace, writing to a different schema and refreshing a different semantic model** depending on which value-set is active — not three separate copies of the Dataflows/Pipelines living in three separate workspaces. Under that model, `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` never change either, for the same reason `ETLWorkspaceId` doesn't — there's only one copy.

So:
- If you're staying with this "one workspace, switch the active value-set" model → `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` don't need parameterizing, same reasoning as the ETL workspace ID.
- If you're planning to move to real multi-workspace Fabric Deployment Pipelines (separate Dev/Test/Prod workspaces, each with its own copy of these Dataflow/Pipeline items) → yes, you'd need it, and the "item reference" variable type (which is built for exactly this — storing a workspace+item pair) unfortunately **doesn't support pipelines yet** — its docs list only Lakehouse shortcuts, user data functions, and notebooks as supported consumers. You'd fall back to plain String variables per dataflow/pipeline (same `libraryVariables` pattern I just used for workspaceId), with one value-set override per stage once each stage actually has its own deployed copy of the item.

Which model are you actually planning to promote through?

I'll hold off on that until the environment-count question below is settled, since it affects whether we're wiring up two or three value-sets.

**On whether you need a Test stage for the EL piece:**

My take: for a pure movement layer (no transform logic, that's dbt's job), **Dev → Prod is usually enough** — skip Test. A separate Test stage earns its keep when at least one of these is true, and none of them sound like they apply here from what you've described:

- **Source schema is unstable** — you need a safe place to validate extraction against a schema change before it hits Prod.
- **Multiple people touch the pipelines concurrently** and need an integration gate before merging to Prod.
- **A bad run is expensive or hard to undo** — e.g., it's append-only, or downstream consumers can't tolerate a bad/duplicate incremental load. (Worth checking: your dataflows use `UpdateMethod = Replace` on the destination, which is forgiving — a bad run just needs a re-run, not a cleanup.)
- **Compliance/change-control** requires a formal staging gate.

The real cost of Test isn't the workspace itself, it's keeping it *meaningfully* representative — same source connections, similar data shape — and actually exercising it before promoting. If Test would just be "Dev with a different label" that gets rubber-stamped, it's pure overhead (extra workspace cost, extra value-set to maintain, extra deployment-pipeline stage) without real risk reduction.

**You**

There are going to be a lot of dataflows.  I think we need some system for managing them, so we don't have to create them in each environment and then pull the ids.  That would be unmanageable.

---

**Claude**

Good instinct — hand-copying GUIDs per dataflow per environment is exactly the workflow Microsoft built a tool to eliminate. Two real options, and I'd lean toward the first:

**1. Adopt `fabric-cicd`** (Microsoft's OSS Python deploy tool) as the actual promotion mechanism from git → workspace, instead of manually creating items in Fabric and copying IDs. Its `parameter.yml` supports name-based reference resolution — e.g. a `RefreshDataflow` activity's `dataflowId` gets a rule like `$items.Dataflow.<Dataflow Name>.id`, and the tool resolves that to whatever GUID it just created/found in the *target* workspace at deploy time. You write the rule once, referencing the dataflow by its stable name (which you already have — it's the folder name), and it never needs touching again as you add environments or recreate items. This is the standard companion to what you're already doing (git-integrated Fabric items) — git integration handles author→branch, `fabric-cicd` handles branch→workspace.

**2. Runtime name lookup instead of deploy-time resolution** — each pipeline calls the Fabric REST API at execution time (a Web activity hitting `/workspaces/{id}/items?type=Dataflow`, filtered by name) to resolve the dataflow's ID in whatever workspace it's currently running in, and feeds that into the refresh activity via a pipeline variable. No GUIDs stored anywhere, ever — but it adds a lookup activity per dataflow reference to every pipeline, plus a service principal with API permissions, and a bit of runtime latency/fragility on every run.

My recommendation: **go with `fabric-cicd`**. It solves the problem at deploy time (once per promotion) rather than at run time (every single execution), keeps the pipelines themselves simple, and is the tool Microsoft explicitly built for this. The tradeoff is setup cost — you'd need a small Python-based deploy script/CI job and a `parameter.yml`, and a decision on how Dev/Prod deploys get triggered (manual run, GitHub Action, etc.).

This is a bigger adoption than a file edit, though — want me to sketch out what the `parameter.yml` and deploy setup would actually look like for your two pipelines before you commit to it, or do you want to weigh this against option 2 more first?

**Claude**

Good questions, and the answer changes the picture a bit.

**On ADO Pipelines:** No, `fabric-cicd` doesn't require ADO Pipelines (the YAML CI/CD runner) at all — it's just a Python package. ADO Pipelines is one *optional* way to trigger it automatically on merge; you could just as easily run it by hand from your machine (`python deploy.py`), from a scheduled task, or skip automation entirely for now. Given your history with ADO pipeline setup being painful, I'd suggest starting with a manual script and only wiring up ADO triggers later if you actually want auto-promote-on-merge. On the "has it improved" question: yes, somewhat — Microsoft has an official ADO + `fabric-cicd` tutorial now, and service-principal auth via an ADO Service Connection is supported (which used to mean hand-managing/rotating secrets). But that's specifically the Fabric-auth pain point getting easier, not ADO YAML pipelines themselves becoming fundamentally simpler.

**On a custom script vs. `fabric-cicd`:** I'd steer away from hand-rolling this, but not away from Python. `fabric-cicd` already supports exactly your three item types (Dataflow Gen2, DataPipeline, VariableLibrary — confirmed in its supported-items list) and already implements name-based reference resolution (`$items.Dataflow.<Dataflow Name>.id` in `parameter.yml`) plus writing values into Variable Library overrides. Building that yourself means reimplementing Fabric API auth, item create/update/publish semantics (base64-encoded definition parts, etc.), and dependency ordering — real effort, and `fabric-cicd` has already worked through the edge cases. The Python-script fit you're imagining still works fine: `pip install fabric-cicd`, write a short script (in the same spirit as `standardize_dataflows.py`) that imports it and calls its publish function with your `parameter.yml` — no ADO required to get value from it.

**On dataflows that don't have an ID yet:** Yes, this works, and it's the whole point. You never pre-populate an ID anywhere — Fabric can't assign one until the item is actually created. What happens instead: on a deploy run, the tool checks the target workspace for an item with that name; if it's missing, it creates it (Fabric mints the GUID at that moment), captures the ID from the API response, and immediately uses it to resolve any `$items.Dataflow.<Name>.id` references in the same run — including in the pipeline that refreshes it. So the first-ever deploy to Dev creates everything and wires up the references in one pass, with no manual GUID-copying step ever.

Want me to sketch what your `parameter.yml` and a minimal deploy script would actually look like for the two pipelines + five dataflows you have now?

**You**

Currently deployment involves going into the workspace and using the source control menu to sync items to the workspace.  This would be circumventing that process for new items, right?

---

**Claude**

Yes, your read is correct — if you use `fabric-cicd`'s deploy function to push items into a workspace, that's a different write path than the "Source Control → Sync" button in the workspace. Both ultimately call Fabric APIs to create/update items, but they're not the same mechanism, and running both against the same workspace would have them fighting over the same items. Adopting `fabric-cicd` as I described it would mean the Prod (and maybe Dev) workspace stops being synced via the UI and instead gets deployed by the script.

Given that you'd rather not disrupt what's already working, here's a narrower option that keeps your current process completely untouched:

**Keep syncing via the Source Control UI exactly as you do now** for both Dev and Prod (e.g., a `dev` branch → Dev workspace, `main` → Prod workspace, promoted by merging and clicking Sync — same motion you already use, just extended to a second workspace). That handles item creation/updates the way it always has.

**Add a small, purely read-only companion script** — in the same spirit as `standardize_dataflows.py` — that runs *after* you've synced. It doesn't create or touch anything in the workspace; it just calls the Fabric REST API's "list items" endpoint against a given workspace, matches items by display name (which is already sitting in each item's `.platform` file), and writes the resulting IDs into that environment's `valueSets/*.json` overrides for you. That's the one manual step you're trying to eliminate — "open Fabric, click into each item, copy the GUID from the URL" — replaced by running a script after each sync.

That's meaningfully smaller than adopting `fabric-cicd`: no auth-for-write, no item creation/ordering logic, no change to your deployment mechanism at all — just a lookup-and-patch step. The tradeoff versus `fabric-cicd` is that it's still two steps (sync in the UI, then run the script) rather than one, and you're maintaining that script yourself rather than relying on a maintained library — but it never touches or bypasses the sync process you already trust.

Want me to build that lookup script, and also go ahead and add the `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` variables (with placeholder overrides) to the Variable Library so it has somewhere to write the values once you run it?

**You**

Is there a reason to think that the fabric-cicd option would cause problems for the workspace source control piece?

---

**Claude**

Yes — there's a concrete, documented reason for concern, not just a theoretical one.

The mechanism: `fabric-cicd` works by reading item definitions from git and pushing them into the target workspace via direct API calls, applying your `parameter.yml` substitutions along the way (real GUIDs, environment-specific values, etc.). That means the content it writes into the workspace is **not byte-identical to what's sitting in the git branch** — it's the git content *plus* parameterization. Fabric's native Git Integration, on the other hand, works by comparing the live workspace content against the last-known git-synced state and flags a **Conflict** the moment they diverge (its docs describe this as: "a conflict occurs when changes are made to the same item in both the workspace and the remote Git repository," and once conflicted, further updates are disabled until you resolve it). If a workspace is both fabric-cicd's deployment target *and* natively git-connected to that same branch, those two systems would fight — every fabric-cicd deployment would leave the workspace looking, to native Git Integration, like it has unsynced/conflicting changes, since the parameterized content never matches the raw git files.

This is exactly why the documented reference architecture for `fabric-cicd` treats it as one-or-the-other, not both: the **Dev** workspace stays natively git-connected (developers commit/sync there via the Source Control pane, same as today), while **PPE/Prod-type workspaces become CI/CD-managed deployment targets, updated only by the pipeline** — explicitly not also wired up to native Source Control at the same time.

So to directly answer your question: yes, if you adopted `fabric-cicd` for Prod, you'd want to disconnect Prod's native Git Integration and let the script own that workspace exclusively — Dev could stay exactly as it is today. This is another point in favor of the narrower companion-script approach I suggested: it never touches the workspace's write path at all, so there's no conflict with Source Control to worry about, on Dev or Prod.

---

**You**

Is it possible to update the repo after creating the content in the workspace so that it does become byte-identical?

---

**Claude**

Technically yes — the Source Control pane's "Commit" direction pushes the workspace's current state back into git, and you could do that after a workspace's items diverge from what's in the branch. But I don't think that's actually the right tool here, and there's a cleaner answer once I separate two things I was conflating earlier.

**The `libraryVariables` bindings we already did (workspaceId, sqlEndpointId, groupId, datasetId) never actually create divergence.** `@pipeline().libraryVariables.Variables_ETLWorkspaceId` is resolved by Fabric **at pipeline run time**, not at sync time — the literal string `"@pipeline().libraryVariables.Variables_ETLWorkspaceId"` sits in `pipeline-content.json` in git, and that exact same string gets synced into Dev's workspace and Prod's workspace unchanged. Each workspace just resolves it differently when a pipeline actually *runs*, based on its own active value-set. So there's no conflict to fix for this class of value — git, Dev, and Prod are already byte-identical, and native Source Control sync handles it perfectly with zero help from `fabric-cicd`.

**The real gap is only `dataflowId`/`pipelineId`, and it's a discovery problem, not a sync problem.** If we bind those the same way (a plain String Variable Library variable per dataflow, resolved via `@pipeline().libraryVariables.X`), the same logic applies — no divergence, same file synced everywhere. The only thing missing is *knowing what GUID to put in the Dev/Prod value-set override* for a dataflow that was just created in that workspace for the first time. That's a one-time lookup, not an ongoing content-sync issue.

Where "commit workspace back to git to make it byte-identical" *would* actually apply is a different design: if you (or `fabric-cicd`) baked a literal, real GUID directly into `pipeline-content.json`'s `dataflowId` field instead of using a Variable Library binding — skipping parameterization for that field entirely. Then yes, that workspace's content would genuinely diverge from the git template, and you could theoretically commit it back. I'd avoid that though: committing one environment's resolved values back into your shared template branch overwrites the general-purpose template with Prod-specific (or Dev-specific) content, so the next sync to the *other* environment would deploy the wrong GUIDs. Making that safe requires a separate "resolved" branch per environment, which reintroduces exactly the branch-drift maintenance burden you're trying to avoid.

So the path that actually avoids all of this: keep `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` on the same `libraryVariables` binding as everything else, and use the companion lookup script only to **populate the Variable Library's value-set JSON** (`Dev.json`/`Prod.json`) with the discovered GUID after a new item's first sync. That's a small, expected diff in files whose entire purpose is holding per-environment values — you then commit it through the exact same native Source Control flow you already use today. No bypass, no conflict, nothing structural changes.

Want me to go ahead and add the `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` variables to the Variable Library now, and build that lookup script?

---

**You**

What do you mean "after a new item's first sync"?  Do you mean that it has to run before you can pull the id values?

---

**Claude**

No — I mean the item just needs to **exist** in the workspace, not run. Two different things:

- **Sync** = the Source Control pane's "Update" action, which creates the actual Dataflow/Pipeline item in the workspace from the git-tracked definition. Fabric assigns the item's GUID the moment it's **created** — that happens during sync, not during execution.
- **Run** = actually executing/refreshing the dataflow or triggering the pipeline. This has nothing to do with the ID — an item that's never been run once still has a perfectly valid ID from the moment it was created.

So the sequence is: you sync a new Dev/Prod workspace to the branch → Fabric creates the five Dataflows and two Pipelines as real items with new GUIDs → the lookup script can immediately query the Fabric API and find those GUIDs, whether or not anything has ever actually run. "First sync" just meant "the first time this item exists in that workspace," to distinguish it from later syncs of the *same* already-existing item, which wouldn't change its ID.

**On what's actually there today:**

None of these four YAML files touch the `ETL` folder at all — their `paths: include:` filters are `DBT`, `Semantic Models`, `Reports`. What they *do* today is: trigger dbt Cloud jobs via REST calls, and deploy Semantic Models using Tabular Editor's CLI plus the `PowerBIActions` ADO Marketplace extension. There's a change-detection pattern (diffing the commit to see which top-level folder changed, then conditionally running steps) that's reusable, and the branch model is confirmed as `master`→Prod, `dev`→Dev — `Deploy_v2.yml` also shows there used to be `Testing_Dev`/`Testing_Prod` branches for the Semantic Model deploy, consistent with the Test tier you just decided to drop. So there's precedent for the branch-per-environment shape, but genuinely nothing built yet for Fabric Dataflows/Pipelines/Variable Library — this would be new, not an extension of existing logic.

**What a full ADO-managed deployment for ETL would actually require:**

1. **A new pipeline (or new stage) triggered on `ETL/` changes** — same shape as the existing ones: trigger on `dev`/`master`, `paths: include: ETL`, reusing the changed-path detection pattern already in `Deploy_v2.yml` if you want granular runs.

2. **Service principal + Fabric permissions (one-time, manual, outside the repo)** — a Microsoft Entra app registration, a Fabric admin enabling "service principals can use Fabric APIs," and that principal added as a member on both the Dev and Prod workspaces. Its client ID/secret need to live in an ADO secret variable group — this is the same kind of secret-handling gap as the dbt token above, so worth doing properly this time.

3. **A Python step in the pipeline** — `UsePythonVersion@0`, `pip install fabric-cicd`, then a small deploy script (in the spirit of `standardize_dataflows.py`) that calls `fabric_cicd.publish_all_items(...)` pointed at the Dev or Prod workspace ID depending on branch — mirroring the `${{ if contains(variables['Build.SourceBranchName'], 'master') }}` pattern already used for `databaseId`/`workspaceName` in the other pipelines.

4. **A `parameter.yml`** defining the target workspace per branch, plus resolution rules for the pieces that can't be known ahead of time — specifically `dataflowId`/`pipelineId`, using `$items.Dataflow.<Name>.id`-style expressions.

5. **One real design decision:** where do those resolved `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` values end up? Baked directly into the deployed `pipeline-content.json` (simpler, but makes that workspace's content diverge from git — reinforcing that it shouldn't stay natively git-connected), or written into the Variable Library's value-sets via the Fabric API and committed back to the repo (keeps git and both workspaces byte-identical, consistent with how we already set up `workspaceId`/`sqlEndpointId`/etc. to resolve at runtime). Given everything we've built this session, I'd lean toward the second — it's more moving parts up front but keeps the whole system on one consistent pattern.

6. **A bootstrap wrinkle:** the very first run against each workspace is different from steady-state — nothing exists yet, so that run has to create all five Dataflows and both Pipelines from scratch and discover their IDs for the first time, before any subsequent run just updates existing items.

This is genuinely comparable in size to what's already built for dbt/Semantic Models — not a small add-on. Given your ADO history, I'd suggest proving out steps 3–5 as a script you can run manually first (same as I suggested for the lookup-only version), and only wrapping it in the actual YAML trigger once you trust it — that de-risks the part you said has bitten you before.

**What rotating/removing actually involves — two separate steps, and only one of them is a repo edit:**

1. **Revoke/regenerate the real credential at its source.** This is the part that actually neutralizes the risk — deleting the token from the YAML file doesn't invalidate it, since it's still valid and usable by anyone who can read git history.
   - The dbt tokens: dbt Cloud → your account's API/service tokens settings → find and revoke those two specific tokens, issue a new one if something still needs it (likely just the `DBT` variable group's `APIKey`, which is already how the live pipeline does it correctly).
   - The ADO PAT: Azure DevOps → your user settings → Personal Access Tokens → identify and revoke it. This one I'd prioritize — a PAT scoped to this org could grant more than read access depending on how it was created, and it's the kind of thing worth revoking immediately regardless of whether you clean up the file today.

2. **Remove the plaintext values from the files themselves.** Straightforward — either delete the three dead files outright (since you said only `pipeline_build_dbt.yml` is in use) or edit out the hardcoded tokens if you want to keep any of the logic for reference. This is a normal repo edit, low risk, I can do it once you tell me which files you want gone vs. kept.

3. **Optional, more invasive: scrubbing git history.** Removing the token from the current file leaves it recoverable from any old commit (`git log -p`, checking out a prior revision, etc.). Truly erasing it from history means rewriting commits (`git filter-repo` or BFG) and force-pushing — which rewrites SHAs and requires everyone to re-clone. That's only worth doing if you're worried about who has/had read access to this repo's history; it's not necessary for security once the credential itself is revoked, since a revoked token is harmless even if it's still visible in an old commit.

Given that, my recommendation: revoke both tokens today (steps you'd do outside the repo, I can't do this part), and let me know whether to delete or clean up the three unused YAML files — I wouldn't bother rewriting git history unless you have a specific reason to.

**You**

Should I generate a personal DBT token or a service token?

---

**Claude**

**Service token**, not personal. This is a CI/CD system calling the API on the pipeline's behalf, not you interactively — the textbook case for a service token.

Reasons:
- **Lifecycle independence**: a personal token is tied to your dbt Cloud user account. If your role changes, SSO/password resets happen, or you're ever offboarded, the token dies with it and the pipeline breaks with no obvious cause. A service token has its own lifecycle, unrelated to any individual.
- **Least privilege**: service tokens let you scope permissions narrowly. This pipeline only needs to trigger a job run and poll its status — assign it **Job Admin** (or the narrowest role that covers "trigger + read run status"), not Account Admin. A personal token carries whatever access you personally have, which is almost certainly broader than the pipeline needs.
- **Auditability**: name it something like `ADO-CI-DBT-Build` so anyone (including future-you) can immediately tell what it's for from the token list — a personal token just shows your name, which doesn't communicate its actual purpose.

Once you've generated it and updated the "DBT" variable group's `APIKey`, let me know and I'll clean up the leftover literal token in `azure-pipelines.yml`.

---

**You**

1. **Check both lists** in dbt Cloud: Account Settings → Service Tokens, and your own profile's Personal API Token (if you still have one set up).
2. **Look at "last used."** The leaked token has been the live credential since 2025-09-24, so it should show ongoing/recent use matching however often the "DBT Build" pipeline actually runs (daily, on merges, whatever your trigger cadence is) — right up until you swap the ADO variable group over to the new one. That's the strongest signal: whichever token has been actively used recently is almost certainly it, especially if the rest of the list is old/stale test tokens.
3. **Cross-check creation date** against 2025-09-24 or earlier — the token had to exist by then to have been hardcoded into that commit.
4. **Don't revoke yet.** Once you've dropped the new service token into the ADO Library and saved it, trigger the "DBT Build" pipeline once (manually if you can, or wait for its next natural run) and confirm it succeeds. Only after you see a successful run on the new token should you go back and revoke the old one — that gives you a safety net in case you misidentify which one it is, and it also gives you one more confirming data point: after the swap, the old token's "last used" should stop advancing while the new one's starts.

Once the new token's in place and you've confirmed a successful run, let me know and I'll clean up the leftover literal copy of the old token sitting in `azure-pipelines.yml`.

**You**

Let's get back to the ADO stuff.  Currently the dbt yml file uses inline powershell to do everything.  It's not a lot of code, but if we moved other things to python it would mean two languages in use.  If we did the parameterization work in python, would it be inline like this or a separate script?

---

**Claude**

I'd do it as a separate script, not inline — and I'd actually recommend that regardless of the "two languages" question.

The core reason: Python and YAML are both whitespace-sensitive, in different ways. PowerShell inline in a YAML `script: |` block works fine because PowerShell doesn't care about indentation — but Python's control flow *is* indentation, so nesting `if`/`for` blocks inside a YAML block-scalar means fighting two indentation rules at once, and it gets fragile fast as the script grows past a few lines (which this one will, given it needs to loop over dataflows, call `fabric_cicd`, handle the target-workspace-per-branch logic). A separate `.py` file also means you can actually run and test it locally (`python etl_deploy.py --environment dev`) before it ever touches the pipeline, get normal editor/linting support, and get clean diffs when the deploy logic changes instead of reformatted YAML noise. The YAML step itself then stays thin: install `fabric-cicd`, run the script, done — same shape as `standardize_dataflows.py` already sitting in this repo, which you also run standalone.

On the two-languages concern specifically: that's really a consequence of picking `fabric-cicd` (a Python-only tool) at all, not of inline-vs-separate-file — you'd have that tradeoff either way. And it's a smaller addition than it might feel, since Python's already present in this exact repo for `standardize_dataflows.py`; this would extend that existing footprint rather than introduce a new one.

---

**You**

Ok.  So it seems like there are two options: 
1. Build all ETL deployments in fabric-cicd (and potentially other things also)
2. Build just the variable swap in fabric-cicd

Is that right?

Does fabric-cicd allow deployment of semantic models and reports also?

---

**Claude**

Yes, that's the right framing, with one sharpening: Option 2 isn't really "fabric-cicd, scoped down" — it's using fabric-cicd's underlying API calls (or hand-rolled ones) purely to *discover* newly-created item IDs and write them into the Variable Library's value-sets, while the actual deployment of Dataflow/Pipeline content keeps happening exactly as it does today, through the workspace's native Source Control sync. fabric-cicd itself doesn't really have a "resolve-only, don't deploy" mode — its whole design is end-to-end publish. So Option 2 means treating it (or the raw Fabric REST API) as a small utility, not as the deployment engine.

Option 1 is the bigger commitment: fabric-cicd becomes the actual mechanism that creates/updates the Dataflows, Pipelines, and Variable Library in each workspace, which means those workspaces stop being natively git-connected (per the conflict we discussed earlier).

**On Semantic Models and Reports** — yes, both are supported. fabric-cicd's item-type list includes `Report` and `SemanticModel` alongside `DataPipeline`, `Dataflow`, and `VariableLibrary`. That's actually a relevant connection given what we found in the ADO files: the Tabular-Editor-CLI + `PowerBIActions` extension approach in `azure-pipelines.yml`/`pipeline_build_dataset.yml` isn't even currently running. If you went with Option 1 for the ETL side, fabric-cicd could eventually absorb that Semantic Model/Report deployment too — one consistent tool across everything instead of the current mix of Tabular Editor, a Power BI ADO extension, and native git sync. That's not a reason to do Option 1 now, just worth knowing it's not a dead end if you later want to consolidate.

---

**You**

There's no good solution for editing dataflows and pipelines outside Fabric.  How could we do editing of these objects in Fabric while still using fabric-cicd?

---

**Claude**

The resolution is to stop thinking of it as "edit in Fabric vs. use fabric-cicd" and instead split the workspaces by role: **one workspace stays the editing surface, the rest become pure deployment targets.**

Concretely, this matches the reference architecture we touched on earlier: **Dev keeps its native Git Integration, exactly as it works today.** You still open the Dataflow in Power Query, still open the Pipeline canvas, edit live in the Fabric UI against Dev — nothing changes there, because Dev is the one workspace you're allowed to hand-edit. When you're happy with a change, you commit it through the Source Control pane like always, into the `dev` branch.

**Prod (and any future Test tier) never gets hand-edited at all.** It's a fabric-cicd deployment target only — every time you promote, fabric-cicd reads from git and overwrites Prod's items to match. Since fabric-cicd doesn't maintain state (it just reconciles the workspace to whatever's in the branch on each run), any direct edit made in Prod's Fabric UI would just get silently clobbered on the next deploy anyway — so the workflow enforces "always edit in Dev, promote through git" almost by construction, not by asking people to remember a rule.

The one practical gap: nothing stops a person from *opening* Prod in the Fabric UI and accidentally making a change there (Power Query and the pipeline canvas both autosave as you interact, not just on an explicit save click), and that change would just quietly vanish on the next deploy with no warning — which is confusing even if not harmful. Worth locking that down properly rather than relying on discipline: give ordinary users **Viewer** (or Contributor without edit rights, if Fabric's role model supports that granularity) on the Prod workspace, and reserve edit/Contributor rights there for the service principal fabric-cicd deploys as. Viewing Prod for troubleshooting (run history, lineage, output data) stays completely fine — it's persisting changes there you want to rule out.

---

**You**

So does that mean that in ETL_Dev, you wouldn't use Variable Libraries at all?

---

**Claude**

Yes, that's actually a fair implication, and it's worth naming explicitly: if you go all-in on fabric-cicd handling deployment to Prod, you could drop Variable Library entirely and rely purely on fabric-cicd's deploy-time substitution instead.

Here's why the two mechanisms are actually in tension, not just complementary. Variable Library works by shipping the *same* unresolved expression (`@pipeline().libraryVariables.X`) to every workspace, and having each workspace resolve it live, at run time, based on its own active value-set — that's why it doesn't care whether the workspace got the content via native sync or fabric-cicd. fabric-cicd's `find_replace`/`$items.X.Y.id` mechanism works completely differently: it bakes a *real, resolved* value into the content **at deploy time**, which only happens for workspaces fabric-cicd actually deploys to. Dev, by design, isn't one of those — it stays natively git-connected so you can keep editing there. So if you dropped Variable Library, Dev's committed pipeline would just contain Dev's actual real values directly (no indirection, no expression syntax) — and fabric-cicd's job when promoting to Prod would be to find those Dev-specific literals and swap them for Prod's, the classic `find_replace` pattern, rather than anything resolving live.

That's a completely coherent alternative design, and arguably simpler to read at a glance — no need to go check "what's the active value-set" to know what a field actually points to. The tradeoff against keeping Variable Library: you lose the ability to tweak a config value in Prod directly through the Fabric UI without a redeploy, and you couple the whole scheme to the fabric-cicd decision (if you ever stopped using it, Variable Library's approach would've kept working unchanged, this one wouldn't).

Given where we already are, I wouldn't treat it as strictly either/or — I'd split it by field, since Variable Library already works cleanly for four of the six things we parameterized and fabric-cicd is only actually *needed* for the two it can't handle:

- **Keep Variable Library** for `workspaceId`/`sqlEndpointId`/`SemanticModelWorkspaceId`/`SemanticModelId` — already built, already working, resolves the same regardless of how a workspace got its content.
- **Use fabric-cicd's name-based resolution** for `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` specifically — the one place Variable Library genuinely can't help, since item-reference variables aren't supported in pipelines yet.

Running both for the *same* field would be confusing (two indirection layers doing one job), but using each where it's actually the only tool that works isn't overlap — it's just each mechanism covering the gap the other one has.

---

**You**

Is there anything stated by Microsoft publicly which suggests that they're working on a solution to this Variable issue for pipelines and dataflows?

---

**Claude**

That search turned up something significant and very recent — let me verify the details directly from Microsoft's source before I report back, since it may change the recommendation I gave earlier.

---

**Claude**

Yes — and it looks like it may have just shipped, which is worth verifying directly in your tenant before you commit to a design around it.

The June 2026 Fabric Feature Summary specifically calls out **Connection reference and Item reference variable types for pipelines** — variables that hold a pointer to another Fabric item (workspace ID + item ID) or an external connection, resolved dynamically per environment. And I found direct corroboration in Microsoft's own pipeline-integration doc, updated **2026-06-22** (about three weeks ago): it now includes this line, which wasn't there when I researched this earlier in our conversation:

> "If you're using a Connection reference or Item reference type, add a '.' to get the variable properties after you add the variable to the expression builder. This step lets you use values like connection ID, item ID, or item workspace."

That's exactly the mechanism that would close the gap we ran into — an Item Reference variable pointing at a specific Dataflow (with a different value per Dev/Prod value-set), consumed in a `RefreshDataflow` activity as something like `@pipeline().libraryVariables.MyDataflowRef.itemId` / `.workspaceId`. If that holds up, `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` could be parameterized the same clean, runtime-resolved way as everything else we already built — no `fabric-cicd` find_replace or name-based resolution needed for that piece at all.

One flag before you rely on it: the **dedicated** item-reference-variable-type doc page (last updated April 2026, not June) still lists only Shortcut-for-Lakehouse, User Data Functions, and Notebook as supported — it hasn't caught up to the pipelines doc yet. That's a pretty normal docs-lag pattern right after a feature ships (different pages get updated on different schedules), not necessarily a sign it doesn't work — but I couldn't fetch the actual June Feature Summary post itself to confirm GA-vs-preview status (it 403'd), so I can't be fully certain of the rollout state in your tenant specifically.

Given that, I'd treat this as: worth testing directly — create an Item Reference variable pointing at one of your five Dataflows, try binding it to a `RefreshDataflow` activity's `dataflowId` in the pipeline canvas, and see if it actually works end to end. If it does, that changes the earlier "you need fabric-cicd for dataflowId/pipelineId" conclusion — Variable Library alone might now cover everything, and `fabric-cicd` (or the companion script) would only be needed for the narrower job of *creating* new Dataflow/Pipeline items in a new workspace and pointing the Item Reference variables at them once, not for ongoing parameterization.

---

**You**

What is a pipeline canvas?

---

**Claude**

The **pipeline canvas** is the visual, drag-and-drop editor in the Fabric web UI for building a Data Pipeline — it's what you see when you open one of your two Pipeline items (e.g., "RPC ERP Dataflows - Hourly Incremental") in the Fabric workspace, rather than editing its `pipeline-content.json` directly like we've been doing in this repo.

On that canvas, each activity (RefreshDataflow, Office365Email, Fail, etc.) shows up as a box, connected by arrows representing the `dependsOn` conditions (Succeeded/Failed/Completed) — visually, it looks like a flowchart. Clicking a box opens a settings pane where you configure that activity's properties (the same fields we've been hand-editing in the JSON, like `dataflowId` or `workspaceId`), including the "Add dynamic content" expression builder I mentioned, where you'd bind a field to a Variable Library reference instead of typing a literal value.

It's the same kind of interface as Azure Data Factory's pipeline designer, if you've used that before — Fabric's Data Pipeline is effectively ADF's pipeline engine running inside Fabric.

---

**You**

Ok.  I created an Item reference variable in the default value set column, and it copied it over to the Alternative Value Sets columns with the same item id.  How is it supposed to behave, or how does it change when you deploy?

---

**Claude**

That's expected default behavior, not a bug: when you create a new variable, only the **default** column gets an explicit value. Every Alternative Value Set that doesn't have its own override just inherits/falls back to that default — the UI shows it as if it "copied," but it's really just "no override set yet for Dev/Prod, so display the default." This is the same behavior we already saw with the plain String variables — `LakehouseWorkspaceId`/`LakehouseId` have no Dev/Prod overrides at all in this repo, for exactly this reason (single shared value, nothing to override).

**On deploy, nothing changes automatically — that's the important part.** Per Microsoft's own docs on Item Reference variables: *"The reference is static, pointing to a specific item and not automatically adjusting across environments... Deploying to a new stage, these references still point to the original workspace unless manually updated."* So if you promote this to Prod without touching the Prod column, Prod's pipeline would still refresh whatever Dataflow the default value points at — likely the Dev copy — not its own. Fabric has no mechanism to infer "this is Prod now, go find the Prod-equivalent item."

So the actual workflow is: go into the **Dev** column for this variable and use the item picker to explicitly select Dev's copy of that Dataflow, then go into the **Prod** column and pick Prod's copy. That's a manual, one-time-per-item step, same as before — the improvement Item Reference gives you over a plain String variable isn't "no more manual configuration," it's that you're picking the item from a browsable list (filtered by workspace/type) instead of hunting down and pasting a raw GUID. Worth setting both overrides now, before you rely on this for anything, so you don't end up with Prod silently pointing at Dev's item.

---

**You**

So after deploying to Prod (with the dataflow in existence now) you have to go into its variable library and overwrite the value in the Prod Value Set?  There's no roadmap solution to resolving that annoyance?

---

**Claude**

Confirmed, that's the correct workflow — and no, there's no roadmap signal suggesting Microsoft is planning to make this automatic. The documentation is explicit and consistent that this is deliberate, static-by-design behavior, not a gap they've flagged for future work: *"the reference is static, pointing to a specific item and not automatically adjusting across environments... Deploying to a new stage, these references still point to the original workspace unless manually updated. Use Multiple Value-Sets for each stage and activate the correct set manually **or via API scripts**."*

That last phrase is the telling part — Microsoft's own stated answer to "this doesn't scale if I do it by hand" isn't a planned auto-rebind feature, it's "go automate it yourself against our API." Which is exactly the companion-script idea we'd already landed on, just with a more concrete resource than I had earlier: there's a documented **Variable Library REST API** (`learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/cicd/variable-library/automate-variable-library`) built specifically for scripting variable and value-set updates. So instead of your script only *reading* item IDs and hand-writing them into the git-tracked JSON files, it could call that API directly to write the Prod value-set's override once the item exists — same job, just through Microsoft's supported automation surface rather than editing the JSON and relying on a subsequent native git sync to push it.

One nuance worth being precise about, since it changes how bad this "annoyance" actually is at scale: this is a **one-time step per item, per environment** — not something you redo on every deployment. Once you've pointed Prod's value-set at Prod's copy of that Dataflow (via the UI or the API) and that value is committed/saved, it stays correct through every subsequent deploy of that same item. It only comes up again when a genuinely *new* item shows up that's never been pointed at before — so it scales with how often you add new Dataflows/Pipelines, not with how often you deploy.

---

**You**

Is there a way to have some kind of job automated run after the user goes into the workspace and deploys the updates manually?

I'm thinking that if we kick off a job to update variables at the same time that the user is going in to deploy the other items manually we could end up in some kind of race with unexpected outcomes.

---

**Claude**

Short answer: no, there's no native Fabric event that fires automatically right after a human manually clicks "Update" in the Source Control pane — I checked specifically, and while Fabric does have a "Remote Lifecycle Notifications" webhook feature, it's part of the Extensibility Toolkit for developers building *custom* Fabric item types (registering your own workload), not something you can subscrible to for built-in items like Dataflow or Data Pipeline. So "listen for a sync to finish" isn't available as a trigger.

But your race-condition instinct is pointing at the right problem, and there's a better fix than trying to detect the human's action after the fact: **don't have the automation react to a manual sync — have the automation *perform* the sync itself.** Fabric has a public "Update from Git" REST API that does exactly what the Source Control pane's "Update" button does. If your script calls that API, waits for it to complete, and *then* runs the ID-discovery + Variable Library update step — all in one sequential script — there's no race at all, because nothing is happening concurrently. It's not "job A might run while job B is mid-flight," it's "step 1, then step 2, in order, in the same process." That also means no human has to remember to go trigger anything separately.

The tradeoff: this only works if you're comfortable letting the automation actually perform the deploy, rather than a human reviewing and clicking Update themselves in the Fabric UI. If you specifically want a human to eyeball the sync before it happens (a deliberate gate, not just habit), then you do need a human-initiated step — but the safe version of that is an **explicit trigger the human fires themselves right after** (a "run pipeline" button in ADO, or a script they run), not a timer or polling loop guessing when they're done. Sequential-by-human-action has the same race-free property as sequential-by-script; a timer running independently of their action is the only version that actually has the race problem you're worried about.

If you did still want an unattended polling job for some other reason (say, running every 15 minutes regardless), it's salvageable, but only if it's designed defensively: look up the item by name, and if it's not found yet, just skip and try again next cycle rather than erroring. That turns "the sync hasn't happened yet" into "the Variable Library update lags by up to one poll interval," not a corrupted or unexpected outcome — but it's strictly worse than the sequential options above, since it trades a clean design for tolerance of a problem you didn't need to have.

---

**You**

is the "Update from Git" rest api compatible with fabric-cicd?

---

**Claude**

They're not "compatible" in the sense of working together on the same workspace — and for the same reason we ran into earlier: `Update from Git` only makes sense for a workspace that has native Git Integration connected, and fabric-cicd's whole design assumes the target workspace is *not* git-connected (since its parameterized output would immediately show as a conflict against the raw git content). So you wouldn't call both against the same workspace.

But they're complementary at the level of your overall design, applied to *different* workspaces — which actually maps cleanly onto the split we already landed on:

- **Dev** stays natively git-connected (the editing surface). `Update from Git` is exactly the API you'd use to automate Dev's sync step instead of a human clicking the button — same mechanism, just scripted.
- **Prod** is the fabric-cicd deployment target (never git-connected). fabric-cicd deploys it directly via Fabric's Item Definition APIs, which is a completely separate API surface from the Git Integration endpoints — `Update from Git`, `Commit to Git`, `Get Git Status`, etc.

Under the hood, fabric-cicd doesn't call `Update from Git` at all — it talks to the generic Create Item / Update Item Definition / Publish endpoints directly. So there's no technical dependency between them either way; they're just two independent tools, and which one you use is determined per-workspace by whether that workspace is git-connected (Dev) or CI-managed (Prod), never both on the same one.

---

**You**

If we were to go the fabric-cicd route for everything ETL, is there any reason to keep variable libraries at all?

---

**Claude**

Yes, there's a genuine reason, not just inertia: the two mechanisms resolve values at fundamentally different times, and that difference matters operationally.

Variable Library resolves **at runtime** — every time the pipeline actually executes, it asks "what's my workspace's active value-set say right now?" fabric-cicd's substitution resolves **at deploy time** — it bakes one specific value into the content the moment you run a deployment. If you went full fabric-cicd and dropped Variable Library, every config change — even a trivial one, like pointing at a different Semantic Model or fixing a wrong SQL endpoint ID — would require a full redeploy to take effect. With Variable Library, you can go change that value directly in Prod's Fabric UI (or via its API) and the next pipeline run picks it up immediately, no deploy required. That's a real operational difference: it decouples "config that needs to change" from "code/structure that needs to change," so a fast, low-ceremony config fix doesn't have to go through your whole deployment path.

There's also a visibility angle: with Variable Library, you can open the Fabric UI and see exactly what value each variable currently resolves to in that workspace, right now. With pure deploy-time substitution, "what's actually live in Prod" only exists baked into the deployed JSON — to know it, you'd have to go inspect the field directly in the pipeline canvas, or reconstruct it from `parameter.yml` plus knowing what was last deployed. Less discoverable.

One honest caveat, though: this advantage is fragile if fabric-cicd deploys frequently. Since fabric-cicd doesn't maintain state — it just reconciles the workspace to match git on every run — a quick manual tweak to a Variable Library value in Prod would get silently reverted the next time *anything else* triggers a redeploy, unless you also update the git-tracked value-set file to match. So the "live tweak" benefit only really holds if you're disciplined about treating git as the actual source of truth even for those quick changes, or if deploys are infrequent enough that it doesn't matter in practice.

Worth connecting back to the Item Reference/Connection Reference finding from earlier: if that holds up when you test it, it actually strengthens the case beyond "worth keeping" — it means Variable Library could become the **primary** mechanism for everything, including `dataflowId`/`pipelineId`, with fabric-cicd's role shrinking down to just creating the physical items in a new workspace the first time. That's a cleaner split than running both mechanisms side by side for different fields.

---

**You**

How does fabric-cicd handle dependencies?  For example, if you have (1) a pipeline calling (2) a pipeline which calls (3) a dataflow you can't finalize 2 until you build 3, and can't finalize 1 until you've built 2 and 3.  How can you tell what order to build them in?

---

**Claude**

Good catch — and the honest answer here is more nuanced than "fabric-cicd figures it out." There are actually two different things going on, and only one of them is automatic.

**Built-in relationships between certain item types ARE ordered automatically** — things like "Environment before Notebook" or "SemanticModel before Report" are hardcoded, type-level rules fabric-cicd knows about.

**But the generic `$items.<Type>.<Name>.<Attribute>` reference mechanism — which is exactly what you'd use for a Pipeline referencing another Pipeline or a Dataflow — is *not* automatically ordered.** fabric-cicd's own docs are explicit about this: *"The source Dataflow must be deployed BEFORE the dependent Dataflow (especially during first time deployment)."* That's fabric-cicd telling you, the author, that you're responsible for sequencing — it resolves the reference at deployment time by looking up whatever's already been deployed, but it doesn't build a dependency graph from your `parameter.yml` and topologically sort the deploy order for you.

So for your exact case — Pipeline 1 → Pipeline 2 → Dataflow 3 — you'd need to sequence it yourself: **deploy Dataflow 3 first, then Pipeline 2 (now it can resolve Dataflow 3's ID), then Pipeline 1 (now it can resolve Pipeline 2's ID).** In practice that means structuring your own deploy script as an ordered sequence of fabric-cicd calls (or scoped passes via `item_type_in_scope`) rather than one big "deploy everything" call and hoping it works out — deploy Dataflows first as a pass, then the pipelines that only reference Dataflows, then the pipelines that reference other pipelines.

The good news: this is a small, static structure in your case — two pipelines and five dataflows, with a fixed, known shape (you already know Pipeline 2 refreshes the dataflows and Pipeline 1 invokes Pipeline 2). That's not something you need automatic dependency resolution to manage — it's reasonable to just hardcode that ordering directly in your deploy script ("dataflows, then the sub-pipeline, then the master pipeline") once, since that structure isn't likely to grow into something that needs a real dependency-graph solver. Where this would start to matter more is if you had many pipelines with a deep or frequently-changing call graph — at that point you'd want your own script to compute the order (a basic topological sort over "what does each pipeline call") rather than hand-maintaining it.

---

**You**

What if you use the Variable Library for references between items, deploy all items first, then update the variable library?

---

**Claude**

That's the right move, and it genuinely eliminates the ordering problem rather than just working around it — worth being precise about why.

The dependency-ordering issue with `$items.X.Y.id` exists specifically because that mechanism bakes a resolved value into the deployed JSON **at the moment of deployment** — so the referenced item has to already exist for that substitution to have something to resolve to. Variable Library references don't have that problem, because nothing gets baked in at deploy time at all: the deployed pipeline just contains the literal, unresolved expression string (`@pipeline().libraryVariables.SomeDataflowRef`). Deploying Pipeline 1, which references that expression, doesn't require Dataflow 3 to exist yet — there's nothing to look up, it's just text being pushed. So you can deploy Dataflow 3, Pipeline 2, and Pipeline 1 in **any order, or even in parallel**, because the deploy step and the resolution step are no longer the same operation. That's a strictly better shape than fabric-cicd's substitution mechanism for this exact problem — you've turned a topological-sort requirement into a non-issue.

Two things worth nailing down before you rely on this:

1. **Verify the Variable Library item itself needs to exist, but not fully-populated values.** The pipeline's `libraryVariables` block names which Variable Library and which variable it expects (`"libraryName": "Variables"`, `"variableName": "SomeDataflowRef"`) — so the Variable Library *item* has to already exist in the workspace for the pipeline to bind to it at all. That's trivial to satisfy (deploy the Variable Library item first, or alongside everything else). What I'm *not* certain of without testing: whether Fabric validates at save/publish time that the named variable actually exists with a value, or whether it defers all checking to when the pipeline actually runs. Worth a quick test — deploy a pipeline referencing a variable name that doesn't exist yet in the library, and confirm it saves without erroring.

2. **After deployment, write the discovered IDs back into the git-tracked value-set files, not just live into the workspace.** This is the same gotcha from a couple messages ago: if your "Phase 2" step (discover IDs, populate the Variable Library) only patches the live workspace via API/UI, the next unrelated fabric-cicd redeploy will reset the Variable Library back to whatever's in git and silently wipe those IDs out. So the actual flow should be: deploy everything → discover each new item's ID → write those into `Dev.json`/`Prod.json` in the repo → commit → optionally re-run fabric-cicd for just the VariableLibrary item type (or let the next deploy pick it up naturally).

With those two caveats handled, this really does simplify your deploy script down to two clean, order-independent phases — deploy everything, then resolve everything — instead of needing to reason about a dependency graph at all.

---

**You**

Ok.  Approximately how many lines of yml and python code would be required?  And would you use item reference variables or just strings?

---

**Claude**

**Rough sizing** — I'd estimate:

- **YAML: ~80–120 lines.** Similar shape and size to `pipeline_build_dbt.yml` (which is about 80 lines) — trigger block scoped to `ETL/` paths on `dev`/`master`, a per-branch target-workspace variable (same `${{ if contains(...) }}` pattern already used for `jobID`/`databaseId`), a Python setup step, `pip install fabric-cicd`, and a step invoking the deploy script with the right environment argument. Thin by design — the YAML's job is just "set up Python and call the script," not contain logic.

- **Python: ~150–250 lines.** This is where the real work is, and it breaks down roughly as: auth/config setup (~20–30 lines), the actual fabric-cicd publish call for Dataflow/DataPipeline/VariableLibrary (~15–25 lines — fabric-cicd's own API is compact here, often just constructing a `FabricWorkspace` object and calling publish), then the part that's genuinely custom code because fabric-cicd doesn't do it for you: discovering the newly-deployed items' IDs via the list-items API and matching by name (~30–50 lines), and writing those IDs into the Variable Library's value-set JSON — both the local file (for git commit) and optionally live via the Variable Library API (~40–60 lines combined). Add error handling/logging overhead and you land around 150 on the lean end, 250 if you want it reasonably robust (retries, clear CLI args, handling "item not found yet" gracefully).

So, call it **~300–350 lines total**, with the bulk being the "discover IDs, patch Variable Library" logic — which makes sense, since that's the one piece nothing off-the-shelf does for you; fabric-cicd's own `$items` mechanism solves an equivalent problem internally, but only for its own deploy-time substitution, not for populating a separate Variable Library.

**String variables, not Item Reference** — for a reason specific to your setup, not a general rule. Item Reference's main advantage is bundling an item's ID *together with its workspace ID* in one variable (useful when the referenced item might live in a different workspace) and giving a human a nicer picker in the UI. Neither applies here: all your Dataflows and Pipelines live in the same workspace as each other (that's what `ETLWorkspaceId` already covers separately), so you only ever need the bare item ID, not a workspace+item pair. And since your script is populating these values programmatically via API rather than a human clicking through a picker, Item Reference's UX benefit doesn't come into play either. Meanwhile String variables are the more mature, longer-proven mechanism in this conversation (it's what already works for `LakehouseWorkspaceId`, etc.), versus Item Reference's pipeline support being something we're not fully certain has stabilized yet. Simpler, already-proven, and you're not giving up anything you'd actually use — I'd go with String.

---

**You**

In this design, would Dev have a variable library?

---

**Claude**

Yes — and it's worth being precise about why, since it's not just "for symmetry."

The Variable Library resolution mechanism is inherently per-workspace: `@pipeline().libraryVariables.X` only resolves to something if there's a Variable Library item present in *that* workspace for Fabric to look up against, with some value-set marked active there. Since Dev's Dataflows and Pipelines contain those same unresolved expressions (they're the same git-committed content as everywhere else), Dev needs its own instance of the "Variables" Variable Library item for its own pipeline runs to resolve anything at all.

Practically, this happens automatically as part of what's already true today: Dev is natively git-connected, and `ETL/Variables.VariableLibrary/` is just another folder in that same git tree — so Dev's native sync already deploys a full copy of it, containing **all** the value-sets (`Dev.json` and `Prod.json` both), the same as Prod's copy would. The thing that differs between Dev's copy and Prod's copy isn't the content — it's the **active value-set**, a per-workspace runtime setting (not itself a git-tracked file, as we found earlier) that you set once per workspace: Dev's copy has "Dev" marked active, Prod's copy (deployed by fabric-cicd) has "Prod" marked active. Same file, different switch.

One thing this surfaces that's worth calling out: we haven't actually populated `Dev.json`'s `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` overrides yet either — only Prod's discovery is pending in what we've been discussing. Since Dev is where you're actively building things, those IDs may already be knowable by hand right now (you can just go look at each Dataflow's URL/settings in Dev today), so it's lower-stakes than Prod's case — but strictly speaking, the same discovery script would need to run once against Dev's workspace too, not just Prod's, to fully populate `Dev.json`. The difference is really about *how* you'd do it for each: for Dev, since it's the workspace you already touch directly, a one-time manual entry through the Fabric UI is perfectly reasonable; for Prod, since nobody should be hand-editing anything there, running the same script against Prod is the right call.

---

**You**

Can a user go into Dev, create a dataflow and a pipeline which aren't using variables, and have the commit replace them with variables?

---

**Claude**

No — committing is just a serialization step, not a semantic rewrite. Fabric's "Commit" operation exports whatever the item currently looks like in the workspace into the git file format (`mashup.pq`, `pipeline-content.json`) as-is. It has no mechanism that inspects a literal value, notices "hey, this GUID matches what's in the Variable Library," and swaps it out for an expression. If someone builds a new Dataflow or Pipeline the normal way — dragging an activity onto the canvas, picking an item from a dropdown, typing a value directly — whatever gets set is what gets committed, literally.

Binding a field to a Variable Library reference is something the author has to do deliberately, in the Fabric UI, at authoring time — clicking "Add dynamic content" and explicitly selecting the Library Variable, or writing the `Variable.ValueOrDefault(...)` call in Power Query. Nothing retroactively does that for you.

That's a real risk worth naming plainly: someone builds a new Dataflow in Dev the quick, obvious way, forgets to wire up the Variable Library bindings, commits it, and it works perfectly fine in Dev — because Dev's literal values happen to be correct for Dev. The mistake is completely invisible until it gets promoted to Prod, where it's now silently pointing at Dev's workspace/items instead of Prod's. That's a nasty class of bug precisely because nothing fails loudly; it just quietly does the wrong thing.

Given that, I'd build a guardrail rather than rely on people remembering every time: a validation step in the ADO pipeline (or even a pre-commit check) that scans changed `pipeline-content.json`/`mashup.pq` files for the specific fields that should always be parameterized — `workspaceId`, `sqlEndpointId`, `groupId`, `datasetId`, and whichever `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` fields you've decided should use Variable Library — and fails the build if it finds a literal GUID sitting in one of those fields instead of the expected `@pipeline().libraryVariables.X` expression. That's the same `git grep`-style pattern-matching approach we used earlier this session to hunt down exposed secrets, just aimed at "un-parameterized environment-specific value" instead of "leaked credential." Want me to sketch what that check would actually look like?

---

**You**

I don't mean that the commit would make the update in Dev.  I mean that the commit would trigger the ADO pipeline which would update and commit the code.  The user would then see that there are git updates available in the source control window.  If they had made more changes in the meantime that would be a problem, but we could probably teach the couple of us who do that to wait.

---

**Claude**

Ah, got it — that's a genuinely workable idea, and it's a natural extension of `standardize_dataflows.py`, which already does exactly this kind of rewrite (it's not a new pattern for this repo). Let me walk through what it'd actually take and where the sharp edges are.

**The mechanism**: push to `dev` with an `ETL/` path change triggers the pipeline (same trigger pattern already used elsewhere) → a script scans the just-committed files for known un-parameterized fields (literal GUIDs sitting in `workspaceId`, `sqlEndpointId`, `dataflowId`, etc. where an `@pipeline().libraryVariables.X` expression belongs) → rewrites them → commits and pushes that fix back to `dev` itself, using the pipeline's own git identity. Dev's Source Control pane then shows "updates available," and the human pulls it in when ready. Structurally sound — the git-push-back part just needs `$(System.AccessToken)` wired up properly this time (the lesson from the ADO token we cleaned up earlier applies directly here — this is exactly the legitimate use case for that token, not a hardcoded credential).

**Two things worth handling deliberately, not just training around:**

1. **Infinite-loop guard.** The bot's own auto-fix commit is itself a push to `dev` with `ETL/` changes — which re-triggers the same pipeline. That run needs to find nothing left to fix and exit cleanly without committing, or you get a runaway loop of empty commits. Easy to prevent (just check "did I actually change anything" before committing), but has to be deliberate, not assumed.

2. **The race you already flagged is real, but the failure mode is mild, not destructive.** If someone keeps editing in Dev's workspace before pulling the bot's fix, and they touch the *same* item the bot just rewrote, Fabric's Git Integration will show that item as **Conflict** when they eventually try to sync — the same conflict state we discussed for the fabric-cicd/native-git-integration collision earlier in this conversation, just triggered a different way. It's recoverable (resolve via "accept incoming" or "keep mine" in the Source Control pane), not data loss — so "teach the couple of us to wait" is a reasonable mitigation for a two-person team, as long as everyone understands the actual failure mode is "you'll have to manually resolve a conflict on that one item," not "something breaks silently." I'd pair the training with one small technical nicety: have the pipeline post a quick notification (Teams message or email) when it pushes an auto-fix commit, so people don't have to remember to go check the Source Control pane — removes the "waiting" from being purely a memory exercise.

Everything else about this — permissions for the pipeline to push directly to `dev`, and the fact that the detection is only as good as the field patterns you teach it to recognize — is standard "bot that commits back to your branch" territory, same caveats you'd have for any auto-fix CI tool.

It's still not showing up.  I think that means that creating a pipeline is not exclusively a code-driven task, but that you have to do it in the UI.  Can you confirm this, and validate whether that means anything for us?

---

**Claude**

Confirmed — that's exactly right, and it's worth being precise about why.

A YAML file sitting in your repo, on any branch, is just a text file as far as Azure DevOps is concerned until something explicitly tells ADO "treat this file as a Pipeline." That's a separate, one-time registration step that creates an actual **Pipeline object** in ADO's system — with its own ID, name, run history, and settings — pointing at a specific repo + file path. Nothing about committing or pushing the YAML file causes that object to spring into existence on its own; ADO doesn't scan your repo looking for pipeline-shaped YAML files and auto-register them. Someone has to explicitly create it.

That registration step is usually done through the "New Pipeline" wizard in the UI, which is what you've been going through — but it's not *strictly* UI-only in an absolute sense. It can also be done via the Azure DevOps CLI (`az pipelines create`) or the REST API directly, both of which just wrap the same underlying registration action. So "code-driven" isn't quite the right frame either — it's "one deliberate registration action, done once, through some interface" — UI is simply the standard way to do it for a first-time setup, and there's nothing wrong with using it.

What this means for you practically:

- **This is a one-time cost, not an ongoing one.** Once the pipeline is registered — regardless of how — ADO will evaluate the actual YAML file's trigger logic fresh on every subsequent push, automatically, forever. You won't need to re-register it or repeat this step.
- **It fully explains why nothing showed up despite the file existing on both branches now.** The missing piece was never about file placement or content — it's that the actual "create the Pipeline object" action hasn't successfully completed yet.
- **This is squarely something only you can do from here** — I don't have access to the Azure DevOps UI, API, or CLI credentials, so I can't perform the registration myself.

Given that, I'd go back through "New Pipeline" now that the file is confirmed present on `master`, and pay attention to exactly where it stops or what it shows — does it successfully find and display the YAML file content before you click through to save it, or does it fail earlier than that? If the UI wizard keeps silently not producing a pipeline, the CLI (`az pipelines create --name <name> --repository <repo> --branch master --yml-path pipeline_etl_enforce_variables.yml`) might surface a clearer error message than the web UI does. Let me know what you actually see when you try it again.

**Tests to run on `ETL_DEV`:**

1. **Positive detection test — the one that actually matters most.** Deliberately create a new Dataflow or Pipeline in the Fabric UI (or hand-edit an existing one) leaving a hardcoded value in one of the fields the script watches — e.g., set a `workspaceId` to a literal GUID instead of picking up the Variable Library reference. You should end up seeing "updates available" in the Fabric Dev workspace's Source Control pane afterward.

2. **No-loop confirmation.** After that auto-fix commit lands, the pipeline will trigger again (its own commit is a push to `ETL_DEV` matching the path filter). Confirm that second run finds nothing left to fix and does *not* create another commit — proving it settles rather than looping.

3. **Round-trip functional check.** Pull the auto-fix commit into the Dev workspace and actually *run* the affected Dataflow/Pipeline. This confirms the rewritten `@pipeline().libraryVariables.X` expression genuinely resolves to the right value at runtime, not just that the JSON is well-formed — we've only verified the latter so far.

4. **Brand-new item test.** Build an actual new Dataflow from scratch (or copied from `_Template`) rather than editing an existing one, to confirm `standardize_dataflows.py` and `enforce_parameterization.py` correctly handle the combination — the first establishes the `DefaultDestination` pattern, the second wraps the shared variables. This is the realistic "what a BI developer actually does" scenario.

5. **Optional — the conflict scenario.** Deliberately edit the same item in the Dev workspace before pulling in an auto-fix commit, to confirm you get the expected recoverable "Conflict" status in Source Control rather than something worse. Lower priority since we already reasoned through this, but worth seeing once for real.

**What to expect in Prod:**

Here's the important recalibration: **this specific pipeline is intentionally Dev-only, and that doesn't change once `ETL_DEV` content eventually reaches Prod.** The trigger is scoped to `ETL_DEV` specifically — even now that the YAML file also exists on `master` (purely so ADO's wizard could discover it), pushes to `master` will never fire it, and that stays true after any future merge, unless someone deliberately widens the trigger later. I'd advise against ever doing that: by design, Prod shouldn't have humans hand-creating Dataflows/Pipelines with hardcoded values in the first place — Prod is meant to only ever receive already-parameterized content via whatever promotion mechanism you land on, not be edited directly. So there's nothing to "deploy" for this particular bot — its job ends once Dev's committed content is clean.

What you should actually expect regarding Prod is the bigger gap we found earlier: **Prod's `ETL/` folder doesn't structurally match `ETL_DEV` at all right now** — no `Dataflows/` folder, no `Variables.VariableLibrary/`, and a flatter `Pipelines` layout. So there's nothing meaningful to test in Prod yet; "deploying this to Prod" isn't a real next step until the larger promotion mechanism (fabric-cicd or otherwise, from our earlier conversation) actually brings Prod's structure and content up to parity with Dev. Once that exists, what you'd expect to see is: Prod's own Variable Library with its "Prod" value-set active, and its Pipelines resolving those same `@pipeline().libraryVariables.X` expressions to Prod-specific values instead of Dev's — the exact same files, different resolved values, which is the whole point of the design.

None of that has been synced into the live Dev workspace's Variable Library item yet — it's all just sitting in git, committed but not pulled in. Clicking **Update** in that Source Control pane is exactly the right move; it'll bring the live item in line with everything we've built this session.

One small thing worth checking right after you sync: the "active value-set" (which of Dev/Prod is currently selected as live) is a workspace-level setting, not something stored in these git files — so confirm it's still set to "Dev" after the update completes, just in case removing the Test tier caused Fabric to reset that selection to something unexpected.
