**You**

So the things that the python script updates in Variables is different than these changes?

---

**Claude**

Yes, they're completely different things, and it's worth being precise about the distinction since it matters for how this all fits together.

**The pending Git changes to the "Variables" item** are edits to the Variable Library's *own* definition — its `variables.json`, `Dev.json`, `Prod.json`, `settings.json` files. That's where a variable actually gets created and given its per-environment values (adding `ETLWorkspaceId` itself, giving it a Dev value and a Prod value, removing the Test tier). Those were changes we made directly to the Library item this session — not something the Python script touched at all.

**`enforce_parameterization.py` never touches the Variable Library item.** It only edits Dataflow (`mashup.pq`) and Pipeline (`pipeline-content.json`) files — rewriting a hardcoded value it finds there into a *reference* to a variable, like `@pipeline().libraryVariables.Variables_ETLWorkspaceId`. It assumes that variable already exists in the Library; it never creates, edits, or manages the Library's own variables or value-sets. If you ran the script before `ETLWorkspaceId` existed in the Library at all, it would still happily rewrite the pipeline to point at `Variables_ETLWorkspaceId` — that reference just wouldn't resolve to anything meaningful until the Library actually had that variable defined, which is exactly the dependency you're about to satisfy by clicking Update.

So the two things are complementary, not overlapping: the Library sync you're about to do supplies the *variables themselves*; the script only ever writes *pointers* to variables it expects to already be there. Order matters a bit — the Library needs the variable to exist for a pipeline's reference to it to actually mean something at runtime, but nothing enforces that order at save time, which is why it's worth doing the Library sync now rather than leaving it pending.

**You**

I thought that the python script would add variables to the library and update them after the items themselves were deployed?

---

**Claude**

Good catch — that's a real mix-up, and worth clarifying precisely, because those are two different tools we discussed at different points in this conversation.

**What I actually built (`enforce_parameterization.py`)** only rewrites *existing* hardcoded values in Dataflow/Pipeline files into references to variables that are assumed to already exist in the Library — the "someone built a new Dataflow in the Fabric UI and forgot to wire up Library variables" safety net. It never creates a new variable in the Library, and it never talks to a live Fabric workspace at all — it's pure git/file manipulation.

**The tool you're remembering — discover a newly-deployed item's real ID and write it into the Library's value-set — is a different piece of work we only discussed conceptually, several messages back**, in the "deploy all items first, then update the variable library" exchange. That one needs to actually call Fabric's REST API (list items in a workspace, match by name, then either call the Variable Library API or edit `Dev.json`/`Prod.json` and commit) — fundamentally different from `enforce_parameterization.py`, since it requires live Fabric access and authentication, not just local file rewriting. We estimated its size (~150–250 lines) and settled on String variables over Item Reference for it, but never actually wrote it.

When you said "go ahead and build it," the immediately preceding conversation was specifically about the auto-fix-bot design (the race-condition question, "teach the couple of us to wait"), so I built that one — not the discovery/populate tool. That's on me for not flagging explicitly at the time that there were two distinct pieces of work floating around and I was only building one of them.

Want me to build the discovery/populate script now? Given you haven't set up either yet, this one can't be tested end-to-end the way I tested `enforce_parameterization.py` locally — it'll need real credentials before it can actually run.

**You**

I don't have a reason to prefer one over the other.  What are the reasons?

---

**Claude**

Here's the actual tradeoff, and I'd lean toward direct REST API calls for this specific script — but let me lay out both sides.

**Direct REST API calls (using something like `requests` + a service-principal auth library):**
- *Pro:* The job here is narrow — list items in a workspace, match by name, read/write the Variable Library's value-sets. That's a small, well-bounded task, and writing exactly the calls you need keeps the script simple and easy to reason about.
- *Pro:* You're not coupling this to a bigger decision you haven't made yet. Whether you ultimately adopt fabric-cicd as your actual deployment mechanism is still an open question from earlier in this conversation — building this discovery script directly against the REST API means it works the same regardless of what you decide there. It only needs to look at whatever workspace exists and read/write the Library; it doesn't care how the items got there.
- *Pro:* There's already a "hand-roll REST calls" pattern in this repo (the dead `Deploy_v2.yml` did this against the ADO API), so it's not an unfamiliar shape of code here.
- *Con:* You own the auth boilerplate yourself — acquiring a token for the service principal, handling refresh/expiry, retries. Small but real.
- *Con:* If Microsoft changes the API surface, you feel that directly rather than it being absorbed by a maintained library.

**Building on `fabric-cicd`'s utilities:**
- *Pro:* Auth is already handled — it knows how to authenticate as a service principal, so you'd skip writing that yourself.
- *Pro:* If you do end up adopting fabric-cicd for actual deployment later, using it here too means one library to learn and maintain instead of two different ways of talking to Fabric in your codebase.
- *Con:* fabric-cicd is built around "deploy items from a repo to a workspace" as its core job — I haven't seen a clean, documented, standalone "just list items and give me their IDs" function in its public surface. You'd likely have to either run it through its full deploy flow to get that side effect, or reach into internals that aren't part of its stable, documented contract — which is more likely to break on a version bump than its actual supported CLI/config interface.
- *Con:* Pulling in a whole deployment framework as a dependency for a task this narrow is disproportionate if you're not also using it for the bigger job.

Given you haven't committed to fabric-cicd as the deployment mechanism yet, and this script's actual job (read a workspace's items, update a Library) is genuinely independent of that decision, I'd build it against the REST API directly — it stays useful and unaffected no matter which way the bigger fabric-cicd question eventually goes. Want me to go ahead with that?

**You**

I have a service principal, though I'm not 100% sure it has all the access it needs.

**Claude**

That's worth sorting out before I write anything, since I can't test this script against live Fabric the way I tested `enforce_parameterization.py` locally — if the service principal is missing something, we won't find out until you actually try to run it. Here's the checklist, in the order I'd verify it:

1. **Tenant-level toggle.** A Fabric admin needs to have turned on "Service principals can use Fabric APIs" in the Fabric Admin Portal (Tenant settings → Developer settings). This is an all-or-nothing tenant-wide switch — without it, no service principal can call Fabric's REST API at all, regardless of anything else being configured correctly.

2. **API permissions on the app registration itself.** In Entra ID, the service principal's app registration needs Fabric/Power BI Service API permissions granted — and, importantly, **admin-consented**, not just added. This is a common gotcha: someone adds the permission in the API permissions blade but nobody clicks "Grant admin consent," and it silently doesn't work.

3. **Workspace membership, with the right role.** This is the one most likely to be missing or under-scoped. The service principal needs to be explicitly added as a member of the Dev workspace (the one we'd test against first) — and it needs at least **Contributor**, not just Viewer. Per Fabric's own Variable Library documentation: Viewer-level access is read-only, and editing variables or value-sets requires Contributor or above. Since this script needs to both list items (read) and write discovered IDs into the Variable Library (edit), Contributor is the actual requirement — Viewer alone won't be enough even though listing items would work with it.

I'd go check #3 first, since it's the most specific to this workspace and the easiest to have overlooked when the service principal was originally set up for something else. Once you've confirmed (or fixed) all three, let me know and I'll write the script — at that point it'll actually be testable rather than something we're hoping works.

That's not a bug in the discovery script — it's telling us something real: **at some point, these Dataflows (and the sub-pipeline) were deleted and recreated in the Dev workspace**, which gives them brand-new IDs, while the git-tracked `pipeline-content.json` still had the *old* IDs from before that happened. Since we deliberately left `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` untouched all session (they were out of scope until just now), that stale reference has been sitting there this whole time.

The practical implication: **the actual Fabric pipeline in Dev has likely been trying to refresh Dataflow items that no longer exist**, or that are orphaned/renamed — which would show up as a real failure the next time someone actually ran it, not something the enforcement-script test we ran earlier would have caught (that test only checked whether hardcoded values needed *parameterizing*, not whether the specific IDs were still valid).

Good news: this discovery script just fixed that. `Dev.json` now has the actual, currently-correct IDs, and once you sync this into the Fabric workspace, the pipeline should be pointing at the real, existing items again.

**Claude**

Good to know — that shifts the likely explanation away from "someone recreated these Dataflows on purpose." A more plausible cause, given no one touched them manually: something on the git-integration side reassigned the IDs without a corresponding commit capturing it — e.g., if the workspace's Git connection was ever disconnected and reconnected (which the Fabric docs explicitly warn "overwrites all items in the workspace" and can involve items getting rebound), Fabric could end up treating the same-named items as fresh ones with new GUIDs, while the git-committed file just never got a "Commit" pushed back to record that. That's a mechanism, not a person, and it would explain why *all five* items drifted at once rather than just one.

Running the actual pipeline is the right way to get real evidence here, and it'll validate two things at once: whether the discovered IDs are genuinely correct, and whether the whole Variable Library resolution mechanism actually works at runtime (not just that the JSON is well-formed, which is all we've confirmed so far).

**Claude**

That job ran correctly, but it's answering a different question than "did this deploy to the workspace" — it's worth being precise about the distinction, since this is exactly the mix-up we're at risk of here.

**`pipeline_etl_enforce_variables.yml` only ever touches git — it never talks to the Fabric workspace at all.** Its whole job is: check the repo for hardcoded values, and if it finds any, commit a fix back to `ETL_DEV`. That's it. Looking at the log: it found the 4 Dataflows, ran both scripts, and reported "Nothing to fix - all files already parameterized" — which makes sense, since we'd already made all those fixes by hand earlier in this session, before anything was committed. The "Commit and push fixes, if any" step succeeded, but with nothing to commit *this* time (there was nothing left broken) — the commit that actually landed and triggered this run was whoever pushed our batch of changes to `ETL_DEV` in the first place.

**Getting changes from git into the actual Fabric workspace is a separate, manual step by design** — that's exactly the "Update" action in the Source Control panel you're looking at, which is what those **3 pending Updates** are waiting for. Nothing in this project has ever been set up to automate that push-into-the-workspace step; that's deliberate, from the architecture decision earlier that Dev stays the human-reviewed, git-connected surface — a person clicks Update, not a pipeline.

So the sequence is: commit/push (already done) → ADO's enforcement pipeline ran and confirmed nothing needs auto-fixing (already done, that's what you're looking at) → **click Update in that Source Control panel** (still needed) → *then* the Dataflow/Pipeline pickers should resolve correctly instead of showing stale GUIDs → *then* you can test-run the actual Fabric pipeline.

---

**You**

Didn't we talk about deploying new items, grabbing their ids, and then storing that in the Variable Library in Git?  

How would we get their values without first deploying?

---

**Claude**

Yes, we did — and the resolution to "how do we get values without first deploying" is: **you can't, and the discovery script isn't meant to run before the item exists.** It's strictly a "the item is already there, tell me its ID" tool — for something that hasn't been created anywhere yet, there's no ID for it to discover. Deploy always comes first, for that specific item.

The reason this doesn't create a chicken-and-egg problem for the *pipeline* that references the not-yet-created item: deploying/syncing a pipeline never requires its library variable references to actually resolve to anything. `@pipeline().libraryVariables.Variables_Dataflow_NewThing` is just a string sitting in the JSON as far as saving/syncing is concerned — Fabric doesn't check "does this variable exist, does it have a value for this environment" until the pipeline actually *runs*. So the real sequence for a brand-new item is:

1. **Deploy the new item itself** (create the Dataflow/Pipeline in the target workspace, however that happens — no dependency on the Variable Library at all).
2. **Deploy/sync the pipeline that references it**, even though its Variable Library reference for this environment is empty or missing — that's fine, nothing validates it at this stage.
3. **Run the discovery script** now that the item actually exists, to find its ID and write it into that environment's value-set.
4. **Only now can you actually *run* the pipeline successfully** — that's the first point where Fabric needs a real value to plug into the expression.

What we just did with Dev was actually a slightly different (and, in hindsight, lucky) case: the items were already deployed — they'd existed in Dev this whole time, just under different IDs than git had on record. So there was no "deploy" step needed; the discovery script just had to catch git up to reality. For Prod, where these five items genuinely don't exist yet (recall Prod's `ETL/` folder is still missing the whole `Dataflows/` and `Variables.VariableLibrary/` structure), running the discovery script there right now would find nothing — that's exactly why it prints "Not found in the Prod workspace (not created there yet?)" instead of erroring, so you get a clear, expected signal rather than a confusing failure.

---

**You**

Does step 3 actually write the variables into the Workspace's Variable Library?  Or does that have to be deployed from the source control UI as a step 3.5?

---

**Claude**

No — step 3 only writes to the local, git-tracked JSON file (`ETL/Variables.VariableLibrary/valueSets/{Dev,Prod}.json`) on disk. It never calls Fabric's API to write into the live workspace's Variable Library directly. So yes, there's a real "step 3.5": commit that file change, push it, then go into the workspace's Source Control panel and click **Update** to actually sync it into the live Variable Library item.

That was a deliberate choice, not an oversight — it's the same reasoning we used throughout this whole design: keep git as the single source of truth, and never have two different mechanisms (a script writing live via API, and native Git Integration syncing from the branch) both able to change the same item, since that's exactly the kind of conflict we discussed way back with the `fabric-cicd`-vs-native-sync question.

You've actually already lived through this exact sequence a couple of messages ago: the discovery script updated `Dev.json` locally, but the Fabric workspace still showed those "3 pending Updates" until the file was committed, pushed, and synced — that *was* step 3.5, playing out concretely rather than just in the abstract.

One nuance worth flagging: this only holds as long as the target workspace stays natively git-connected, the way Dev is. If Prod eventually becomes a `fabric-cicd`-managed target instead (rather than git-connected), "step 3.5" would look different there — `fabric-cicd` would push the updated Variable Library content directly via API as part of its own deploy step, not through a Source Control sync, since a `fabric-cicd`-managed workspace isn't git-connected in the first place.

---

**You**

So that means we have to go into the Source Control menu two times:
1. After creating the initial item.
2. To accept git updates for variables (and substituted variables into items).

Is that right?

---

**Claude**

Yes, that's exactly right — two separate trips into Source Control, with a git-only step sandwiched in between them that isn't itself a Fabric UI action:

1. **Update #1 — creates the item.** Pull the already-authored Dataflow/Pipeline/Variable Library content from git into the target workspace for the first time. This is what actually creates the physical item in that workspace and gives it a brand-new GUID — Fabric can't assign that ID any earlier than this, since it doesn't exist until the item does.

2. **(git-only, no Fabric UI involved)** Run the discovery script against that workspace, which finds the new GUID and writes it into the local `Dev.json`/`Prod.json` file. Then commit and push that file change — a normal git commit, not something done through Fabric's Source Control panel.

3. **Update #2 — accepts the fix.** Back in that workspace's Source Control panel, click Update again to pull in the just-pushed commit, so the live Variable Library actually reflects the correct, discovered value — and so any pipeline referencing it (via the `@pipeline().libraryVariables.X` expression) resolves correctly the next time it runs.

Worth noting: each of those two "Update" clicks applies to *everything* pending at once, not per-item — so even though multiple items might be changing (like the "3 pending Updates" you saw earlier: two Pipelines and Variables), that's still one click each time, not one click per item. The two-trip shape is inherent to the design, not a rough edge — there's no way to skip straight to step 3 with the correct value already in place, since the ID genuinely doesn't exist until step 1 creates it.

**You**

I think we talked about Prod not being a git-connected workspace at all.  Is that accurate?

---

**Claude**

Good catch — that's a real inconsistency in what I just told you, not a subtlety you're missing. Yes, you're remembering correctly: we established earlier that Prod should specifically **not** be git-connected if you adopt `fabric-cicd` for it, precisely to avoid the conflict problem (its parameterized output would diverge from raw git content, and native Git Integration would perpetually flag that as unresolved changes).

My last answer described "Update #1... Update #2... back in that workspace's Source Control panel" — which only makes sense for a workspace that actually *has* a Source Control panel, i.e. one that's git-connected. That's accurate for Dev. It's not accurate for Prod under the architecture we settled on, and I shouldn't have described it that way without flagging the distinction.

For a genuinely `fabric-cicd`-managed Prod (no git connection at all), the same two-step shape still exists, but it's two `fabric-cicd` **deploy runs**, not two Fabric UI clicks:

1. **Deploy run #1** — `fabric-cicd` reads from git and creates/updates the item directly via the Fabric API. This is what actually creates the item in Prod and gives it a GUID — no Source Control panel involved, since there isn't one.
2. **Discovery + commit** — same as before: run the discovery script against Prod, find the new GUID, write it into `Prod.json`, commit and push.
3. **Deploy run #2** — `fabric-cicd` runs again, pushing the now-corrected Variable Library content into Prod. Again, purely an API-driven deploy, not a UI action.

One honest caveat: we've only actually built the Dev-side pieces so far — `enforce_parameterization.py` (git-only) and `discover_item_ids.py` (which happens to accept either workspace as a parameter, so it already works against Prod once items exist there). The actual `fabric-cicd` deploy wiring for Prod — the thing that would perform steps 1 and 3 above — hasn't been built yet. So this is still the correct conceptual shape, but not something we've implemented or tested end-to-end for Prod specifically.

**Claude**

Right now, it's entirely manual — there's no ADO pipeline that runs `discover_item_ids.py` or commits/pushes its output automatically. What we did for Dev a few messages back (running the script by hand, then committing the `Dev.json` change ourselves) is exactly how it would need to happen today for Prod too: run the script, review what it found, commit, push.

The only ADO pipeline that actually exists (`pipeline_etl_enforce_variables.yml`) doesn't touch this at all — it only runs `standardize_dataflows.py` and `enforce_parameterization.py`, and has nothing to do with discovering IDs or talking to Fabric's API.

It absolutely *could* be automated, though — the shape would be a single ADO pipeline that runs fabric-cicd's deploy, then `discover_item_ids.py --env prod`, then commits and pushes the resulting `Prod.json` change (reusing the same "check git status, commit if something changed" pattern already in `pipeline_etl_enforce_variables.yml`), then runs fabric-cicd's deploy a second time to push the corrected Variable Library content. That would turn the two-manual-step dance into one pipeline run with no human in the loop.

But that pipeline doesn't exist yet — it's a natural next piece of work, not something we've built. Want me to build it, or hold off until the Prod fabric-cicd deploy piece itself exists (since this pipeline's first and third steps depend on that being built first)?

**You**

You're suggesting that would be a new pipeline.  Why couldn't the existing pipeline handle this?

---

**Claude**

No hard technical reason — I was defaulting to "new pipeline" without spelling out why, so let me actually make the case rather than just assert it.

ADO pipelines can absolutely have multiple branch triggers and conditional stages in one YAML file — your own `Deploy_v2.yml`/`azure-pipelines.yml` already do exactly this, with branch-conditional variables (`${{ if contains(variables['Build.SourceBranchName'], 'master') }}`) picking different values depending on what triggered the run. So there's nothing stopping `pipeline_etl_enforce_variables.yml` from also having a Prod-triggered stage that does the fabric-cicd deploy + discovery + re-deploy sequence.

The reasons I'd still lean toward separate, though:

- **Different blast radius.** The current pipeline is low-stakes by design — it only ever touches git, auto-fixing hardcoded values in a dev branch. A Prod-deploy pipeline actually creates/updates real production Fabric items. Bundling a "safe, auto-committing dev hygiene bot" and "thing that deploys to production" into one file makes it easy to lose track of which parts of a shared pipeline carry which risk, especially months from now.
- **Different credentials.** The existing pipeline only needs `$(System.AccessToken)` — it never touches Fabric's API at all. A Prod-deploy pipeline needs the service principal's client ID/secret. Keeping them separate means the low-stakes pipeline's permission footprint stays minimal; it never even has access to credentials it doesn't need.
- **Matches how this repo already organizes things.** Your four existing pipelines are already split by concern — dbt, semantic models, reports, this ETL enforcement — rather than one combined pipeline. A Prod ETL deploy is a genuinely different concern (deployment) from what this one does (git hygiene), so a separate file is consistent with the pattern already here, not a deviation from it.

None of that is a hard blocker, though — if you'd rather have one unified "ETL CI/CD" pipeline with a Dev stage and a Prod stage, that's a legitimate design too, and ADO's environment/approval-gate features could still let you require manual sign-off on just the Prod stage even within a single file. Which do you want?

One thing worth deciding before I build it: should discovery run on *every* push (cheap, since it's just a few API calls, and catches drift early), or only when something suggests it's needed (e.g., a new Dataflow/Pipeline folder appears)? **Claude**

Good clarification, and there's a technical wrinkle worth flagging: Azure DevOps triggers can only match on **branch name**, not "which Fabric workspace initiated this push" — ADO has no visibility into that at all, it just sees a git push to a named branch. So there's no way to write a trigger that literally means "whatever branch the Dev workspace happens to be connected to right now."

The practical way to get what you actually want — this keeps working without anyone having to remember to edit the YAML when `ETL_DEV` gets retired — is to include both branch names in the trigger now: `ETL_DEV` for today, and `dev` for after the switch. The pipeline then just keeps firing correctly through that transition with no further changes needed.

**Claude**

That log tells the whole story, and importantly: **nothing went wrong — no bad commit landed, and no harm was done.** Here's what actually happened, reading between the lines:

1. `git status --porcelain -- ETL` found `Dev.json` "modified" — but this was a **line-ending artifact, not a real content difference**. My Python script explicitly forces LF line endings when it writes the file (`newline="\n"`), but this ADO Windows agent's git has `core.autocrlf` normalization enabled, which expects/wants CRLF in the working tree — hence that `warning: ... LF will be replaced by CRLF the next time Git touches it` line.
2. When the script ran `git add ETL`, git did exactly what it warned about: normalized the line endings back to CRLF as it staged the file — which made the staged version **byte-identical to what's already committed**.
3. So `git commit` correctly found nothing real to commit (`"nothing to commit, working tree clean"`), and `git push` correctly reported `"Everything up-to-date"` since there was no new commit to send.

So the "Changes detected" message was a false alarm caused by a line-ending mismatch, and git's own normalization caught it before anything spurious got committed — the system worked, just with a confusing intermediate message. (Separately: `HEAD detached at origin/...` is normal, expected ADO checkout behavior — by default it checks out a specific commit rather than a live branch, which is fine for this script since it explicitly pushes to `HEAD:$(Build.SourceBranchName)` regardless of that detached state.)

The clean, root-cause fix is a `.gitattributes` entry that forces a single, consistent line-ending policy for these files regardless of which OS or agent checks them out — that way there's nothing for the Windows agent's `autocrlf` and Python's explicit LF-writing to disagree about in the first place. Let me check if one exists and add it, plus still tighten up `discover_item_ids.py` to compare before writing (worth doing regardless, since "always claim I updated something" is misleading either way).

2. **`discover_item_ids.py`** now compares before writing, so it only touches the file (and only reports a change) when a value is genuinely different — matching how `enforce_parameterization.py` already behaves.

The reason: our current design is a lookup table of specific known items, not an auto-discovery system — it's very good at generic housekeeping (any Dataflow's own config) but has no way to know "this specific GUID showing up in another pipeline is a cross-reference worth tracking" without being told explicitly.

Is there a reason that any raw guid should ever exist in any dataflow or pipeline?  That would be a simple check to run each time if the answer is no.

---

**Claude**

Good instinct, and the answer is nuanced in a way that actually points to a real gap in what we've built — let me walk through it precisely rather than just saying yes/no.

**Within a Pipeline's `typeProperties` and `externalReferences` fields specifically: no, there's no legitimate reason for an unrecognized raw GUID to sit there.** Every GUID in those two places refers to something environment-specific — a workspace, an item, or a **connection**. That last one is actually a gap we haven't covered at all: activities like `RefreshSqlEndpoint`, `InvokePipeline`, and `PBISemanticModelRefresh` each have an `externalReferences.connection` GUID (we saw these earlier — `"connection": "198a2204-..."`, `"a3086a63-..."`, `"72dc2161-..."`) that our current checks don't look at whatsoever. Those are exactly as environment-specific as `dataflowId`/`workspaceId`, and right now nothing would catch a wrong or stale one. So generalizing the check would genuinely improve coverage, not just simplify the code.

**But it does not extend to every GUID in every file.** Two places have raw GUIDs that are *supposed* to be there, and would become false positives under a blanket check:
- **`.platform`'s `logicalId`** — this is Fabric's own git-integration bookkeeping, tracking "which physical item does this folder correspond to." It's meant to stay a literal GUID forever, generated by Fabric itself.
- **`queryMetadata.json`'s `queryId`** — same idea, one per query/table inside a Dataflow, internal identity bookkeeping, not environment config.

So the honest answer is: **the simple check is buildable, but scoped to the right surface** — scan every value inside a Pipeline activity's `typeProperties` and `externalReferences` generically (not a hand-maintained list of specific field names), flag any raw GUID that isn't already a recognized Variable Library expression, and explicitly leave `.platform`/`queryMetadata.json` alone since those GUIDs are supposed to be literal.

That's actually a nice simplification over what exists now — instead of maintaining `FIELD_VARIABLE_MAP` as a curated list of specific (activity type, field name) pairs, the check becomes "any GUID here that isn't in our known-good list gets flagged," which is both less code to maintain and catches the connection-ID gap for free. Want me to rebuild it that way?

---

**You**

What happens when you find one that doesn't have a pair somewhere else?

---

**Claude**

Same treatment as what we already built for `dataflowId`/`pipelineId`: it hard-fails, with the same clear reporting — which file, which activity, which field, what the GUID is. No difference in behavior based on *where* the unrecognized GUID turns up; the risk (this might point at something in a different environment, possibly production) is the same regardless of which field it's sitting in, so the response should be too.

One refinement worth making to the design, now that we're talking about connection IDs specifically: unlike `dataflowId`/`pipelineId` (where each activity's field points at a *different specific item*, so we have to match by the literal GUID value), a connection ID actually behaves more like `workspaceId` — a given activity type's connection is *always* supposed to point at "the Dev SQL connection" or "the Dev Semantic-Model-refresh connection," regardless of which specific pipeline it's in. That means connections fit the same `(activity type, field name) → variable name` pattern `FIELD_VARIABLE_MAP` already uses, not the GUID-by-GUID matching `ITEM_ID_VARIABLE_MAP` needs. So the real design is: extend `FIELD_VARIABLE_MAP` with entries for `externalReferences.connection` per activity type (three known ones to add now), and *underneath* that, keep a genuinely generic scan of every `typeProperties`/`externalReferences` value as a safety net — so that when you bring in a new pipeline with an activity type we haven't seen yet, an unrecognized GUID still gets caught and hard-fails instead of silently slipping through unenumerated.

Want me to build both pieces — the three connection-ID entries added to `FIELD_VARIABLE_MAP`, and the generic fallback scan for anything else?

**You**

Why do we want to maintain FIELD_VARIABLE_MAP rather than just keeping the variables in the Variable Library and erroring on any pipelines which don't have a dataflow/pipeline guid or dataflows which don't have a destination guid?

**Claude**

That's a real simplification, and I think it's the right one — it eliminates `FIELD_VARIABLE_MAP` entirely rather than just extending it, and it's more consistent with the "fail rather than guess" philosophy you already pushed toward for `dataflowId`/`pipelineId`.

Here's the reasoning for why this works: `FIELD_VARIABLE_MAP`'s auto-fix logic rests on an assumption — "any `RefreshDataflow`'s `workspaceId` is *always* supposed to be `ETLWorkspaceId`, no exceptions." That assumption happens to be true today, given this ETL solution lives in one workspace per environment. But it's still an assumption baked into code, not something derived from what's actually there — and if it were ever wrong (a future Dataflow needing a different Lakehouse, say), the auto-fixer would silently "fix" it to the wrong thing rather than failing. That's the same category of risk you flagged for untracked item references, just less visible because it's been reliably true so far.

Replacing it with your proposal: **stop trying to auto-fix anything, for either Dataflows or Pipelines. Just validate that the fields which are supposed to be Variable Library references actually are — `workspaceId`, `sqlEndpointId`, `groupId`, `datasetId`, `dataflowId`, `pipelineId`, `connection` for Pipelines, and `LakehouseWorkspaceId`/`LakehouseId`/`SchemaName` for Dataflows — and hard-fail on any of them that's still a raw literal.** No lookup table of "which specific variable does this field map to" needed at all, since we're not trying to guess the right variable anymore — we're just requiring that a human already picked one, the same way they already have to for `dataflowId`/`pipelineId`.

What this actually costs: right now, a hardcoded `workspaceId` gets silently fixed for you. Under this design, it fails instead, and a person has to go into the Fabric UI and wire up the Variable Library reference themselves before the pipeline goes green. Given where you've been landing on every decision this session — correctness over convenience, fail loudly rather than let something maybe-wrong slide through — that seems like the trade you'd want.

This also simplifies `enforce_parameterization.py`'s whole identity: it stops being an auto-fixer and becomes a pure validator/linter. Worth renaming at that point too, so its name matches what it actually does.

Want me to rebuild it this way — one uniform "these fields must already be Variable Library references, everything else gets left alone" check, no auto-fixing, for both files?

That said, I'd recommend using a plain **String** variable instead, for the same reason we picked String over Item Reference for `dataflowId`/`pipelineId`: the pipeline-integration docs specifically note that Connection reference and Item reference types need an extra `.` (dot notation) to pull out the actual connection ID property, since their value is a structured object, not a bare string. A plain String variable holding the connection GUID directly avoids that — it's referenced the exact same simple way `ETLWorkspaceId` already is, no special syntax, and it's the more mature, already-proven mechanism in this project rather than a newer type we haven't tested.

**Claude**

Actually, no — and this is an important correction to what would otherwise happen: **the pipeline will fail before it ever reaches the discovery step**, because "Data Availability" is still untracked.

Here's why: `enforce_parameterization.py` still exits with code 1 due to that one known untracked reference (we just re-confirmed this in the last test). The YAML step explicitly checks that exit code and propagates the failure (`exit $LASTEXITCODE`), and ADO's default behavior is to stop the job the moment a step fails — it won't continue on to installing packages, running discovery, or committing anything. So on this push, you'd see a **red, failed pipeline run**, stopped at the "Run standardization and parameterization scripts" step, with the log clearly identifying "Data Availability" as the reason. Nothing downstream runs at all. That's not a bug — it's the hard-fail behavior we deliberately built, doing exactly its job.

That'll keep happening on every future push to `ETL_DEV`/`dev` that touches `ETL/`, until "Data Availability" gets resolved one way or the other (brought into tracking, or fixed by hand in the Fabric UI).

**Once that's resolved**, then yes, your original expectation becomes accurate: the pipeline would run clean through the parameterization check, proceed to install packages and run discovery, and discovery *might* find something to update (a newly-tracked item's ID, or any other drifted value) — if it does, it auto-commits and pushes that fix, which re-triggers the same pipeline once more (should run clean the second time, no further changes, no loop) — and *that* commit is what you'd then need to go sync into the Fabric workspace via Update, on top of whatever you already synced from the first push.

So: two syncs are realistically likely the first time this whole thing runs for real — one for your manual commit, a possible second one for whatever the pipeline's own discovery step finds and commits back — but only after "Data Availability" stops blocking the pipeline entirely.

---

**Claude**

Here's the current, correct procedure — Dataflow Gen2 doesn't have a one-click "move to another workspace" button, so this is an export/import process:

1. **In the source workspace**, open "Data Availability," and from the toolbar use the **Export template** option — this saves a `.pqt` (Power Query Template) file to your machine.
2. **In the Dev ETL workspace** (the one this repo is connected to), create a new Dataflow Gen2, and choose **Import from Power Query template** — upload the `.pqt` file you just exported.
3. **Reconfigure the destination settings** — this is the part that doesn't carry over automatically. The imported dataflow will still be pointing at wherever it used to write data; you'll need to set its destination to the same Lakehouse the other four dataflows use, matching the `DefaultDestination` pattern (`WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`). Scheduled refresh settings also don't transfer, though that's probably not relevant here since this dataflow is triggered by the Pipeline's `RefreshDataflow` activity, not its own independent schedule.

Once it exists in the Dev workspace with the right destination:

4. **Commit it to git** via Source Control, the same way the other four got in — this creates `ETL/Dataflows/Data Availability.Dataflow/` in the repo.
5. From there, the existing tooling takes over: `standardize_dataflows.py` will apply the `DefaultDestination`/`BindToDefaultDestination` pattern, and once you add the one `ITEM_NAME_MAP` line we discussed, `discover_item_ids.py` creates its variable and finds its real ID automatically.

Want me to add the `ITEM_NAME_MAP` entry now so it's ready the moment step 4 is done, or wait until it's actually committed?

**You**

I don't recall a discussion of ITEM_NAME_MAP.  What does that have that the Variable Library and variable references can't?

---

**Claude**

Fair to ask again — here's the actual gap it fills.

The Variable Library only stores **name → value** pairs (`DF_Orders` → `51c35d8c-...` in Dev). It has no idea that `DF_Orders` corresponds to the folder `ETL/Dataflows/RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental.Dataflow/` — that association isn't recorded anywhere in `variables.json` or the value-sets. Nothing in the Variable Library's own data links a variable back to a physical item in this repo.

`discover_item_ids.py` specifically needs that link to do its job: for a given folder, it has to (1) read that folder's `.platform` file to get the item's current display name — "RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental" — since that's what it searches for in Fabric's list of workspace items, and (2) know which variable name to write the discovered ID into once it finds a match. Neither of those is derivable from the Variable Library alone; it needs to start from "here's a folder" and end at "here's the right variable," and nothing else in the system records that connection.

Could that connection be derived automatically instead of hand-maintained? We covered this when I first built it: folder names don't reduce to clean variable names in any reliable, mechanical way — "RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental" and "RPC ERP Dataflows - Hourly Incremental" don't share a consistent shape you could pattern-match generically, especially for names we haven't seen yet. The alternative would be using something unambiguous like the folder path itself, or Fabric's own `logicalId`, as the variable name directly — but that trades away the whole point of `DF_Orders` being a readable, meaningful name in the Variable Library.

So `ITEM_NAME_MAP` isn't duplicating what the Variable Library does — it's the one small piece of human judgment (picking a name) that has to exist somewhere, kept as plain data rather than folded into any of the per-field/per-GUID logic we eliminated earlier.

---

**You**

I see.  So you can't tell which item something is without tying its variable to that item.  Actually you could, but you've have to use Item type variables.  Should we consider using those?

---

**Claude**

Good question, but I don't think it would eliminate `ITEM_NAME_MAP` — it would change how a *human* sees the variable in the Fabric UI, not what the *script* needs to know.

Here's why: even with an Item Reference variable, something still has to explicitly set which item each value-set's reference points to — recall from the Item Reference docs: *"the reference is static... deploying to a new stage, these references still point to the original workspace unless manually updated."* That "manually updated" part is exactly what `discover_item_ids.py` automates today. Switching the variable's *type* from String to Item Reference doesn't remove the need for the script to know two things: which folder to read the current display name from, and which variable to write the result into. That association — folder ↔ variable — has to exist somewhere regardless of what shape the variable's value takes, because the Variable Library itself has no concept of "which git folder does this variable represent." Item Reference wouldn't give the *script* that link any more than String does; it's still something we'd have to tell it, which is exactly what `ITEM_NAME_MAP` is.

What Item Reference *would* actually buy you: if a human is looking directly at the Variable Library in the Fabric UI, an Item Reference variable resolves to a friendly picker/display ("Orders Dataflow") instead of a raw GUID string sitting there. That's a real, but narrow, benefit — and it only helps for manual browsing/editing in the UI, which isn't really how we're using this anymore, since `discover_item_ids.py` already automates the whole write path via the API.

It also reintroduces the two things we specifically avoided when choosing String over Item Reference earlier: the value becomes a structured `{workspaceId, itemId}` pair rather than a plain string (meaning the script would need to handle that shape when writing it, and the pipeline expression would need the `.itemId` dot-notation rather than a bare reference), and we don't actually need the bundled workspace ID here anyway, since every Dataflow/Pipeline in this project lives in the same workspace already covered by `WS_ETL`.

So I'd stick with String variables and `ITEM_NAME_MAP` — the naming association is unavoidable either way, and Item Reference would add complexity back in for a benefit (nicer UI browsing) that doesn't really apply to a fully-scripted workflow.

---

**You**

What does "which folder to read the current display name from" mean?

---

**Claude**

Concretely: each Dataflow/Pipeline folder has a `.platform` file with a `metadata.displayName` field — the exact name Fabric shows for that item (e.g., opening `ETL/Dataflows/RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental.Dataflow/.platform` gives you the string `"RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental"`).

When `discover_item_ids.py` asks Fabric's API "list every Dataflow/Pipeline in this workspace," Fabric hands back a list of items with their current display names and IDs — but nothing in that list says "this one corresponds to that folder in your git repo." Fabric has no concept of your repo's folder structure at all. So the script has to bridge that gap itself: for each item it's trying to discover, it opens that item's `.platform` file, reads the `displayName` out of it, and then searches Fabric's returned list for an entry whose display name matches exactly. Whichever one matches, it takes that entry's `id` — that's the discovered GUID.

So "which folder to read the current display name from" means exactly that lookup step: for the `DF_DataAvailability` variable, the script needs to know its corresponding folder is `ETL/Dataflows/Data Availability.Dataflow/`, so it can open *that* folder's `.platform` file, pull out `"Data Availability"`, and search for an item named exactly that in the Fabric API response. Without `ITEM_NAME_MAP` telling it "this variable ↔ this folder," it wouldn't know where to go get the name to search for in the first place.

---

**You**

Would it make sense to rename dataflows and variables so that they could match exactly?  Would that solve this issue?

---

**Claude**

Yes, that would actually work — but it trades one kind of simplicity for another, worth being clear about which.

If the Dataflow/Pipeline names in Fabric itself were simplified to match a predictable pattern — say, just "Orders" instead of "RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental" — then a trivial, fully mechanical rule (strip spaces/punctuation, prefix `DF_`/`PL_`) would reliably produce the right variable name every time: "Orders" → `DF_Orders`, "Product" → `DF_Product`. At that point `discover_item_ids.py` wouldn't need `ITEM_NAME_MAP` at all — it could just compute the expected variable name straight from each folder's display name, matching your existing convention exactly.

The reason this doesn't work *today* isn't a limitation in the tooling — it's that the current names ("RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental," "RPC ERP Dataflows - Hourly Incremental") don't reduce to `DF_Orders`/`PL_ERPDataflowsHourly` through any simple, consistent rule. A mechanical strip-and-prefix on the *current* names would produce something like `DF_RPCERPOrdersIncremental` — technically deterministic, but uglier and not what you actually want to see in the Variable Library.

So the real question is whether you want to rename the underlying items to enable this. A few things worth knowing before deciding:

- **It's safe in the sense that matters most**: renaming a Fabric item doesn't change its ID — it's a metadata change, not a delete-and-recreate, so nothing about the discovered GUIDs or pipeline references would break.
- **It does mean a git folder rename**, since the folder name mirrors the display name — you'd commit that rename through Source Control like any other change.
- **The code impact is small**: I checked, and `ITEM_NAME_MAP` is the *only* place any folder name is hardcoded anywhere in this tooling — `standardize_dataflows.py` and `enforce_parameterization.py` both glob generically. So a rename just means updating a couple of dictionary keys, nothing cascades further.
- **The real tradeoff is human-facing, not technical**: "RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental" is more descriptive when someone's just browsing the workspace in the Fabric UI, especially as you bring in a lot more items — the "RPC ERP" grouping helps scanning a long list. Shortening everything to "Orders"/"Product" reads cleaner in the Variable Library, but loses that context in the item list itself.

If you're fine with that trade, this is a real simplification worth making — want me to sketch the naming rule and figure out what the equivalent short names would look like for the current five items plus "Data Availability," so you can decide if you like how they'd read?

**Claude**

That's a very reasonable place to pause — this genuinely did surface more complexity than a simple "auto-fix hardcoded values" task usually would, precisely because it kept exposing real questions (stale IDs, untracked references, naming conventions, Prod's actual deployment mechanism) rather than staying a small bolt-on. **Built, tested, and working (all uncommitted, sitting in the `ETL_DEV` working tree):**

- `discover_item_ids.py` rebuilt to scan every Dataflow/Pipeline folder and auto-create missing variables, with `ITEM_NAME_MAP` as the one remaining human-decided piece (naming).

**Open, undecided questions worth bringing to the discussion:**
- Whether to rename the Dataflow/Pipeline items in Fabric to short, consistent names so `ITEM_NAME_MAP` could eventually be eliminated entirely — real tradeoff between automation-friendliness and readability in the Fabric UI as more items get added.
- The Prod-side deployment mechanism (`fabric-cicd` or otherwise) is still just a discussed direction, not built — everything so far is Dev-only by design.

Nothing's committed, so there's no cleanup needed if the answer comes back "not worth it right now" — it's all just sitting in the working tree until you decide.

**You**

In the future, when creating dataflows how do we put variables into the destination?  We did it manually for these, I think.

---

**Claude**

Good question, and worth nailing down since this repo's whole safety-net design assumes people are building these by hand sometimes. Here's the actual Fabric mechanism for wiring a new Dataflow's destination to the Variable Library — confirmed against Microsoft's docs, not something obvious from the UI alone:

**Fastest, most reliable option for you specifically: start every new Dataflow from `_Template`.** It already has the `DefaultDestination` query correctly wired to `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`/`SchemaName`, plus the `DefaultOutputDestinationSettings` header. Duplicate it (or paste its mashup.pq into a new Dataflow's Advanced Editor) and you inherit the whole binding for free — you'd just add your data queries and set `BindToDefaultDestination = true` on each, which is a normal checkbox/property, not something requiring variable wiring at all.

**If building the binding from scratch instead** (useful to know for whenever `_Template` doesn't fit, or just to understand what's actually happening):

1. Go to **Home → Options → Data destinations**, and check **"Enable advanced edit for data destination queries."** Fabric warns that this can break the dataflow if misused — that warning is real, take it seriously.
2. Create the three variable-holding queries as **blank queries** (Get data → Blank query), one per variable, each renamed to match the variable and with its Source step set to `Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/WS_Platform)", "<default GUID>")` (and the same pattern for `LH_Lakehouse` and `SchemaName`).
3. In the **Queries pane**, right-click the destination query and choose **Advanced editor**, then replace the hardcoded `workspaceId`/`lakehouseId`/schema values with references to those three queries by name (not by re-typing the GUIDs).

One important, easy-to-miss catch straight from Microsoft's own docs: *"After you modify a destination query using advanced edit, the standard UI for changing that destination no longer works. You must continue to use the advanced editor for any further modifications to that destination."* So once you go this route for a given Dataflow, you're committed to the Advanced Editor for that destination going forward — another reason starting from `_Template` (which already made that trade once) is the lower-friction path.

Either way, `standardize_dataflows.py`/`enforce_parameterization.py` remain the safety net for whenever someone skips this and hardcodes something instead — but starting from `_Template` should mean they rarely need to catch anything for the destination fields specifically.

**You**

---

**Claude**

You're right to push back — that's not quite accurate, and I should correct it rather than let a wrong assumption stand.

You're correct that mashup.pq is fundamentally the ingest/transformation script. But Fabric's specific `DefaultDestination` pattern (the one `_Template` and your existing Dataflows use) *does* partly live inside mashup.pq — the `DefaultDestination` shared query and the `BindToDefaultDestination` attribute on each data query are real M-code constructs sitting right there in the file. However, that's only half the picture: `standardize_dataflows.py` also has to write a matching entry into `queryMetadata.json` (a `queryId`, `isHidden`, `loadEnabled` flags for that same "DefaultDestination" name) for Fabric to actually recognize it as a legitimate destination rather than just a coincidentally-named query. So the destination binding is really split across *two* files, not fully contained in mashup.pq alone.

That means my suggestion to "paste the mashup.pq into a new Dataflow's Advanced Editor" doesn't actually hold up — the per-query Advanced Editor in the Fabric UI edits one query's M formula at a time, not a whole multi-query file, and it has no way to touch `queryMetadata.json` at all. So hand-pasting text wouldn't reliably reproduce the full destination setup even for the parts that do live in mashup.pq, and it can't touch the part that doesn't.

Let me actually verify what Fabric supports for cloning an *entire* Dataflow item (not just its script) within the same workspace, rather than keep guessing at the mechanism — I don't want to give you a second inaccurate answer on this.

---

**Claude**

Confirmed — my "paste the mashup.pq" suggestion was wrong, and there isn't a clean fix for it: **Dataflow Gen2 doesn't have a native "duplicate/Save As" feature at all yet** — Microsoft has only "confirmed plans" for one, not shipped it. So there's no simple right-click "copy this item" either.

The reliable way to actually clone `_Template`'s full setup (both the mashup.pq destination binding *and* its matching `queryMetadata.json` entries) is the same **export/import via Power Query Template (.pqt)** mechanism we already covered for migrating "Data Availability" — just within the same workspace this time instead of across workspaces:

1. Open `_Template`, use **Export template** from the ribbon to save a `.pqt` file.
2. Create a new Dataflow Gen2 in the same workspace, and choose **Import from Power Query template**, uploading that file.

This round-trips through Fabric's own internal representation of the item, so it correctly carries over the `DefaultDestination` query, the `BindToDefaultDestination` bindings, and the matching `queryMetadata.json` entries together — not just the M-code text, which is exactly what a hand-paste into the Advanced Editor would have missed.

There's a secondary option worth knowing about, though I'm less certain it fully preserves the destination metadata: Fabric does support **copying individual queries and pasting them into another Dataflow Gen2 in the same workspace** (a proper query-level copy operation in the Power Query UI, not text paste). That might carry the binding over correctly too, but since I can't verify that with the same confidence as the documented template export/import path, I'd treat the template method as the one to actually rely on, and the query-copy approach as something to test carefully before trusting it.

**You**

Should we wrap _Template into a .pqt file?  Is that just a json under the hood?

---

**Claude**

That's a great offer — if you've got an actual `.pqt` file, inspecting the real thing beats trusting web research summaries. Point me at its path and I'll unzip it and look directly.

To answer both questions with what I found so far (worth verifying against your real file, not just trusting this):

**Is it just JSON?** Mostly, but not entirely. A `.pqt` is a ZIP archive containing exactly four files: `MashupDocument.pq` (the actual M script — plain text, not JSON), `MashupMetadata.json` and `Metadata.json` (JSON metadata), and `[Content_Types].xml` (a boilerplate MIME-type manifest, the same kind of thing Office Open XML formats like `.xlsx`/`.docx` also carry). So it's "M script + a couple of JSON files + one small XML boilerplate," all zipped together — not purely JSON, but mostly text-based and inspectable, not a binary blob.

**Should we wrap `_Template` into a `.pqt`?** More promising than I expected, actually — I found there's already prior art for this: an open-source tool (`jpmicrosoft/pqt_generator` on GitHub) specifically built to "automate the conversion of Microsoft Fabric Dataflow Gen2 exports into portable Power Query Template (.pqt) files." That means programmatically generating a `.pqt` directly from our git-tracked `mashup.pq`/`queryMetadata.json` — rather than manually exporting through the Fabric UI every time — is a solved problem, not something we'd be pioneering blind. That would also sidestep the staleness issue a manually-exported, checked-in `.pqt` would otherwise have (it'd need re-exporting by hand every time `_Template` changes; a generator script wouldn't).

Send me the path to your existing `.pqt` and I'll confirm the exact structure against a real example before we commit to anything.

Inspected the real file — genuinely useful, and it reveals something important that changes the calculus here.

**Confirming the format:** `MashupDocument.pq` is plain M text (not JSON) — in this case, a self-contained query computing a DST-aware "data freshness" timestamp for Central time, no source connection at all. `MashupMetadata.json` is simple, learnable JSON — just a `QueriesMetadata` array with `QueryName`/`LoadEnabled`/`IsHidden` per query. `Metadata.json` is trivial (`Name`/`Description`/`Version`). `[Content_Types].xml` is **fixed, unchanging boilerplate** — the exact same content in every `.pqt`, so a generator wouldn't even need to think about it.

That changes the value of "should we wrap `_Template` into a `.pqt`": yes, it's genuinely buildable (the format is simple enough, and there's prior art for generating these programmatically) — but it would only save you from retyping the relatively small `DefaultDestination` scaffolding text itself. It wouldn't save the actual tedious part (reconfiguring the Lakehouse destination binding after import), since that never travels through a `.pqt` regardless of how it's produced. Given that, I'd weigh whether it's worth building versus just keeping `_Template` as a live Dataflow you copy from via the same export/import dance each time — the automation saves less than it initially looked like it would.

4. Reuses the fixed `[Content_Types].xml` we already confirmed is identical every time,
5. Zips those four into a `.pqt`,

I tested an import and it works.  What's a generator script?

---

**Claude**

A **generator script** would be a Python script that builds a valid `.pqt` file directly from what's already in this repo, without going through the Fabric UI's Export Template button at all.

Concretely, it would:

1. **Read a Dataflow's existing `mashup.pq`** from the repo (e.g., `_Template`'s, or any of the four we already track) — this becomes the `MashupDocument.pq` content, verbatim, including whatever destination binding is already wired up (like `_Template`'s `DefaultDestination` pointing at `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`).
2. **Parse out the query names** from that mashup.pq (the `shared X = ...` declarations — the same kind of parsing `enforce_parameterization.py` already does) and generate a matching `MashupMetadata.json`, with a `QueriesMetadata` entry per query (name, `LoadEnabled`, `IsHidden` flags).
3. **Generate a trivial `Metadata.json`** (just `Name`/`Description`/`Version` — we saw this is nearly nothing).
4. **Reuse the fixed `[Content_Types].xml`** we already confirmed is identical byte-for-byte across both files you showed me — no per-item variation needed there at all.
5. **Zip those four files together** into a `.pqt`.

The payoff: instead of manually exporting `_Template` through the Fabric UI every time someone wants to start a new, correctly-wired Dataflow, they'd run this script and get a ready-to-import `.pqt` generated fresh from whatever's currently committed in git — so it can never go stale the way a one-time manually-exported file would, and it's guaranteed to match whatever `_Template` (or any source dataflow) actually looks like right now.

Good news that your import test worked — that resolves the one open uncertainty from before (whether Fabric's import side accepts a hand-shaped destination reference the same way it accepts its own export). Want me to build it now, or do you want to sort out the "Data Availability" workspace/Lakehouse question first, since that's still an open flag from a couple messages back?

**You**

Is it possible to edit a .pqt file somehow in code?

**Claude**

Yes, straightforward — a `.pqt` is just a ZIP archive, and Python's `zipfile` module reads and writes those directly. Since `MashupDocument.pq` inside it is plain text (M code), you can extract it, apply ordinary string/regex transformations — the exact same kind `enforce_parameterization.py`/`standardize_dataflows.py` already do on the git-tracked `mashup.pq` files — and write a modified `.pqt` back out. Nothing about the format resists editing in code.

That's actually directly useful for the exact file you just showed me: `DataAvailability_Dest.pqt` uses the old per-query `DataDestinations` pattern, pointing at a workspace/Lakehouse that doesn't match our Dev setup. An editor script could:

1. Open the `.pqt`, pull out `MashupDocument.pq`.
2. Convert the `DataDestinations`/`X_DataDestination` pattern into our `DefaultDestination`/`BindToDefaultDestination` pattern — essentially reusing `standardize_dataflows.py`'s existing transformation logic, just applied to text extracted from a zip instead of a file on disk.
3. Swap the hardcoded destination GUIDs for `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse` Variable Library references (once we've settled where this dataflow's data should actually go).
4. Re-zip it into a corrected `.pqt`.

The result would be a ready-to-import file that already matches this repo's conventions, rather than something you'd still need to hand-fix in the Fabric UI after importing. That's a more targeted, immediately useful version of the "generator script" idea — less "build `_Template` from scratch," more "fix up whatever gets exported before it ever touches the Dev workspace."

Still worth resolving first: where "Data Availability" is actually supposed to write to, since that determines what the corrected destination should even say. **Claude**

Good clarifying questions — let me be precise about what I actually did versus what you'd do manually.

**What I did**: I never "opened" it in the sense of a UI at all — I used Python's `zipfile` module (via a script) to read the archive's contents directly in memory, without extracting anything to disk. That's a code-level operation, not something that requires any file manager or editor.

**If you want to look inside it yourself, manually:** yes — make a copy of the file and rename the copy's extension from `.pqt` to `.zip`. Since a `.pqt` genuinely *is* a ZIP archive under the hood (just with a different extension as a label), Windows will then treat it as a normal compressed folder — you can either double-click it to peek inside without fully unzipping, or right-click → **Extract All** to pull the four files out into a real folder on disk.

**For VS Code specifically**: it doesn't browse inside zip archives in the Explorer sidebar natively out of the box — a `.pqt`/`.zip` just shows up as one opaque file. The reliable path is the same as above: rename the copy to `.zip`, extract it to a real folder (via Windows Explorer, or `Expand-Archive` in PowerShell), and *then* VS Code's Explorer will show those extracted files completely normally, since at that point they're just ordinary files on disk. There are VS Code extensions that claim to let you browse inside a zip archive directly without extracting, but I'm not confident enough about their current state to recommend one over just extracting — that's the option that's guaranteed to work with zero extra tooling.

It's also the most recently updated option among the alternatives I found (January 2026), which is a reasonable proxy for it still being actively maintained.

One alternative worth knowing about if you ever want to actually *edit* files inside a zip directly in VS Code (not just peek): **[ZipFS](https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=arcanis.vscode-zipfs)** mounts a zip as a virtual filesystem you can read and write in place. Given you're planning to just keep a live template in the Dev workspace and copy it via export/import rather than script-editing `.pqt` files, Zip Viewer's read-only preview is probably the better fit — simpler, and matches what you'd actually use it for (an occasional look inside, not a workflow).

**You**

When I asked about credentials, I wasn't thinking of the destination but the source. When I first opened each dataflow, I had to re-sync those credentials, and you noted that they had changed. Is that something that should be made into a variable?

---

**Claude**

Good clarification — and this is actually two separate things, worth untangling, because only one of them is something a variable can fix.

**The server/database identifiers** (`"192.168.0.10:448"`, `"S21D504V"`) are literal strings sitting in the M code — those *could* become Variable Library variables, the same way everything else has, if they actually differ between Dev and Prod (e.g., a separate Dev DB2 instance vs. the real production one).

**But the re-authentication you had to do isn't about those string values at all — it's Fabric's credential store, and no variable touches that.** Power Query/Fabric never stores actual DB2 credentials (username/password) in the M code or anywhere in the dataflow's definition — credentials are held separately, keyed to the exact (server, database, workspace) combination. When a Dataflow with that connection signature shows up in a workspace for the first time — whether because it's genuinely new, or because it was copied/imported via `.pqt` — Fabric has no stored credential for that combination yet in *that* workspace, so it prompts you to re-authenticate. That would happen **even if the server/database were perfectly parameterized as variables** — turning them into `Variable.ValueOrDefault(...)` calls changes what value gets passed into `DB2.Database(...)`, but it doesn't change the fact that Fabric still needs someone to establish trust for whatever server/database that resolves to, in that specific workspace. That's inherent to how credentials work across Fabric/Power BI generally (never silently inherited across copies, for good security reasons), not something specific to how we've built this.

So: worth parameterizing the connection string values only if they genuinely differ Dev vs. Prod — do they? If it's the same physical DB2 server/database for both (which would match the pattern you already have for the Lakehouse — `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse` don't vary by environment either, only the schema does), then there's no real benefit to variable-izing something that never changes. If there *is* a separate Dev/test DB2 instance, that's a legitimate case for a new variable, following the same pattern as everything else — but it still wouldn't eliminate the one-time re-auth step you're describing; that's just an unavoidable part of standing up a Dataflow in a workspace for the first time.

---

**You**

Ok.  So we need to test this.  Once I've authenticated into one dataflow then can that connection string guid be made into a variable and re-used?

---

**Claude**

Good instinct to test this rather than guess — and I should be upfront that I'm genuinely less certain here than I've been on the rest of this, so testing is the right call.

Here's what I do know: unlike the Pipeline `externalReferences.connection` GUIDs (which are explicit, visible fields in `pipeline-content.json`), a Dataflow's connection to a source like DB2 isn't represented as an explicit GUID anywhere in `mashup.pq` — Power Query resolves credentials implicitly, by matching the connector type and its arguments (server, database) against whatever's already stored, not through a reference you can see or edit in the M text. So there's likely no "connection string GUID" sitting in the git-tracked files for us to pull into a variable the way we did for the Pipeline connections.

That said, Fabric does have a real, separate concept that might be exactly what you're getting at: a shareable **Connection** resource (visible under "Manage connections and gateways" in the Fabric UI), especially relevant here since `192.168.0.10:448` looks like an internal address — meaning this DB2 source is almost certainly routed through an **On-premises Data Gateway**, and gateway-routed connections are exactly the kind of thing Fabric lets multiple items bind to the same stored Connection object, rather than each one authenticating independently.

So the actual test worth running: authenticate the DB2 source on one Dataflow, then when opening the *next* one that also hits `192.168.0.10:448`/`S21D504V`, check whether Fabric's credential prompt offers **"use an existing connection"** rather than making you re-enter credentials from scratch. If it does, that confirms the Connection is a shared, reusable Fabric resource — and worth checking, at that point, whether anything actually changes in the git-tracked files (`.platform`, `mashup.pq`, `queryMetadata.json`) after binding to it, or whether this is purely a Fabric-side association that never shows up in git at all. If nothing in git changes, then this isn't something Variable Library parameterization can capture or needs to — it'd just be "authenticate once, reuse via Fabric's own connection picker," independent of everything we've built so far.

Want to run that test and report back what you see, or should I look into whether there's a way to check the current connection bindings via the Fabric REST API instead of relying on the UI prompt?

---

**You**

Yes I'll test that.  Last question for today.  Deploying Pipeline, Dataflow, and Variable Library stuff turned out to involve more code and challenge than we anticipated, even with fabric-cicd.  Is there any reason to think that Semantic Model or Report deployment would be significantly easier?

---

**Claude**

Some real reason for optimism, but not a clean "yes, much easier" — it's genuinely mixed, worth being honest about both sides.

**Where Semantic Models/Reports have a real head start:** the "auto-rebind on promotion" problem — the core thing we had to build all this custom tooling for with Dataflows/Pipelines — has mature, native support for Semantic Models and Reports that Dataflows/Pipelines simply don't have. Fabric's Deployment Pipelines have supported "deployment rules" for semantic model data-source and parameter rebinding since the Power BI Premium days, well before "Fabric" existed as a brand — years of investment, not something bolted on recently. Report → Semantic Model rebinding on promotion is similarly mature. And for scripted manipulation specifically, **Tabular Editor** is a genuinely proven, purpose-built tool with a long track record — notably, it's already sitting in your own dead `pipeline_build_dataset.yml`/`azure-pipelines.yml` files, which tells you someone already reached for the right, established tool for this job rather than needing to build something from scratch, unlike our situation with Dataflows/Pipelines where nothing off-the-shelf fit well.

**Where I'd expect the same categories of friction to resurface, though:** the credential/connection re-authentication issue we just discussed for the DB2 source isn't specific to Dataflows — a Semantic Model connecting to a Lakehouse/Warehouse SQL endpoint for the first time in a new workspace would likely need the same one-time credential binding. And the underlying data-source connection details for a Semantic Model live in files like `expressions.tmdl` — notably, your own `.gitignore` already excludes that file entirely (`**/definition/expressions.tmdl`), which is a pretty strong signal someone already hit this exact "environment-specific connection string in a tracked file" problem and worked around it by just not tracking it, rather than solving it cleanly. That's the same shape of problem we spent most of today on, just handled with a blunter tool. And `fabric-cicd`'s own Semantic Model support isn't friction-free either — there's enough community writing specifically about "getting a *working* Direct Lake semantic model deployed with fabric-cicd" that it clearly isn't a solved, paved path either.

So: expect a real head start on the promotion/rebinding piece specifically, thanks to mature tooling that's been battle-tested far longer than anything in the Dataflow/Pipeline space — but not a free pass on the credential and connection-string categories of pain, which seem to be more fundamental to how Fabric handles environment-specific bindings generally, not particular to what we built today.

**You**

Working with Tabular Editor for ADO Pipeline deployments was a huge hassle that a week of work didn't allow me to finish in the spring of 2025.  It's helpful for other things, but deployments were poorly documented and buggy.  If they've made huge strides then I'm interested, but only if they've done that.

**Claude**

Genuine investment, but not yet at the bar you're describing — worth being precise about the difference.

There's a **brand new, dedicated Tabular Editor CLI** (distinct from the old TE2 `-DEPLOY` command-line flag your dead YAML files used) that's specifically targeting this — over 50 commands spanning inspect/edit/validate/**deploy**/refresh/test, explicitly built for "using it in automated workflows." The most recent release (0.6.0) is described by its own team as a **"hardening release... no flashy new features but solid foundations"** — that framing is meaningful: it's an explicit acknowledgment that the deploy/automation path needed reliability work, not cosmetic polish. That's a real signal they know this was a weak spot, which lines up with what you experienced.

But it's **still Limited Public Preview** — and notably, the current preview build has a hard expiration date of **2026-09-30**, after which it stops functioning entirely. That's not "shipped and stable," that's "actively being reworked, don't build a long-term dependency on this exact build." It is backward-compatible with the old TE2 CLI syntax, so nothing breaks if you'd tried the old path — but that also means the thing you struggled with a year and a half ago hasn't been replaced by something GA yet, just by something newer and preview-stage.

So: genuine strides on the *specific* thing that burned you (deployment reliability, hardening, automation focus) — but not yet the maturity bar you're asking about. If you're willing to treat it as "promising, worth a fresh look, but still evolving and time-boxed," it's worth trying. If your bar is "stable and done," it isn't there yet — I'd wait for it to reach general availability before betting real deployment work on it.

**You**

I was thinking that you'd suggested fabric-cicd was the new way, not tabular editor.

**Claude**

Good catch — you're right, and I should reconcile that clearly rather than leave two different tools hanging there unresolved.

I did spend this whole session building around `fabric-cicd`, and it does explicitly support Semantic Models and Reports as item types — so for **deployment** specifically, that's the more consistent recommendation: one tool, one mechanism, across Dataflows, Pipelines, Variable Library, *and* Semantic Models, rather than mixing in Tabular Editor as a second, separate deployment path. I brought Tabular Editor up because it happened to be sitting in your dead YAML files, which was a fair observation about tooling maturity in general — but it wasn't a considered recommendation over `fabric-cicd` for the deployment step, and given your actual experience with it, it shouldn't be.

Tabular Editor still earns its place for *other* things — it's genuinely strong at model editing, DAX-aware scripting, and Best Practice Analyzer-style validation (that's literally what your dead `pipeline_build_dataset.yml` was using it for, separate from the deployment step). That's a different job than "push this model to a target workspace," and there's no reason to give up a good editing/validation tool just because its deployment story burned you.

So, revisiting my actual answer through the `fabric-cicd` lens specifically, since that's the tool that matters here: I found earlier that there's enough community writing specifically titled around "deploying a *working* Direct Lake semantic model with fabric-cicd" that its Semantic Model support isn't friction-free either — similar in kind to what we ran into today with Dataflows, not a clean pass. The genuine advantage fabric-cicd would bring is the same one I mentioned before: Fabric's native auto-rebind support for semantic model data-source/parameter connections is more mature than anything that exists for Dataflows/Pipelines, so some of today's pain (chasing down cross-item references by hand) is less likely to recur. But I wouldn't call it "solved" — expect real complexity, just a different flavor of it, and plan to use `fabric-cicd` as the deployment mechanism with Tabular Editor kept around for editing/validation, not as competing deployment options.
