**You**

Are there problems with us creating dataflows in Prod and then bringing over to Dev later, if we keep both workspaces in Fabric Workspace Git?  We'd just update all the variables, so it seems like which direction we "promote" wouldn't matter at that point.

---

**Claude**

The specific thing you're pointing at is genuinely true: since content is now environment-agnostic (just variable references, not baked-in values), the *file itself* doesn't care which workspace it was first authored in. That part of your reasoning holds up. But there are real problems with building in Prod first that have nothing to do with environment parameterization — they're about what Dev is actually *for*, and parameterization doesn't touch that at all.

**The new-item discovery/registration step would happen against Prod first.** We spent this whole session building tooling specifically because a brand-new item gets a raw GUID the moment it's created, and needs to be caught, registered, and wired up correctly. If that item is created in Prod first, the *first real exercise* of that whole discovery/registration process — including any bugs in `ITEM_NAME_MAP`, the discovery script, or a human forgetting to add the entry — happens directly against production, not a safe sandbox. Same for the credential/connection binding we just discussed for a new data source: the first live authentication and first live query run would be against Prod's actual infrastructure.

**More fundamentally: Dev's real job isn't holding different config values — it's catching logic bugs before they're business-critical.** Variable Library solves "does this point at the right workspace/item." It does nothing for "does this DAX/M transformation actually compute the right numbers," "does this filter condition do what I think it does," or "did I introduce a bug like the DST calculation in Data Availability." Building directly in Prod means whatever mistakes exist in the actual logic surface in production first, potentially in front of real stakeholders looking at real reports, rather than getting caught in a throwaway environment where a wrong answer costs nothing.

**There's also a git-discipline cost to bidirectional promotion**, separate from the technical parameterization question. If sometimes Dev merges into Prod and sometimes Prod merges into Dev, ad hoc, it gets harder to answer "which branch represents the actual current, intended state of things" — most teams want one clear direction so Prod is always a known, trailing subset of what's already been validated in Dev, not two branches that can each originate independent work and cross-pollinate unpredictably.

So: you're right that parameterization removes one specific reason to insist on a strict direction, but it doesn't remove the reasons that actually matter most — bug-catching before it's live, and a clean, legible git/promotion history. I'd still keep Dev as the place new things get built and validated first, even with both workspaces git-connected and fully parameterized.

---

**You**

I'm thinking that regular dataflows which don't do any incremental loading will be simple enough to build in Prod.  We've been doing that up until now and it's been fine.  Only complex loading scenarios need to be tested.  So it's not like we'd be intentionally migrating from Prod to Dev, but it might happen at some point that someone merges main into dev for other reasons and then the other items would come along.

---

**Claude**

That reframing changes things meaningfully — the concerns I raised were really about deliberately using Prod as the default place *new, untested* things get built. What you're describing is narrower and, I think, genuinely fine: simple dataflows you've already been building directly in Prod safely, occasionally sweeping into Dev as a side effect of routine branch maintenance, not as an intentional promotion.

Walking through it against what we've built: when one of these simple dataflows lands in Dev's branch via an unrelated `main`→`dev` merge, and someone eventually syncs that into the Dev workspace, Fabric creates a new physical item there with a new GUID — which triggers exactly the same discovery/registration flow we already built, just running in the Dev direction instead of the Prod direction. Nothing new needs to happen; it's the same "new item shows up, gets caught, gets registered, gets its ID discovered" process, just less risky than usual since it's landing in Dev, not Prod. So the bootstrapping concern I raised earlier doesn't really apply here — if anything, this direction is safer than the Dev→Prod case, not riskier.

The logic-validation concern is the one that actually matters, and you've already drawn the right line: simple, non-incremental dataflows are low-risk enough that skipping Dev-first testing has been fine, and you're explicitly keeping complex incremental-loading scenarios (the kind of thing that turned out to hide a DST calculation in "Data Availability") in the "must validate in Dev first" bucket. That's a reasonable, defensible split — the risk was never "building in Prod is bad," it's "building untested logic in Prod is bad," and simple dataflows don't have much logic to get wrong in the first place.

The one thing I'd flag isn't architectural — it's about making sure that classification stays deliberate. The failure mode isn't "the approach is wrong," it's "something *looked* simple, got built directly in Prod out of habit, and turned out to have more logic than expected" — which is exactly the shape of the Data Availability surprise. As long as "is this simple enough to skip Dev-first" stays a conscious judgment call each time, rather than a default nobody re-examines, this seems like a sound way to work.

---

**You**

I'm assuming as I think this thorugh that we'd be abandoning the automation code we'd built as too much complexity that still doesn't work completely.  The team doesn't like complexity.

---

**Claude**

That's a reasonable place to land, and I don't think it's the wrong call — this session genuinely did surface more moving parts than "fix hardcoded values" usually implies (naming conventions, connection references, credential rebinding, `.pqt` quirks, a service principal to maintain), and if the team's actual preference is for less machinery even at the cost of some manual diligence, that's a legitimate value judgment, not a mistake.

Worth being clear-eyed about the actual tradeoff before that conversation, though, since it's easy to only remember "this was complicated" and forget what it was buying you: the automation is what caught the stale-ID problem (five items silently pointing at nonexistent Dataflows), and it's what would keep catching that class of problem going forward without anyone needing to remember to check. Going manual doesn't make that risk disappear — it just moves the job of noticing it back onto a person remembering to look, the same way nobody noticed it *before* we built this. That's a fair price to pay for simplicity if the team weighs it that way, but worth naming explicitly rather than assuming the risk goes away with the code.

If "all automation or none" ends up feeling like a false choice once the team actually discusses it, there's a middle ground worth keeping in your back pocket: just the *detection* half without the auto-fix or discovery machinery — a much smaller script that only checks "does every dataflowId/pipelineId/connection look like a Variable Library reference" and fails loudly if not, with no value-matching, no `ITEM_NAME_MAP`, no service principal, nothing to maintain beyond the check itself. That's a fraction of today's complexity and still catches the scariest failure mode (an unrecognized reference silently pointing at the wrong environment) — worth mentioning if the team's objection turns out to be "this has too many pieces" rather than "we don't want any tooling at all."

Either way, sounds like a good note to take into that conversation with others.

**You**

Given how tricky it is to parameterize dataflow destinations and how there are two types, does it make more sense to migrate existing dataflows over or create new ones?  If new ones, should I try and capture the code from the existing ones first in order to pull over the

---

**Claude**

Given that we already have `standardize_dataflows.py` specifically built to convert *both* destination patterns you've now seen (the old per-query `DataDestinations`/`X_DataDestination` pattern, and the standard `DefaultDestination`/`BindToDefaultDestination` pattern) — I'd migrate the existing dataflows rather than recreate them from scratch. Here's the reasoning:

Migrating means bringing the actual, already-working transformation logic over as-is (via the `.pqt` export/import, or a direct git commit if it's already somewhere git-connected), and then letting `standardize_dataflows.py` do the destination-pattern conversion automatically — it already knows how to delete the old `_DataDestination` companion query, drop the `DataDestinations` attribute, and replace it with `BindToDefaultDestination = true` plus the shared `DefaultDestination` query. That's precisely the conversion "Data Availability" needs, and it's a solved problem, not a new one. The only manual step left is swapping the resulting hardcoded destination values for Variable Library references — which `enforce_parameterization.py` now also catches and fixes automatically if the values match something known.

Creating new ones from `_Template` avoids the destination-pattern conversion entirely (since `_Template` already has the correct pattern), but it means rebuilding the actual transformation logic by hand for every dataflow — real, tedious work, and real risk of introducing a subtle difference from the original while retyping or restructuring it. Given you mentioned there will be "a lot" of these to bring over, that manual-rebuild cost multiplies fast, whereas the migrate-and-standardize path scales for free — it's the same script run regardless of how many dataflows you're processing.

To directly answer your trailing question, though: if you *do* end up creating new ones for some dataflow (say, one where the existing setup is unusually messy or you want a clean rebuild for other reasons), yes — capture the actual transformation logic first, specifically the `let ... in ...` blocks for each real data query, and copy that M code directly into the new dataflow (built from `_Template`) rather than re-deriving the logic from memory or documentation. That preserves exact behavior and only replaces the part that's actually supposed to be different (the destination scaffolding, which `_Template` already provides correctly). But for the common case, migrate-and-standardize is the less risky, less manual path.

**You**

source information?

---

**Claude**

Yes — the source side is simpler than the destination side, and doesn't need the same conversion treatment.

The actual source query (`DB2.Database("192.168.0.10:448", "S21D504V", [Query = "...", ...])`) is just plain M text sitting in `mashup.pq`, and every one of your existing dataflows already uses the exact same consistent pattern for it — unlike destinations, there's no "two variants" problem here. So migrating a dataflow carries the source query over perfectly as-is; `standardize_dataflows.py` doesn't need to do anything to it, since there's nothing to standardize.

What *doesn't* travel with the migration, though — same as we found for the destination's Lakehouse connection — is the actual DB2 credential binding. That's stored separately by Fabric, keyed to the (server, database, workspace) combination, not embedded in the M text at all. So after migrating, the connection string/query itself is already correct, but whoever opens the migrated dataflow in its new workspace will still hit the same "authenticate this data source" prompt we discussed earlier — that's unavoidable regardless of migration method, and isn't something `standardize_dataflows.py` or the Variable Library can address.

**You**

Using the DataAvailability.Dataflow, where can I see the parameters used?

---

**Claude**

Within the Dataflow's own Power Query editor in Fabric — open "DataAvailability" and look at the **Queries pane** on the left side. Since `WS_Platform` and `LH_Lakehouse` are regular (non-hidden) shared queries, they'll show up right there in the query list alongside `DataAvailability` and `DataAvailability_DataDestination`.

Click on either one, and the main preview pane shows its **currently resolved value** — the actual GUID `Variable.ValueOrDefault(...)` evaluates to right now, based on whichever value-set is active in that workspace. That's the direct way to confirm "what is this dataflow actually using" for a given environment, as opposed to just reading the fallback default sitting in the M code.

Two other places worth knowing about, depending on what you're actually trying to verify:

- **Diagram view** (also in the Dataflow editor) shows the dependency graph visually — you'd see `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse` feeding into `DataAvailability_DataDestination`, which is a quick way to confirm the wiring is correct, not just the resolved values.
- **The Variable Library item itself** (opening "Variables" directly in the workspace) shows every variable and its value under the currently active value-set — useful for seeing the full picture across all variables at once, rather than just the two this particular dataflow references.

**You**

Please look in the AprilAire BI ETL repo.  I'm moving things over there to be dedicated to ETL and lakehouses.  Where can I see the parameter in code?

---

**Claude**

Good context — this repo already mirrors most of the ETL work, but this specific file (`DataAvailability.Dataflow/mashup.pq`) is the version *without* the destination wiring — matching the original `DataAvailability.pqt` (no destination at all), not the `DataAvailability_Dest.pqt` I edited to add `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`. So there's no parameter to see in this particular file yet — it hasn't been re-imported/re-committed with that destination fix.

Let me show you where the parameters *do* show up, using one of the other Dataflows already in this repo as the reference:

---

**Claude**

In this repo, using `RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental.Dataflow/mashup.pq` as the example, parameters show up in two places within the code:

1. **Where they're *defined*** — three lines near the bottom of the file:
```
shared WS_Platform = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/WS_Platform)", "fbe48c0a-f9df-4473-b296-e42ceb06f693");
shared LH_Lakehouse = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/LH_Lakehouse)", "ae57bec6-d925-4a93-94d5-0f6d8e2b7115");
shared SchemaName = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/SchemaName)", "dbo");
```
Each one names the Variable Library variable it pulls from (`$(/**/Variables/WS_Platform)`, etc.), with the current default as a fallback if the lookup fails.

2. **Where they're *used*** — the `DefaultDestination` line near the top of the file, which references those three by name rather than containing any literal value:
```
shared DefaultDestination = Lakehouse.Contents(...){[workspaceId = WS_Platform]}[Data]{[lakehouseId = LH_Lakehouse]}[Data]{[Id = SchemaName, ItemKind = "Schema"]}[Data];
```

`DataAvailability.Dataflow/mashup.pq` in this same repo doesn't have either of these yet — it's still the version without a destination at all. Once you bring in the fixed version (the one from `DataAvailability_Dest.pqt` with `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse` added), its `mashup.pq` would show the same two-part pattern: the two new `shared` definitions near the bottom, and its `DataAvailability_DataDestination` query referencing them by name instead of the hardcoded GUIDs it has now.
