**You**

Is there a formatting plugin or a linter for Mashup code?  Feel free to do a web search if necessary.

---

**Claude**

Yes, there are real options here, both directly relevant to editing the `mashup.pq` files in this repo:

**For formatting: Microsoft's own official extension — [Power Query / M Language](https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=PowerQuery.vscode-powerquery)** (`vscode-powerquery`). It's built and maintained by Microsoft (part of their `powerquery-language-services` project), and gives you syntax highlighting, IntelliSense (autocomplete, hover info, function/parameter hints), and — the part you're asking about — a built-in formatter wired up to VS Code's standard "Format Document" command (Alt+Shift+F). Since it's official and actively maintained, I'd install this one regardless of whether you also want linting, just for the editing experience alone when working with these `.pq` files directly.

**For linting specifically: a third-party extension — [Power Query Lint](https://github.com/clientfirsttech/power-query-lint-vs-code-extension)**. This one's separate from Microsoft's official tooling and focuses on actual lint-style checks rather than just formatting — worth knowing it also advertises some Copilot-agent integration for linting and semantic model testing, though I'd treat that part as a bonus rather than the reason to install it.

There's also a **[free online formatter](https://pqformat.maxanatsko.com/)** if you ever want to paste a snippet in and get it formatted without touching VS Code at all — useful for a one-off check, not something to build a workflow around.

I'd start with Microsoft's official extension first, since it covers both editing and formatting well and comes from the source most likely to stay aligned with the actual M language as it evolves — then add the lint-specific one only if you find yourself wanting rule-based checks beyond what formatting alone gives you.

---

**You**

M code is used in Semantic Models too, right?  I don't see any .pq files in there.

---

**Claude**

Confirmed — I can see it clearly in this repo. Semantic Models don't use standalone `.pq` files at all; they use the **TMDL** (Tabular Model Definition Language) format instead, where M code gets embedded directly inside `.tmdl` text files. Let me show you exactly where:

---

**Claude**

Confirmed — M code is absolutely used in Semantic Models, it's just serialized differently. You won't find `.pq` files because Semantic Models use the **TMDL** format instead: the M code lives directly inside a table's own `.tmdl` file, in a `partition ... = m` block with a `source = ...` expression. Here's the actual one from "Data Availability" in this model:

```
partition 'Data Availability' = m
    mode: import
    source =
            let
                Source = Sql.Database("uu25jtp6ymeebf6cigwecws2dy-...datawarehouse.fabric.microsoft.com", Environment & "_Warehouse"),
                Gold_dim_Product = Source{[Schema=if Developer <> "None" then Developer & "_Gold" else "Gold",Item="Data_Availability"]}[Data]
            in
                Gold_dim_Product
```

That `= m` right after the partition name is what marks this partition's source as M code — same language as `mashup.pq`, just embedded inline in TMDL's text format instead of living in its own file.

Worth flagging something directly relevant that this happened to surface: **this Semantic Model already has its own, different parameterization scheme** — see `Environment` and `Developer` in that snippet. Those aren't Variable Library references; they're classic M **Parameter** queries (matching the `.Parameter - *.tmdl` files I saw in that same folder), and they're exactly what the old, currently-unused `azure-pipelines.yml`/`Deploy_v2.yml` files were setting via the `PowerBIActions@5` "UpdateParameters" task (`{"name": "Developer", "newValue": "None"}`, `{"name": "Environment", "newValue": "$(environmentParameter)"}`). So this repo already has a working, pre-existing pattern for Semantic Model environment-switching — a genuinely different mechanism than the Variable Library approach we built this session for Dataflows/Pipelines, and one more data point for your "would Semantic Model deployment be easier" question from earlier: at least the parameterization *scheme* here already exists and has presumably been used before, rather than needing to be invented from scratch the way it was for Dataflows.

---

**You**

I know that. It's been my understanding that Semantic Model parameterization is applied at build time, so can't be managed using variable libraries.  Can you confirm?

---

**Claude**

Confirmed — yes, and there's concrete evidence for it from earlier in this conversation, not just architectural reasoning.

When we researched Variable Library's supported items, the list was explicit: **Pipeline, Shortcut for a Lakehouse, Notebook, Dataflow Gen2, Copy job, User data functions, and Plan (preview)**. Semantic Model isn't on that list. That's not an oversight I'm inferring — it's the actual documented scope, and it means there's no `Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/X)", ...)`-style runtime lookup available to a Semantic Model's M Parameter queries at all. The mechanism simply isn't wired into that item type.

That lines up exactly with what "Environment"/"Developer" are doing in that `Data Availability.tmdl` snippet: those are classic M **Parameter** queries, and the way you change them is by explicitly *setting* their value as a discrete action — via `PowerBIActions@5`'s "UpdateParameters" step (or the equivalent Tabular Editor command, or the underlying XMLA/REST "Update Parameters" call) — as part of a deploy sequence: deploy the model, then push new parameter values into it, then refresh. That's a fundamentally different shape than Variable Library's approach: Variable Library resolves a reference *live, at run time*, based on whichever value-set is currently active in that workspace; Semantic Model parameters get *pushed into* the already-deployed model as an explicit write, and then just sit there as a fixed value until the next deploy sequence overwrites them again. So your understanding is right — it's a build/deploy-time mechanism, not a runtime one, and Variable Library genuinely can't manage it, not because of some workaround we haven't found, but because Semantic Models aren't a supported consumer of Variable Library at all.

---

**You**

Is there any risk in reformatting .pq files to adding carriage returns in the code?  What about replacing "#(lf)" values with carriage returns?

---

**Claude**

These are two very different questions with different risk levels — worth separating clearly.

**General reformatting (adding line breaks/indentation to the M code structure itself):** Low risk from Fabric's own perspective — M, like most languages, treats whitespace between tokens as insignificant, so reformatting the `let ... in ...` structure for readability shouldn't change what the code actually does. But there's a risk specific to *this repo's tooling*, not to M itself: `enforce_parameterization.py` and `standardize_dataflows.py` both use **regex-based, line-anchored pattern matching** (e.g. `^shared\s+VAR\s*=\s*Variable\.ValueOrDefault\(` — matching from the start of a line), not a real M parser. If a formatter reflows a `shared WS_Platform = Variable.ValueOrDefault(...)` declaration across multiple lines or adds leading indentation, our scripts might stop recognizing it as "already correctly parameterized," and start (incorrectly) flagging or mangling it. So: safe for Fabric, not automatically safe for us — run both scripts in `--dry-run` immediately after any reformatting pass, before committing, to confirm they still see everything as already-fine.

**Replacing `#(lf)` with literal embedded newlines: I'd actually advise against this,** for a few concrete reasons, not just caution:

1. **Uncertain this is even valid M syntax.** `#(lf)` exists specifically as an *escape sequence* for embedding a line-feed character inside a single-line string literal — that's usually a sign the language expects string literals to stay on one physical line, and that a raw, unescaped newline breaking a `"..."` string across multiple lines in the source file may not be valid at all. If it isn't, you get an outright parse error, breaking the Dataflow — not a cosmetic issue.
2. **Even if it parses, it may not be byte-identical.** `#(lf)` unambiguously means LF (`\n`). A literal line break you type into the file depends on the file's own line-ending convention — if the file is CRLF (common on Windows), you'd end up embedding `\r\n` into the SQL query text instead of a plain `\n`. That's a real behavioral difference in what gets sent to DB2, not just a formatting change, and I don't know DB2's SQL parser well enough to promise that's harmless.
3. **This would likely get silently undone anyway.** These `mashup.pq` files get regenerated by Fabric's own serializer every time someone edits the Dataflow in the UI and commits from Source Control — and Fabric's serializer is clearly the one choosing `#(lf)` in the first place. A manual "unescape to real newlines" edit could just get reverted the next time anyone syncs from the Fabric side, making it a non-durable change you'd be redoing indefinitely.

If more readable SQL text in these files matters to you, I'd test this on one query, in a low-stakes Dataflow, and specifically check two things before trusting it: does it still run correctly in Fabric, and does it survive the next edit-and-commit cycle from the Fabric UI without reverting. Given the real chance of a parse error or a silent revert, I wouldn't apply this broadly without that verification first.

---

**You**

Do .pq files allow for comments?

---

**Claude**

Yes — M supports both comment styles, same as C-style languages:

- **Single-line**: `// comment text` — everything after `//` to the end of the line is ignored.
- **Block/multi-line**: `/* comment text */` — can span multiple lines.

You've actually already got a concrete example sitting in your own repo: the "Data Availability" DST calculation logic uses `//` comments throughout — things like `// Current UTC time`, `// Base Central time (CST) for DST evaluation`, and the section dividers `// ----- DST START: Second Sunday in March -----`. So comments are already in active use in this codebase, not just theoretically supported.

One practical note tying back to the reformatting discussion: comments are a safe way to add readability without touching string literal contents or risking the `#(lf)` parsing concerns from before — if you want more self-documenting M code, adding `//` comments around tricky logic is a much lower-risk way to do it than reformatting the actual query structure or string escaping.

---

**You**

Is there a way to do a bulk replace of #(lf) with a carriage return?

---

**Claude**

Mechanically, yes — it's a trivial find-and-replace (`#(lf)` → an actual newline), doable with a short script, `sed`, or VS Code's find-and-replace with regex mode on. That part's easy.

Given the risks I flagged last message, though, I'd do this as a **controlled test on one file first**, not a bulk operation across everything — a bulk replace means if there's a parse error or a CR/LF issue, it hits every dataflow's SQL queries simultaneously instead of one. The asymmetry here is real: the replace itself takes seconds either way, but testing one file first costs you almost nothing extra, while testing after a bulk change means untangling which of several files broke and why.

Want me to write a small script that applies this to just one query in one Dataflow's `mashup.pq` (in this repo, not live in Fabric), so you can sync just that one change to Dev and verify it actually parses, runs, and produces correct results — and specifically check whether it survives being re-committed from the Fabric UI afterward, per the reversion concern? Once that's confirmed clean, turning it into a bulk replace across all the files is a one-line change to the script.

---

**You**

No, I want do it using VS code's find and replace functionality if possible.

---

**Claude**

That's doable directly in VS Code's Search & Replace panel, using regex mode. Here's how, scoped to one file first per the earlier caution:

1. Open the **Search panel** (`Ctrl+Shift+H` opens it directly in "Replace in Files" mode, or click the search icon in the sidebar and expand the replace field).
2. Click the **"Use Regular Expression"** toggle (the `.*` icon) in the search box — this is required for the replacement to work correctly.
3. In the **"files to include"** field, scope this to just one file first — e.g., paste the exact relative path like `ETL/Dataflows/RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental.Dataflow/mashup.pq` — so the test only touches that one file, not everything at once.
4. In the **search field**, enter: `#\(lf\)` — note the backslashes before the parentheses. This matters: parentheses are regex grouping syntax, so without escaping them, VS Code would treat `(lf)` as a capture group instead of literal characters to match, and it wouldn't find `#(lf)` correctly.
5. In the **replace field**, enter: `\n` — in regex-replace mode, VS Code interprets this as an actual newline character, not literal backslash-n text.
6. VS Code shows a **preview** of every match before applying anything — review that carefully, since this is exactly the kind of change worth eyeballing before committing to it.
7. Apply, then test that one file in Fabric (does it still parse, does the query still run correctly) before widening the "files to include" scope to the rest.

Once that one file checks out, widening to all of them is just broadening step 3 to a glob like `ETL/Dataflows/**/mashup.pq` — but I'd still hold off on that until you've confirmed the single-file test survives both a Fabric parse/run check and a re-commit-from-the-UI cycle, per the concerns from a couple messages back.

---

**You**

Can it be done in the editor for a single file?

---

**Claude**

Yes — and for a single-file test, the in-editor find/replace is actually the more foolproof choice, since there's no "files to include" filter that could accidentally be too broad.

1. Open the specific `mashup.pq` file you want to test in the editor.
2. Press `Ctrl+H` (opens find-and-replace scoped to just that open file, as opposed to `Ctrl+Shift+H` which searches across the whole workspace).
3. Click the **"Use Regular Expression"** toggle (the `.*` icon) on the left side of the find/replace widget.
4. In the **find field**: `#\(lf\)` — same escaping as before, since the parentheses need to be escaped to match literally rather than being treated as a regex group.
5. In the **replace field**: `\n` — with regex mode on, this inserts an actual newline.
6. You can step through matches one at a time (the arrow buttons) and replace individually, or use "Replace All" (the button that looks like two overlapping squares) to do the whole file at once — worth doing "Replace All" here since you're already scoped to one file, and the workspace-level caution was about accidentally hitting *other* files, not about this one.

Same next step as before: save, sync just this file to Dev, and confirm it still parses and runs correctly in Fabric before touching any other files.

---

**You**

Does the destination need to remain at the top of the .pq file and the destination variables at the bottom, or can they be put next to each-other?

---

**Claude**

No — there's no requirement for that specific layout, and there's already concrete proof of it sitting in your own files: `DefaultDestination` is defined at the *top* of every `mashup.pq`, yet it references `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`/`SchemaName`, which are defined at the *bottom* — a forward reference that already works today. M resolves top-level `shared` declarations within a section by name, not by textual order (it's a lazily-evaluated, declarative language, not line-by-line imperative code), so it doesn't matter whether a query's dependencies appear before or after it in the file.

Your own tooling doesn't care about position either, which I double-checked given the reformatting concerns from earlier:

- `standardize_dataflows.py` only inserts `DefaultDestination` at the top as a fallback *if it's missing entirely* — if it's already present anywhere in the file, the script just preserves whatever order things are already in; it doesn't enforce or expect a specific position.
- `enforce_parameterization.py` scans line-by-line for the specific `shared VAR = ...` pattern, wherever it happens to sit in the file — it has no concept of "top" or "bottom" at all.

So moving the `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`/`SchemaName` declarations to sit right next to `DefaultDestination` (or anywhere else) is safe from both Fabric's parsing side and our scripts' side — the current top/bottom split is just an artifact of how Fabric's own authoring tool happened to generate these files, not a real constraint. Same advice as before applies, though: test the reorganized layout on one file first (dry-run both scripts against it, and confirm it still loads/runs in Fabric) before applying it broadly, just to catch anything I haven't accounted for.

---

**You**

Does the position of "DefaultOutputDestinationSettings" or "section" matter?  

Do we have dataflows with more than one section?  Why would we want sections?

---

**Claude**

Confirmed — every `mashup.pq` in this repo has exactly one `section Section1;`, always on line 2, immediately after the line-1 attribute record. That's not a stylistic choice you can move around.

**Does the position of `DefaultOutputDestinationSettings`/`section` matter? Yes — hard requirement, not convention.**

This is a different case from the `DefaultDestination`/variable ordering question. In M's grammar, a **Section Document** has a fixed shape:

```
[ optional section-level attributes ]
section [Name];
member;
member;
...
```

The bracketed record on line 1 (`StagingDefinition`, `DefaultOutputDestinationSettings`) is a **section-level attribute** — it has to sit immediately before the `section` keyword. The `section Section1;` declaration itself has to come before any query/member declarations. You can't move it below queries, split it, or push the destination-settings record down next to `DefaultDestination` — that bracket down in the body is a *query-level* attribute (attached to the next `shared` query), a completely different grammatical slot than the section-level one on line 1, even though they look similar syntactically.

Your `standardize_dataflows.py` (`process_mashup`) also hard-codes this assumption: it does a linear split on the line matching `section\s+Section1\s*;`, treats everything before it as `pre_lines` (where it searches for `StagingDefinition`), and everything after as the query body. If that ordering were violated, the script would silently misparse the file. So both Fabric/M and your own script agree: line 1 (attributes) → line 2 (`section Section1;`) → query bodies, in that order, always.

**Do we have dataflows with more than one section?**

No — every tracked Dataflow (`_Template`, Product, Customer/Locations, Business Unit/Channel, Orders) has exactly one `section Section1;`.

**Why would you ever want multiple sections?**

In practice, you wouldn't, for a Fabric Dataflow Gen2. Each Dataflow Gen2 mashup document is compiled as a single Section Document — Fabric's own authoring UI never generates more than one `section` per dataflow, and there's no UI concept of "add a second section." Multiple sections are a more general M-language feature (mainly relevant to custom Power Query connectors / library projects, where you might split code across logical namespaces), not something dataflow authoring exposes. The "Query Groups" you see in the Power Query UI (folders for organizing queries visually) are a separate, UI-only metadata concept stored elsewhere — they don't correspond to additional `section` blocks in the `.pq` text at all. So for your use case, there's no reason to introduce a second section — one `Section1` per dataflow is both the current state and the only pattern Fabric actually supports for this item type.

---

**You**

Looking at these three variable definitions, why would the first two have guids but the third have a fixed value?  Is this like a fallback if the variable library's value is missing?  If so, wouldn't it be better to generate an error than fallback to something generic that could be wrong?

shared WS_Platform = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/WS_Platform)", "fbe48c0a-f9df-4473-b296-e42ceb06f693");
shared LH_Lakehouse = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/LH_Lakehouse)", "ae57bec6-d925-4a93-94d5-0f6d8e2b7115");
shared SchemaName = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/SchemaName)", "dbo");

---

**Claude**

It's not a deliberate design choice about which variables "need" a real value versus a generic one — it's an artifact of what each query's hardcoded value happened to be *before* it got parameterized.

`Variable.ValueOrDefault(path, default)` is auto-generated by Fabric's own UI the moment you bind an existing query to a Variable Library variable. Fabric doesn't ask you to type a default — it just snapshots whatever literal value was already sitting in that query at the moment of binding and bakes it in as the fallback. `enforce_parameterization.py` in this repo does the same mechanical thing: it matches `shared VAR = "some literal";` and rewrites it to `Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(.../VAR)", "some literal")`, carrying the pre-existing literal forward unchanged.

So the difference between the three is just history: `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse` were originally hardcoded to real workspace/lakehouse GUIDs (required, since `Lakehouse.Contents()` can't resolve without one), while `SchemaName` was originally hardcoded to the literal string `"dbo"`. Neither is a "safer" or "riskier" choice — both are just whatever was there before.

You're right that this is a fallback for when the Variable Library resolution fails — but it's worth being precise about *when* that actually happens. The `$(/**/Variables/X)` token is resolved by Fabric's own build/git-integration engine, generally before/at the point the item is deployed to a workspace, not at every runtime refresh. So the fallback rarely exists to catch "the Variable Library entry is missing in Prod" — it mostly exists so the item still works when: it's opened before ever being connected to a Variable Library, exported as a `.pqt` template (like you were just doing), or run somewhere Fabric's templating engine doesn't apply at all. Prioritizing "don't break authoring/portability" over "fail loudly" is Fabric's own convention here, not something this repo introduced.

Your underlying critique is legitimate, though: for `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse` specifically, if the binding ever *did* silently fail to resolve in a real environment, the fallback would point the dataflow at whichever workspace/lakehouse that baked-in GUID happens to belong to — possibly Dev — with no error, no warning, just quietly wrong data. That's a worse failure mode than `SchemaName` falling back to `"dbo"`, which is far more likely to either not exist or produce empty/obviously-wrong results downstream that get noticed. If you wanted fail-fast behavior instead, you could wrap the destination-critical variables in something like `if Variable.ValueOrDefault(...) = null then error "WS_Platform could not be resolved from Variable Library" else ...` — but that's a deviation from Fabric's auto-generated pattern, and since Fabric's UI will happily regenerate the plain `ValueOrDefault` form if you ever re-bind the parameter through the UI, you'd need to re-apply it (or encode it into `enforce_parameterization.py`) every time.

---

**You**

If I wanted to create and edit dataflows in code, would it make sense to remove the fallback value?

---

**Claude**

Largely yes — the main reason Fabric bakes in a real literal default (so the Power Query Online editor can still evaluate/preview the query before it's ever wired to a Variable Library) matters a lot less when you're authoring the `.pq` text yourself in code, since you're not relying on Fabric's drag-and-drop "bind to variable" flow to produce a working draft mid-edit. If you're generating these files programmatically and always writing the `$(...)` token from the start, you don't need Fabric's fallback-for-graceful-authoring behavior the way a human clicking through the UI does.

That said, I'd stop short of leaving the second argument empty or removing it outright, for two reasons:

1. **`Variable.ValueOrDefault` requires two arguments** — there's no simpler single-arg `Variable.Value(path)` form used anywhere in this repo, so "removing the fallback" really means changing what the second argument *does*, not deleting it.
2. **You still open these items in the Fabric web UI to sanity-check them** (your test-import ritual all session) — if the second argument is something that can't evaluate at all, a query you're just trying to eyeball before the Variable Library binding is fully synced could fail to even preview, which works against you during authoring/testing.

The better fit for a code-first workflow is what you proposed two messages ago — fail loudly instead of silently succeeding with a plausible-but-wrong value — but implemented as an explicit check rather than relying on a bare literal:

```
WS_Platform_Raw = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/WS_Platform)", null),
WS_Platform = if WS_Platform_Raw <> null then WS_Platform_Raw else error "WS_Platform not resolved from Variable Library",
```

This keeps `null` (an inert, unambiguous placeholder) as the literal fallback — so the query still evaluates to *something* if you preview it before binding — but any real refresh in Fabric (Dev, Prod, wherever) that fails to resolve the Variable Library token gets a hard, visible error instead of silently pointing at a baked-in GUID that might belong to the wrong environment. That directly fixes the risk you flagged earlier, without depending on `Variable.ValueOrDefault`'s internal argument-evaluation order — I don't have documented confirmation of whether it lazily evaluates the second argument only on fallback or evaluates both eagerly, and putting an `error "..."` expression directly as the second argument would depend on that being lazy. Splitting it into "resolve-or-null" then "null-check-or-error" as two separate steps sidesteps that uncertainty entirely.

If you want, this pattern could be templated in `enforce_parameterization.py`/`standardize_dataflows.py` the same way `DEFAULT_DESTINATION` is templated now, so every variable gets generated this way automatically instead of hand-written per file — but worth testing the two-line pattern on one real dataflow in Fabric first (both the "unbound preview" case and a real refresh) before baking it into the scripts.

---

**You**

Do I need the word "shared" in there somewhere?

---

**Claude**

Good catch — my snippet was structurally wrong. In this repo's convention, `shared` marks a top-level *section member* (one per variable, ending in `;`), not something you sprinkle on intermediate steps. A `let...in` block's internal steps are always local/unshared by nature — they can't take `shared` even if you wanted them to.

The two lines I wrote (comma-joined, no `let`/`in`) looked like two separate top-level declarations, which isn't valid — you'd either need `shared` on both (turning `_Raw` into its own visible query, cluttering the queries pane for no reason) or fold it into a single `shared` member using an actual `let...in` block, which is the right fix:

```
shared WS_Platform =
    let
        Raw = Variable.ValueOrDefault("$(/**/Variables/WS_Platform)", null)
    in
        if Raw <> null then Raw else error "WS_Platform not resolved from Variable Library";
```

This is still exactly **one** `shared NAME = ...;` statement — same as every other variable in the file — it just happens to be a multi-line expression instead of a single-line one. `shared` goes once, on `WS_Platform` itself.

One thing this changes for your tooling: `standardize_dataflows.py`'s `parse_blocks()` will handle this fine — it identifies a query block by its first line (`shared WS_Platform =`) and treats everything indented after it (plus the special-cased `in`) as continuation lines of that same block, regardless of how many lines it spans. But `enforce_parameterization.py`'s `fix_mashup()` currently checks for the "already parameterized, skip it" case with a single-line regex (`^shared\s+VAR\s*=\s*Variable\.ValueOrDefault\(`) — a multi-line `let` form wouldn't match that pattern, so the script wouldn't recognize it as already-fixed. It also wouldn't break anything (it just wouldn't match the "still a bare literal" pattern either, so it'd leave the line alone), but if you want the script to auto-generate this multi-line form going forward rather than hand-writing it per file, that detection logic would need updating to be block-aware instead of line-by-line.

---

**You**

Can you see linter errors in files?

If so, then what are the errors in "C:\Git\AprilAire BI ETL\ETL\Dataflows\RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental.Dataflow\mashup.pq" about?

---

**Claude**

No, I don't have a diagnostics/linting tool — I have no access to VS Code's Problems panel, no M language server, and no way to see whatever squiggly-line errors your editor is showing you. I can only read the raw text of a file and reason about it manually (syntax review by eye), which is not the same as running an actual linter or parser.

If you're seeing specific error markers in your editor for that file, the fastest path is to paste the error text/tooltip here, or tell me the line number and what the squiggle says — then I can look at that spot in the file and figure out what's wrong.

---

**You**

Here are two.

---

**Claude**

Both of these are false positives from your editor's linter, not real problems with the code — and I can confirm that by comparing against the commented-out original at line 72 of that same file, which uses the identical `Variable.ValueOrDefault` call and would trigger the same warning if uncommented.

**Error 1 — "Cannot find the name 'Variable.ValueOrDefault'... did you mean 'Variable.Value'?"**

This is the M/Power Query VS Code extension's static type-checker, and it's wrong here. `Variable.ValueOrDefault` is a Fabric-specific function tied to the Variable Library feature — it only exists inside Fabric's actual runtime execution environment, not in the base M standard library that a VS Code extension bundles for offline analysis. The extension has no idea this identifier exists at all, so it flags *every* reference to it as unknown, including the original single-line form you've been using successfully in every other dataflow all session (right there, commented out at line 72). This isn't something the new Raw/null/error pattern introduced — it's a pre-existing blind spot that would show up on the old code too. The "did you mean `Variable.Value`?" is just fuzzy string-matching against whatever it does recognize, not a real suggestion to act on. Fabric itself resolves `Variable.ValueOrDefault` fine at runtime; nothing to fix.

**Error 2 — "'Lakehouse': Unknown word. cSpell"**

That's the cSpell extension's dictionary check, not a code problem — it's just flagging that "Lakehouse" (inside `LH_Lakehouse`) isn't in its English word list. Purely cosmetic; you can ignore it or add "Lakehouse" to a cspell project dictionary if you want it to stop nagging.

The hover tooltip under it, `[section-member] LH_Lakehouse: none`, is the M language service correctly recognizing `LH_Lakehouse` as a valid declared query — the `none` is just its inferred *type*, which comes out unresolved for the same reason as Error 1: since it can't resolve what `Variable.ValueOrDefault` returns, it can't infer the type of `Raw` or the shared query. Same root cause, not an independent issue.

Net: the code is fine. Both warnings trace back to the editor's linter not knowing about the Fabric-provided `Variable` API — expected, and safe to ignore.

---

**You**

For dataflows that don't need testing I'm planning to create them in Prod.  If I then create new dataflows in Dev and merge them into Prod (git main), will dataflows created in Prod be affected?  If not, then is there any reason to parameterized prod-created dataflows?

---

**Claude**

**Will Dev→Prod merges affect Prod-created dataflows? No — with one important caveat.**

Git merges are additive/diff-based: merging your Dev branch into main only touches the files/folders that actually changed between the branches' common ancestor and Dev's tip. A dataflow created directly in Prod lives in its own folder (`ETL/Dataflows/SomeName.Dataflow/...`), which Dev's branch never touched, so there's no overlap and no conflict — the merge just adds Dev's new dataflow folders alongside it. Same logic applies on the Fabric side: when Prod's workspace does "Update from Git" after the merge, Fabric only creates/updates/deletes items that actually differ from what's already in the workspace — it won't touch an item that's already identical between git and the live workspace.

The caveat: **this only holds if the Prod-created dataflow actually gets committed to git main.** If you create it live in the Prod workspace and never sync/commit it back to git, it exists only in the workspace — invisible to source control, invisible to your `discover_item_ids.py` automation, and not something any of your tooling (`enforce_parameterization.py`, the ADO pipeline) knows about. Worse, if Prod's workspace is ever rebuilt or reset from git (disaster recovery, workspace migration), an uncommitted item is just gone — git has no record of it. So "created in Prod" is fine; "created in Prod and left uncommitted" is a real gap.

**Is there still a reason to parameterize Prod-only dataflows? Yes — but the reasoning shifts.**

The Dev/Prod-promotion argument (avoiding a hardcoded Dev GUID accidentally shipping to Prod) genuinely doesn't apply here, since these items never move between environments. But two other reasons still hold:

1. **GUID resilience independent of environment promotion.** A hardcoded workspace/lakehouse GUID only stays correct as long as that exact Lakehouse/workspace item is never recreated. If you ever rebuild the Lakehouse, migrate it, or the workspace ID changes for any reason unrelated to Dev/Prod, a hardcoded dataflow silently breaks (points at a GUID that no longer exists), while a parameterized one just needs the Prod `valueSet` updated once and every parameterized item picks it up automatically.
2. **Consistency with your own tooling.** `standardize_dataflows.py` and `enforce_parameterization.py` assume every dataflow uses the `DefaultDestination` + `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`/`SchemaName` pattern. A hardcoded exception becomes something those scripts either silently ignore or need special-casing for — and it's a pattern a future teammate (or future you) will have to notice and remember is "different on purpose," rather than the repo being uniformly self-explanatory.

Given the template text is now essentially copy-paste (the same `DefaultDestination` block + three variable declarations), there's not much effort saved by hardcoding instead — so I'd lean toward parameterizing Prod-only dataflows too, purely for consistency and GUID resilience, even though the original "protect against cross-environment promotion mistakes" motivation doesn't apply to them.

---

**You**

What do these items mean?
[DestinationDefinition = [Kind = "Reference", QueryName = "DefaultDestination", IsNewTarget = true], UpdateMethod = [Kind = "Replace"]]

---

**Claude**

This is the value assigned to the `DefaultOutputDestinationSettings` section-level attribute (the one on line 1 of every `mashup.pq`, before `section Section1;`). It defines the fallback destination policy that any query marked `[BindToDefaultDestination = true]` inherits, so individual queries don't have to repeat this whole record — that's the payoff of the pattern `standardize_dataflows.py` builds.

Breaking down the two fields:

**`DestinationDefinition`** — describes *where* the output goes:
- `Kind = "Reference"` — the destination isn't defined inline here; it's defined by pointing at another query in the same document.
- `QueryName = "DefaultDestination"` — that other query is the one named `DefaultDestination`, the `shared DefaultDestination = Lakehouse.Contents(...)...` expression you've seen resolving `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`/`SchemaName` into an actual Lakehouse schema target.
- `IsNewTarget = true` — this binding was set up as creating a *new* destination table, as opposed to attaching to a table that already existed in the Lakehouse independently of this dataflow.

**`UpdateMethod`** — describes *how* the destination table gets written on each refresh:
- `Kind = "Replace"` — full overwrite: each refresh truncates and reloads the destination table with the query's current output. The alternatives Fabric's UI exposes here are things like `Append` (add rows without touching existing ones) or a merge/upsert-based method — this dataflow uses none of those, it fully replaces the table every time.

One thing worth flagging since it's a bit counterintuitive: several of your dataflows are named `..._Incremental` (e.g., `RPC ERP - Orders_Incremental.Dataflow`), but their `UpdateMethod` here is `"Replace"`, not an incremental append/merge. That's not necessarily wrong — "Incremental" likely refers to the *source* query only pulling recent/delta rows from DB2, while the *destination* write behavior still fully replaces whatever rows got pulled that refresh — but it's worth double-checking that's the intended split (source-side incremental extraction, destination-side full replace) rather than an artifact of the standardization script always emitting `"Replace"` regardless of what each dataflow originally had configured.

---

**You**

How does "IsNewTarget = true" handle situations where the table does already exist (either having been created by this dataflow, manually, or by another dataflow) or where we delete it to test again?

---

**Claude**

I can walk through the mechanics I'm confident about, but I want to flag upfront that `IsNewTarget`'s exact runtime enforcement isn't something Microsoft documents in detail publicly — this flag mostly surfaces through exported files like the ones you've been inspecting, not through official docs. So treat the middle case below as "reasoned inference, worth testing" rather than a confirmed guarantee.

**What `IsNewTarget` actually is:** it's a design-time flag that reflects what you chose in the destination-picker dialog when you first bound the query — "Create new table" vs. "Use an existing table." It's not something Fabric re-checks and flips automatically on every refresh; once baked into `queryMetadata.json`/`mashup.pq`, it stays `true` until someone manually re-binds the destination.

Working through your scenarios:

**Table exists because *this* dataflow created it (steady-state, every refresh after the first)** — this is the normal case and it's fine. `IsNewTarget = true` doesn't mean "the table must not already exist" — it means "this dataflow owns/manages this table's lifecycle." Combined with `UpdateMethod.Kind = "Replace"`, each refresh just truncates/overwrites the table the dataflow already created. No conflict.

**Table gets deleted and you refresh again (your test-iteration case)** — this should also be safe and is probably the best-behaved scenario. Deleting the table just puts you back in the "destination doesn't exist yet" state, which is exactly what `IsNewTarget = true` + `Replace` is designed to handle: the next refresh recreates it fresh with whatever schema the current query output produces. This is functionally identical to the very first refresh ever run, so I'd expect it to just work — but it's cheap to confirm once in Dev if you want certainty before relying on it as a routine test-reset trick.

**Table already exists because it was created manually, or by a *different* dataflow, and this dataflow's binding still says `IsNewTarget = true`** — this is the genuinely risky case, and where I don't have confident, documented behavior to point to. `Replace` mode's whole purpose is unconditional overwrite; I'm not aware of any ownership check that would make Fabric pause and ask "this table wasn't created by you, overwrite anyway?" My working assumption is that if the table name collides, the refresh just overwrites it — silently replacing whatever was there (schema and data) with this query's output, regardless of who actually created it or what depended on it. If the existing table's schema differs meaningfully from what this query now produces, that could also trigger a schema-mismatch failure rather than a clean overwrite, depending on whether automatic schema evolution is enabled — but either way, the outcome (silent overwrite or an obscure refresh error) is bad if it happens by accident.

Practical takeaway: table-name collisions between dataflow-managed destinations and anything else — manual tables, another dataflow's output — are the actual danger here, not the `IsNewTarget` flag itself. Since I can't confirm from documentation exactly how Fabric handles that collision, if you want certainty rather than inference, the cheap way to find out is to manually create a table with a name matching one of your dataflow destinations in Dev, run the refresh, and see whether it errors or clobbers it. Otherwise, the safer practice is just to make sure destination table names stay unique per-dataflow so this scenario never comes up for real.

---

**You**

What would happen if IsNewTarget = false and UpdateMethod.Kind = "Replace"?

---

**Claude**

`IsNewTarget = false` is the other branch of that same binary choice — it's what Fabric records when you pick "Use an existing table" in the destination picker instead of "Create new table." Same caveat as before: I'm reasoning from how that UI choice and the underlying flag names are generally understood, not from documented, guaranteed behavior — worth confirming empirically if this matters for your workflow.

The key conceptual difference is **who owns the schema**:

- `IsNewTarget = true` → the dataflow is authoritative over the table's structure. It takes the query's output schema as ground truth and will create the table (with that schema) if it doesn't exist.
- `IsNewTarget = false` → the table's schema is treated as already fixed/pre-existing, independent of this dataflow. Fabric's "use existing table" flow generally goes through a column-mapping step (aligning your query's output columns to the destination table's existing columns) rather than letting the query dictate structure.

So `IsNewTarget = false` + `UpdateMethod.Kind = "Replace"` most likely means: "this is a pre-existing table I'm intentionally targeting — don't try to (re)define its schema, just fully overwrite its contents with my query's output on each refresh, mapped onto the existing columns." That's actually probably the *correct*, intended way to express the "table already exists, created elsewhere" scenario I flagged as risky in my last answer — rather than that collision being an accident under `IsNewTarget = true`, it's likely what this combination is designed for.

Two practical implications, both fairly directly relevant to how you work:

1. **It probably doesn't self-heal if you delete the table.** With `IsNewTarget = true`, deleting the table for a test-reset just puts you back in "doesn't exist yet, create it" territory, which that mode handles gracefully. With `IsNewTarget = false`, the dataflow isn't expecting to have to create anything — deleting the table out from under it would more likely produce a "table not found" refresh failure rather than a clean recreation. If you rely on delete-and-rerun as a dev-testing reset trick, that trick probably only works reliably on bindings with `IsNewTarget = true`.
2. **It likely validates against the existing schema rather than overwriting it.** If your query's output columns don't match the existing table's columns, you'd more likely get a mapping/schema error than a silent schema replacement — which is actually the safer behavior if the intent is genuinely "write into this specific pre-existing table."

Given the uncertainty I flagged, if this distinction matters for a real decision (e.g., choosing which mode to use for a shared/reused table), it's worth a quick empirical check in Dev: try refreshing an `IsNewTarget = false` binding after deleting its target table, and see whether it fails or recreates.

---

**You**

How do I drop or add a column from a table?  I'm seeing this in the ui: 

	The specified ALTER TABLE statement is not supported in this edition of SQL Server.

---

**Claude**

That error text is generic SQL Server wording that Fabric reuses to say "this DDL operation isn't supported here" — and given the file you have open writes to a Lakehouse via `Lakehouse.Contents()`, this is almost certainly the Lakehouse's **SQL Analytics Endpoint**, which is read-only at the DDL level. You cannot run `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN` / `DROP COLUMN` against a Lakehouse table through its SQL endpoint — it's an auto-generated, read-only T-SQL mirror over the underlying Delta table in OneLake, not a real writable database. Any DDL attempted there gets rejected with exactly that kind of "not supported" error, regardless of syntax.

The actual ways to add/drop a column on a Lakehouse table:

1. **Through the dataflow itself (the normal path for anything this repo owns).** Since these destinations use `IsNewTarget = true`, the query's output schema is authoritative — add or remove the column as a Power Query transformation step (e.g., `Table.AddColumn` / `Table.RemoveColumns`, or just do it via the UI's column context menu), then refresh. As long as schema evolution is enabled for that destination (which it generally is for `Replace` + `IsNewTarget = true`), the table's structure updates to match the query's output on the next refresh — no manual DDL needed at all.
2. **Via a Notebook (Spark).** If you need to alter the table directly without going through a dataflow refresh, a Lakehouse Notebook running Spark SQL (`ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMNS ...`) or PySpark with schema merge/overwrite options has full DDL capability against the Delta table — this is the "real" way to hand-edit a Lakehouse table's schema outside of dataflow refreshes.
3. **The Lakehouse Explorer UI itself doesn't support column-level schema editing** on an existing table — it's oriented around creating tables from files, not altering existing ones.

One thing worth confirming: if you actually hit this error while querying a Fabric **Warehouse** (not the Lakehouse SQL endpoint) — Warehouses do support full T-SQL DDL, so if that's where you saw it, the table in question is more likely a cross-item reference back to Lakehouse data (which inherits the same read-only restriction) rather than a native Warehouse table. Which surface were you in when you got the error — the Lakehouse's SQL analytics endpoint, or the Warehouse query editor?

---

**You**

My reason for wanting to edit a table is to determine what the dataflows do with IsNewTarget = true if they encounter a mismatched schema.  This is a testing situation.  

You mentioned earlier that there is a pattern for using notebooks in Git to manage Lakehouse table definitions.  Why would someone want a solution like this if they're using dataflows?  Is it primarily for testing, or for other reasons?

---

**Claude**

Quick honest note before I answer: I don't have a clear record of introducing "notebooks in git to manage Lakehouse table definitions" as its own pattern earlier in this conversation — the closest thing I said was that a Notebook is *one way* to run DDL directly against a Delta table, in the context of your ALTER TABLE error. If that fuller pattern came up earlier in the session (before this got summarized), I don't have it in view, so let me know if I'm missing context. Either way, it's a real and answerable question on its own terms, so here's the substance of it.

Testing is one legitimate use — exactly what you're doing right now, since the Lakehouse SQL endpoint won't let you hand-edit a table's schema, and a notebook is the only supported way to manipulate the Delta table directly, out-of-band from the dataflow, to set up a deliberate mismatch. But it's not the main reason this pattern exists. The bigger reasons:

1. **Schema-as-code instead of schema-by-accident.** A dataflow with `IsNewTarget = true` infers the destination schema from whatever the Power Query output currently produces — it's implicit. If someone tweaks a transformation step and adds/drops/renames a column, the table schema quietly drifts to match on the next refresh, with no PR diff, no review gate, nothing declaring "this is what the table is supposed to look like." A notebook with an explicit `CREATE TABLE ... (col1 STRING, col2 INT, ...) USING DELTA` statement checked into git gives you a reviewable, versioned contract for the table's structure — much closer to how a traditional database migration works.
2. **Control over things dataflows don't expose.** Delta/Lakehouse tables support partitioning columns, table properties (retention, auto-optimize, etc.), and specific type precision that Power Query's inferred-schema model doesn't give you fine control over. If a fact table needs to be partitioned by date for query performance, you'd typically define that via notebook DDL before any dataflow ever writes to it.
3. **Pre-provisioning for `IsNewTarget = false` bindings, or for standing up a brand-new environment.** This ties directly to the gap we just discussed — an `IsNewTarget = false` binding likely doesn't self-heal if its target table is missing (it expects the table to already exist). A notebook full of `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` statements, run once against a fresh Lakehouse, is the mitigation: it deterministically provisions the whole schema surface up front, so a new/rebuilt environment (or a workspace recovering from disaster) doesn't depend on however many dataflow refreshes it'd take to lazily create everything, and doesn't leave `IsNewTarget = false` bindings pointing at nothing.
4. **Tables that don't fit the single-dataflow-owns-a-table model** — reference/dimension tables seeded once and rarely refreshed, or tables assembled from more than one source that a single Dataflow query can't cleanly represent.

So: real, ongoing reasons to have it even in a dataflow-based pipeline — testing is just the use case you happen to need it for right now.

---

**You**

Given that we're not using any of those features, is there any advantage to our using Lakehouse to land tables over Warehouse?

---

**Claude**

Given that none of the schema-as-code/partitioning/pre-provisioning reasons apply to you, the strongest reasons to prefer Lakehouse-as-landing evaporate — and there's actually a concrete point in the other direction, visible in your own pipeline.

**The one already-evidenced advantage of Warehouse-as-landing: it eliminates the SQL Endpoint refresh-lag problem.** Your `RPC MASTER HOURLY_INCREMENTAL` pipeline has a whole dedicated `RefreshSqlEndpoint` activity (plus its own failure-handling branch) that exists *only* because a Lakehouse's SQL Analytics Endpoint doesn't immediately reflect newly-written Delta data — it's a separate, asynchronously-refreshed metadata layer sitting on top of the actual table files, and Fabric needs an explicit nudge to catch it up before downstream consumers (your Warehouse, the Semantic Model) query it. A Fabric Warehouse doesn't have this problem at all — writes go through a real transactional T-SQL engine with immediate consistency, no separate "endpoint" to refresh. If dataflows landed directly in a Warehouse, that entire activity — and its failure-handling — becomes unnecessary. That's a genuine simplification, not a hypothetical one.

**Other Lakehouse advantages that might still matter, but aren't things you're using today either:**
- **Native Spark/notebook access** — Lakehouse tables are directly readable/writable from Spark notebooks without going through a SQL layer at all. Warehouse tables are Delta under the hood too (Fabric's whole pitch is one OneLake copy regardless of engine), but access patterns and parity here have shifted over time and I'm not fully confident there's zero practical difference today — if you ever wanted a data scientist doing ad-hoc Spark work directly against this data, Lakehouse is the more natural fit.
- **OneLake Shortcuts** — zero-copy references to this data from other Lakehouses/workspaces/external storage. Native to Lakehouse; I'm less certain of current parity for Warehouse-sourced tables.

Neither of those is something you're using, so weigh them as "future flexibility" rather than a current need.

**The real caveat before switching:** I don't have visibility into exactly how your existing Gold-layer `_Warehouse` (the one the Semantic Model queries) currently gets populated. If it's built via views doing cross-database queries against the Lakehouse's SQL endpoint — a common, supported Fabric pattern — then landing directly in a Warehouse instead might actually simplify that downstream wiring too. But it might also mean re-plumbing that layer, depending on how it's built. That's worth checking before treating this as a small change, since switching destinations means rewriting `DefaultDestination` (from `Lakehouse.Contents()` to whatever the Warehouse-write connector expects) across every dataflow, not flipping one setting.

Net: for your actual usage, Warehouse looks like the better fit — mainly because it removes a real, already-present pain point in your pipeline — but it's a genuine architecture change, not a tweak, so worth confirming the Gold-layer dependency before committing to it.

---

**You**

While we have three Warehouse for transformations (Dev, Test, Prod) we only have two Lakehouses for Landing (Dev and Prod).  This is working fine. 

So we'd probably set up landing Warehouses similarly and point the transformation Warehouses at them.  

Would anything need to change about this pattern if we used Warehouses instead of Lakehouses for landing?

---

**Claude**

Nothing needs to change about the *parameterization* pattern itself — the Variable Library / `Variable.ValueOrDefault` / Dev-Test-Prod valueSet mechanism is completely agnostic to what item type a variable's GUID points at. `LH_Lakehouse` today just happens to hold a Lakehouse's GUID; if landing moves to Warehouse, you'd rename it to something like `WH_Landing` and point it at the new Warehouse's GUID in each valueSet — same `$(/**/Variables/X)` substitution, same branch-based promotion, same everything. The asymmetry you have today (3 transformation Warehouses, only 2 landing Lakehouses, with Test presumably pointing at Dev's landing data) also carries over unchanged, since that sharing is driven by which variable value each environment resolves to, not by the destination item type. Nothing about switching item types forces you to add a third landing object.

What genuinely would need to change:

1. **`DefaultDestination`'s M expression.** Right now it's `Lakehouse.Contents(...)`. A Warehouse destination in Dataflow Gen2 almost certainly uses a different connector function — I don't have confident, verified knowledge of the exact syntax Fabric generates for a Warehouse-type destination binding, so rather than guess, I'd recommend the same empirical approach you used for the `.pqt` investigation: configure one dataflow's destination as a Warehouse through the Fabric UI, then inspect the resulting `mashup.pq`/`queryMetadata.json` to see the actual pattern it generates, before updating `standardize_dataflows.py`'s template.
2. **`standardize_dataflows.py` and `enforce_parameterization.py`.** Both currently hard-code the Lakehouse-specific `DEFAULT_DESTINATION` string and the `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`/`SchemaName` variable set. These would need updating to match whatever the real Warehouse destination pattern turns out to be from step 1.
3. **Drop `RefreshSqlEndpoint` from the pipeline.** As discussed, that activity (and its failure-handling branch) exists solely to work around the Lakehouse SQL endpoint's async metadata lag — a Warehouse write is immediately consistent, so this step becomes dead weight and should come out.
4. **Rewrite the transformation Warehouses' Gold-layer views/queries** that currently reference the landing Lakehouse (via whatever cross-database reference syntax they use today) to instead point at the new landing Warehouse's name. Mechanical, but real work across however many Gold objects exist.
5. **The one thing actually worth verifying before assuming this is symmetric:** how does the transformation Warehouse currently reach the landing Lakehouse's data — same-workspace cross-database query, a OneLake Shortcut, or something else? If landing and transformation live in different workspaces today, whatever mechanism bridges that gap might not carry over identically to a Warehouse-to-Warehouse reference. I don't have visibility into how that link is currently wired, so I'd confirm that before assuming "point the Warehouse at the other Warehouse" is a drop-in rename rather than something requiring new cross-workspace plumbing.

Net: the environment/promotion architecture you've already built stays exactly as-is — this is really just a destination-connector swap plus a mechanical downstream-reference update, not a rethink of the Dev/Test/Prod pattern.

---

**You**

Do Lakehouses perform better (on load or on query) than warehouses?

---

**Claude**

I don't have solid, current benchmark numbers I can cite with confidence — Fabric's Lakehouse and Warehouse engines have been converging, and stale performance claims from even a year ago could easily be wrong now. What I can give you is the architectural reasoning, with the caveat that if this decision hinges on precise numbers, the only reliable answer is benchmarking both in Dev with your actual data volumes.

**Storage is identical.** Both Lakehouse tables and Warehouse tables are stored as Delta Parquet in OneLake — there's no underlying storage-format advantage either way.

**Query engine is largely converged.** Microsoft has moved both the Lakehouse's SQL Analytics Endpoint and the Warehouse onto the same distributed query engine (Polaris) for reading Delta data from OneLake. For typical analytical/BI-style queries — the kind your Gold-layer star schema and Semantic Model do — I wouldn't expect a strong, systematic performance advantage either direction. Where Warehouse tends to have an edge is query *optimizer reliability*: since Warehouse is the authoritative write path, it maintains its own statistics natively, whereas the Lakehouse SQL endpoint's statistics/metadata are derived asynchronously from externally-written Delta files and can lag or be less complete — which can occasionally make query plans on the Lakehouse endpoint less predictable, though this isn't the same as "slower" across the board.

**Load/write performance is the more genuinely asymmetric side, but in a way that cuts both directions depending on what you mean by "load":**
- Writing into a Lakehouse (via Dataflow Gen2 or Spark) is essentially writing Parquet files directly to storage — lightweight, no transactional SQL engine overhead, generally efficient for high-throughput data landing.
- Writing into a Warehouse goes through a full T-SQL transactional engine, which carries more overhead per operation (though Fabric's Warehouse is specifically built for large analytical bulk loads, so this gap is narrower than it would be against a traditional OLTP engine).
- But — and this is the part that actually matters for your case — a Lakehouse write isn't immediately SQL-queryable; there's the async metadata-refresh lag we've already discussed (the reason `RefreshSqlEndpoint` exists). A Warehouse write is immediately consistent and queryable the moment it commits. So if "load performance" means "raw ingest throughput," Lakehouse likely has a slight edge; if it means "time until the data is reliably queryable downstream," Warehouse wins outright, since it has no lag to account for at all.

Given your actual workload — hourly incremental pulls from DB2, not big-data-scale ingestion — I'd expect the raw throughput difference to be small enough that it's not the deciding factor either way. The bigger, already-concrete difference remains what we discussed last turn: eliminating the SQL-endpoint lag and its pipeline workaround. If you want an actual answer rather than reasoning, the only way to get one with real confidence is to load a representative batch into both a test Lakehouse and test Warehouse and time it directly — I don't have benchmark data solid enough to hand you a number instead.

---

**You**

If we switched to Warehouses, would the data be immediately available in the next pipeline step or would there be a lag?

---

**Claude**

Immediately available — no lag, and no equivalent of `RefreshSqlEndpoint` would be needed.

The reason the Lakehouse lag exists at all is that the SQL Analytics Endpoint is a separate, asynchronously-refreshed metadata layer sitting on top of the actual Delta files — a dataflow's write activity reports "Succeeded" as soon as the Parquet write completes, but that success signal is independent of whether the SQL endpoint's catalog has caught up yet. That's exactly why the pipeline needs an explicit refresh step in between.

A Warehouse doesn't have that split. There's no separate "endpoint" — the write path and the query path are the same engine. A write to a Warehouse table is a transactional commit, and once the pipeline activity that performed the write reports "Succeeded," that data is immediately, fully visible to the very next query against it — including the very next pipeline activity, since Fabric only advances past a `dependsOn: Succeeded` condition once the activity has actually finished. So the next step in the pipeline can safely read the landed data with no polling, no wait, no separate refresh step required.

One thing worth keeping separate in your head, since it's easy to conflate: your `Refresh Semantic Model` step is still necessary regardless of which landing tier you use. That's a different kind of staleness — if the Semantic Model is in Import mode, it caches data and only picks up changes when it refreshes, which is orthogonal to Lakehouse-vs-Warehouse; that step stays in the pipeline either way. Only the `RefreshSqlEndpoint` step is the one that becomes unnecessary.

---

**You**

The Refresh SQL Endpoint can take up to 4 minutes.  Is there any equivalent (even if hidden) step with Warehouses that would cause it to take longer than a Lakehouse load?

---

**Claude**

Not in the same category of risk, though there's one thing worth distinguishing carefully rather than just saying "no lag at all."

The Lakehouse SQL endpoint lag is a **visibility/correctness** problem — during that window, a query against the endpoint may not see the new data or schema at all, meaning without the explicit refresh step you risk downstream queries running against stale or incomplete state. That's exactly why the pipeline needs a step that blocks progress until the endpoint has caught up.

A Warehouse write doesn't have an equivalent of that, because there's no separate catalog to catch up — the engine that writes the data is the same engine that serves queries against it. Once the write transaction commits (and the pipeline activity reports "Succeeded"), the data is fully and correctly visible to the next query. There's no analogous "the data might not be there yet" risk to guard against.

What Warehouse likely *does* have, though I don't have hard, current timing data to quantify it, is a **performance-tuning** background process rather than a correctness one: automatic statistics maintenance (and possibly file compaction, given the Delta storage underneath) that may run asynchronously after a load. If that lags behind, a query run immediately after the load might get a less-than-optimal query plan for a while — but it would still return complete, correct results. That's a fundamentally different risk profile than the Lakehouse endpoint gap: "maybe slightly slower for a bit" versus "maybe wrong/incomplete." I can't confirm the actual timing of that stats process without testing it directly, so if query *speed* immediately after load matters to you (not just correctness), that's worth a real benchmark rather than taking my word for it.

The other place a Warehouse write could genuinely take longer for the same data is the write itself — a transactional T-SQL commit carries more inherent overhead than a lightweight Parquet file write, so the load step itself might run a bit slower than an equivalent Lakehouse load. But that overhead scales with data volume rather than being a flat, semi-unpredictable tax the way the endpoint refresh is — given your data volumes (hourly incremental ERP pulls), I'd expect that difference to be seconds, not minutes.

Net: I don't see a hidden equivalent of "up to 4 minutes before the data is safely queryable." The closest analogous cost is a much lower-stakes performance nuance, not a correctness gate you'd need to build a pipeline step around.

---

**You**

We are actually truncating and reloading most tables.  Can you do a web search to assess Lakehouse vs. Warehouse write timing?

---

**Claude**

The search surfaced something concrete and directly relevant to your truncate-and-reload pattern that changes my earlier answer a bit — there is a hidden cost analogous to the SQL endpoint lag, just a different kind.

**Write/load speed:** Directionally, Lakehouse is the faster ingestion path. Microsoft's own Dataflow Gen2 performance guidance positions Lakehouse as optimized for high-throughput bulk writes — staging is disabled by default for non-Warehouse destinations so data writes directly to the destination, and Lakehouse-specific options like "Optimized copy to Lakehouse" or High-Scale Compute can push this further (one source cites up to ~10× faster versus non-high-scale runs). I didn't find a precise head-to-head number for Warehouse write time by comparison, but nothing suggests Warehouse writes are faster — a full T-SQL transactional load simply does more work per write than a direct Parquet write.

**Query-time visibility after a write:** This part of my earlier answer holds up — a Warehouse write is a transactional commit, so there's no separate catalog that needs to "catch up" the way the Lakehouse SQL endpoint does. No correctness/visibility risk equivalent to what you have today.

**The actual hidden cost, specific to truncate-and-reload:** Fabric Warehouse statistics are *not* automatically updated after data changes on an existing table — Microsoft's own docs are explicit that "statistical updating doesn't happen automatically," and recommend manually running `UPDATE STATISTICS` after a significant number of rows change. A full truncate-and-reload is precisely that kind of change, every single refresh. On top of that, there's a documented "cold start" behavior: if statistics don't exist yet (or data isn't cached), the *first* query to hit a table after a load can be measurably slower while Fabric synchronously builds the statistics it needs. So under your actual pattern — truncate and reload most tables, then immediately have downstream Gold-layer queries and Semantic Model refreshes read from them — every refresh cycle risks running against stale (pre-reload) statistics unless you explicitly add a statistics-refresh step to the pipeline.

This is a meaningfully different risk than the Lakehouse SQL endpoint lag, so I don't want to overstate the parallel: it's not a blocking "wait N minutes or risk wrong data" gate, it's a per-query performance risk (a worse query plan, not incomplete/incorrect results), and it's something you control by adding your own `UPDATE STATISTICS` step rather than something Fabric imposes on you unconditionally. But it is a real, recurring cost your current design doesn't have to think about with Lakehouse, and it's specific to the exact truncate-and-reload pattern you just told me you're using — so if you move landing to Warehouse, I'd plan on adding an explicit stats-maintenance step after each load, the same way `RefreshSqlEndpoint` exists today, just for a different reason.

One more data point for context: query performance is roughly symmetric and workload-dependent — one source pegs Warehouse ~15–25% faster on T-SQL analytical queries, Lakehouse ~15–25% faster on Spark SQL queries — each engine is faster at its own native query language, which for your case (T-SQL Gold-layer + Semantic Model) favors Warehouse on the *query* side, separate from the write-side tradeoffs above.

Sources:
- [Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse vs Warehouse: Complete Comparison and Decision Guide 2026](https://powerbiconsulting.com/blog/fabric-lakehouse-vs-warehouse-2026)
- [Microsoft Fabric Data Warehouse vs Lakehouse: Complete Architecture Guide for 2026](https://powerbiconsulting.com/blog/microsoft-fabric-data-warehouse-vs-lakehouse-guide-2026)
- [Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse vs Warehouse 1 Billion Rows which will be faster? - FourMoo](https://www.fourmoo.com/2026/02/25/microsoft-fabric-lakehouse-vs-warehouse-1-billion-rows-which-will-be-faster/)
- [Best practices for getting the best performance with Dataflow Gen2 - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-factory/dataflow-gen2-performance-best-practices)
- [Dataflow Gen2 data destinations and managed settings - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-factory/dataflow-gen2-data-destinations-and-managed-settings)
- [Performance guidelines in Fabric Data Warehouse - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-warehouse/guidelines-warehouse-performance)
- [Statistics - Microsoft Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-warehouse/statistics)
- [Microsoft Fabric Table Maintenance - Checkpoint and Statistics - MSSQLTips](https://www.mssqltips.com/sqlservertip/7962/microsoft-fabric-automatic-table-maintenance-checkpoint-statistics/)

---

**You**

Does Lakehouse not use query statistics at all?

---

**Claude**

Good catch to check — and this actually refines (and partly corrects) what I told you last turn. Lakehouse's SQL Analytics Endpoint absolutely does use query statistics — it's not something unique to Warehouse. Per Microsoft's own docs, "Fabric Data Warehouse and Lakehouse SQL analytics endpoint use and automatically maintain histogram statistics, average column length statistics, and table cardinality statistics." Both engines rely on the same kind of optimizer-driven statistics.

What differs is *how* that maintenance is bundled, and this is the part worth being precise about:

**For Lakehouse:** statistics maintenance is folded into the same background metadata-sync process that also updates table definitions and column types after a Delta table changes — i.e., the exact same sync mechanism responsible for the up-to-4-minute lag you already deal with today. So when you run `RefreshSqlEndpoint`, you're not just fixing schema/data visibility — you're incidentally freshening statistics too, as a side effect of the same process.

**For Warehouse:** statistics are still "automatic," but lazily — they get created/updated on demand when a query's optimizer actually needs them and they're missing or stale, which is the "cold start" cost I described last turn. Crucially, this is *decoupled* from data visibility (which is immediate), so there's no equivalent forcing-function in your pipeline that happens to freshen stats as a side effect the way `RefreshSqlEndpoint` does today. Microsoft's own guidance explicitly recommends running `UPDATE STATISTICS` proactively during a maintenance window — e.g., right after a load — specifically to avoid your first real downstream query paying that cost.

So the accurate framing isn't "Warehouse has a hidden stats cost that Lakehouse doesn't" — both have essentially the same lazy-stats model. It's that **Lakehouse gets stats freshening bundled into a step you already have, while Warehouse would need that added as its own explicit step**, since you'd otherwise be dropping the one step that happened to cover it. If you move landing to Warehouse, I'd still plan on adding an `UPDATE STATISTICS` step after each truncate-and-reload — not because Warehouse is worse here, but because you'd no longer have anything else in the pipeline incidentally doing that job.

Sources:
- [SQL Analytics Endpoint Performance Considerations - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-warehouse/sql-analytics-endpoint-performance)
- [What is the SQL analytics endpoint for a lakehouse? - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/lakehouse-sql-analytics-endpoint)
- [SQL Analytics Endpoint Metadata Sync - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/sql-analytics-endpoint-metadata-sync)
- [Performance guidelines in Fabric Data Warehouse - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-warehouse/guidelines-warehouse-performance)

**You**

Are "Optimized copy to Lakehouse" or High-Scale Compute available/useful when pulling from a DB2 source?  I'd been assuming that most of the time was the query against the source system rather than the database write, though I'm not sure about that.

---

**Claude**

Good instinct to question that — the search results actually confirm your suspicion pretty directly, and reveal that neither feature would likely apply to your DB2 source anyway.

**Fast Copy (the mechanism behind "Optimized copy to Lakehouse") has an explicit supported-connector list: ADLS Gen2, Fabric Lakehouse, Fabric Warehouse, OData, SharePoint, and Web.** DB2 isn't on it. Unsupported connectors "fall back to the standard engine automatically" — meaning your DB2 source read would just run through the normal (non-accelerated) path regardless of whether you enable staging or the optimized-copy option downstream. One source does mention Fast Copy now supporting on-premises gateway sources like SQL Server, but DB2 specifically isn't called out anywhere as supported. So even setting aside whether it's useful, "Optimized copy to Lakehouse" wouldn't get applied to the actual pull from DB2 — it only accelerates the hop from staging *to* the Lakehouse destination, which is a write-side optimization, not a source-read one.

**High-Scale Compute requires staging enabled + a Lakehouse destination, and it scales downstream *transformation* compute** — not the initial source read either. Per the docs, the intended pattern is "Fast Copy to accelerate ingestion and High-Scale Compute to scale downstream transformation work." Looking at your actual dataflows, though, there isn't much downstream transformation happening in Power Query at all — your queries embed the real work as a native SQL string passed straight to `DB2.Database(...)` (e.g., the `ORD_HEAD_Hourly` query), so DB2 itself executes the select and Power Query is mostly just streaming the result through. That means there's little "downstream transformation" work for High-Scale Compute to meaningfully accelerate even if you did enable it.

**On your actual question — where the time is going:** this supports your assumption well. The docs explicitly flag on-premises gateways as a real bottleneck source, but the mechanism they describe is gateway-side *compute* for transformations that can't fold back to the source — "the gateway processes all the transformations on the computer hosting the gateway" when a dataflow is complex. Since your queries push the real logic into native DB2 SQL rather than doing it in Power Query steps after the fact, you're largely avoiding that specific gateway-compute penalty already. That leaves DB2's own query execution time and the data-transfer volume/latency through the gateway and network as the most likely dominant costs — which lines up with your assumption, not against it.

Practical implication for the Lakehouse-vs-Warehouse decision: neither engine's write-acceleration features (Fast Copy, High-Scale Compute, Optimized copy) apply to a DB2 source, so the write-speed difference between Lakehouse and Warehouse is probably not the dominant lever in your total refresh time either way. If refresh time actually matters enough to optimize, the higher-value place to look is the DB2 side — query efficiency/indexing on the RPC ERP tables and gateway sizing/throughput — rather than which Fabric destination type you land in.
