**You**

You mentioned a source that suggested that SQL queries against a Warehouse are faster than SQL queries against a Lakehouse.  Currently we have Warehouse views pointing to Lakehouse data.  Is it realistic to think that we could achieve that 10%+ improvement in query performance if we switched to using a Warehouse as the landing place for data?

---

**Claude**

Short answer: I wouldn't bank on that specific 10-25% figure, because it's not actually measuring the scenario you're describing — but there are two real, narrower reasons a genuine (if unquantified) improvement is plausible, plus one much cheaper thing worth trying first.

**Why that stat likely doesn't transfer directly:** the 15-25% figure was comparing T-SQL-against-native-Warehouse vs. Spark-SQL-against-Lakehouse — two different query engines. But your current setup (Warehouse views querying Lakehouse tables via cross-database T-SQL) isn't using Spark SQL at all — it's already going through the same underlying engine that powers the Warehouse itself, since Microsoft's own docs confirm the SQL Analytics Endpoint "runs on the same engine as the Fabric Data Warehouse." So the big "different engine" gap that stat is measuring doesn't really apply to what you're doing today.

**Two real, documented reasons a smaller improvement is still plausible:**

1. **Predicate pushdown maturity.** One source is explicit that "the Warehouse has more mature support for cross-warehouse joins with full predicate pushdown" compared to cross-item joins against a Lakehouse. If your Gold-layer views filter or join against the landing tables, moving those tables into the same Warehouse (eliminating the cross-item boundary entirely) could plausibly get better pushdown and less data scanned — but I don't have a specific percentage for this, only that it's called out as a real, mature-vs-less-mature distinction.
2. **Parquet file layout/fragmentation.** This is the more concrete, actionable one: SQL Analytics Endpoint performance "depends heavily on the physical layout of the underlying Parquet files," and a large number of small files (which is exactly what frequent truncate-and-reload cycles tend to produce if you're not running maintenance) "creates overhead and negatively affects query performance." Given your Lakehouse tables get fully replaced on every refresh, this is a genuinely likely factor if you're not periodically running `OPTIMIZE`/compaction on them.

**One correction to my own earlier reasoning:** I'd speculated V-Order might explain some of the Warehouse advantage, but this search shows V-Order is enabled by default in *both* Lakehouse and Warehouse — so that ~10% figure is V-Order-on vs. V-Order-off, not a Lakehouse-vs-Warehouse gap. Scratch that as a differentiator.

**Practical recommendation:** the cheapest, fastest, fully reversible experiment here isn't switching landing to Warehouse — it's running `OPTIMIZE` (and checking `VACUUM` settings) against your existing landing Lakehouse tables and re-measuring your Gold-layer view query times. If file fragmentation is the real culprit, you'd see a measurable improvement without touching the architecture at all. That result would also tell you how much of the gap is "fixable Lakehouse maintenance" vs. genuinely structural — which is the piece neither I nor these sources can quantify for your specific queries without you actually testing it.

Sources:

- [SQL Analytics Endpoint Performance Considerations - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/sql-analytics-endpoint-performance)
- [Querying Lakehouse data from Warehouse in Microsoft Fabric - LinkedIn](https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/querying-lakehouse-data-from-warehouse-microsoft-fabric-jovan-popovic)
- [Microsoft Fabric Query Performance Optimization: Complete 2026 Guide](https://www.e6data.com/query-and-cost-optimization-hub/how-to-optimize-microsoft-fabric-query-performance)
- [Does V-order auto enabled in Datawarehouse and Lakehouse - Microsoft Fabric Community](https://community.fabric.microsoft.com/t5/Data-Warehouse/Does-V-order-is-auto-enabled-in-Datawarehouse-and-Lakehouse/m-p/4402100)

**You**

How would we run Optimize or set Vaccum settings?

---

**Claude**

Two ways to do this, and which one fits depends on whether you're testing once or building this into your regular pipeline. One thing confirmed by this search that ties back to your earlier ALTER TABLE error: **`OPTIMIZE` and `VACUUM` can't be run from the SQL query editor at all** — same restriction as before. They only work from a Notebook, a Spark Job Definition, or Fabric's dedicated "Lakehouse Maintenance" pipeline activity.

**Option 1 — Lakehouse Maintenance pipeline activity (best fit for your setup, since everything else you have is pipeline-based).**

1. Open your pipeline (or a new one) in the Fabric workspace, and in the Activities pane search for **Lakehouse Maintenance**.
2. Drag it onto the canvas, point it at the target Lakehouse and table(s).
3. It exposes `OPTIMIZE` (with an optional **V-Order** checkbox, and Z-Order if you want to cluster by specific columns) and `VACUUM` as configurable options directly in the activity settings — no code required.
4. Wire it in as a dependency right after your dataflow refresh (or after all landing dataflows finish for a given run), the same way `RefreshSqlEndpoint` is wired in today. You can run it ad hoc or schedule it.
5. Runs show up in Monitoring hub → Pipelines, filterable by the Lakehouse Maintenance activity name, and as a `TableMaintenance` entry.

This is the natural place to add it given your existing `RPC MASTER HOURLY_INCREMENTAL` pipeline structure — same pattern as your existing activities, no new tooling to learn.

**Option 2 — Notebook (Spark SQL), better for a quick one-off test to see if this is actually your bottleneck.**

```sql
OPTIMIZE Gold.YourTableName VORDER
VACUUM Gold.YourTableName RETAIN 168 HOURS
```

- `OPTIMIZE ... VORDER` compacts small files and re-applies V-Order sorting — worth noting: V-Order's benefit is bigger than the ~10% I mentioned last turn for SQL-based queries specifically; it's actually documented as **40-60% improvement for Power BI Direct Lake cold-cache queries**, and ~10% for SQL analytics endpoint/Warehouse reads. Since V-Order is on by default already, running `OPTIMIZE VORDER` explicitly matters most when files have accumulated from repeated incremental writes and need re-compaction/re-sorting, which is exactly your truncate-and-reload pattern.
- `VACUUM ... RETAIN 168 HOURS` is the default (7 days) — this is the safe floor. Going below 7 days works but requires explicitly disabling a Spark safety check (`spark.databricks.delta.retentionDurationCheck.enabled`), and risks breaking any in-flight reads or time-travel queries against files that get deleted too soon. I'd stick with the 7-day default unless storage costs specifically require reclaiming space faster.

For your immediate question from last turn — is Parquet fragmentation actually hurting your Gold-layer query times — the fastest path is a quick Notebook, run `OPTIMIZE` on one or two of your busiest landing tables, then re-run your existing Gold view queries and compare timing. If you want it as a permanent fix rather than a one-off test, that's when it's worth formalizing as a Lakehouse Maintenance activity in the pipeline.

Sources:
- [Lakehouse Maintenance Activity - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-factory/lakehouse-maintenance-activity)
- [Delta Table Maintenance in Microsoft Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/lakehouse-table-maintenance)
- [Optimize Delta Lake tables with V-Order in Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/delta-optimization-and-v-order)
- [VACUUM Delta tables - Microsoft Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/delta-lake-vacuum)
- [Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse OPTIMIZE and VACUUM for Table Maintenance - MSSQLTips](https://www.mssqltips.com/sqlservertip/7989/microsoft-fabric-lakehouse-optimize-and-vacuum-for-table-maintenance/)

---

**You**

We're going to start using some incrementally loaded tables. Does Optimize and Vacuum work differently on them?  

Would Refresh SQL Endpoint run faster if we ran Optimize or Vaccum nightly?

---

**Claude**

Good questions — and the search actually surfaced something important that bears directly on both, plus a separate finding that could matter more than either.

**Do OPTIMIZE/VACUUM work differently on incrementally loaded tables?**

The commands themselves are identical regardless of load pattern (`OPTIMIZE table VORDER`, `VACUUM table RETAIN N HOURS`), but the fragmentation behavior — and how you should apply them — differs meaningfully:

- **Truncate-and-reload** replaces the table's entire active file set in one shot per refresh. Fragmentation is bounded to whatever that single write produces, so OPTIMIZE is a once-per-refresh concern, and there isn't really a "just the new data" distinction — you're rewriting the whole table every time anyway.
- **Incremental loads accumulate files continuously between compactions, and Microsoft's own docs confirm this directly**: "When there's an update or delete operation, a Delta table adds new Parquet files with the changeset, which increases the number of files over time... If you don't schedule maintenance, this pattern eventually creates a read overhead and impacts the time it takes to sync changes to SQL analytics endpoint." So incremental tables need OPTIMIZE on a regular, ongoing cadence — not just an occasional cleanup — and best practice is to target *just the newly added data* with a predicate rather than recompacting the whole table each time, since that avoids rescanning files you already optimized last cycle.
- **MERGE-based incremental loads (upserts) are the most fragmentation-prone of all.** Touching even a single row in a file forces Delta to rewrite that entire file, so upserts generate real file churn — and correspondingly more dead files for VACUUM to clean up — even for small updates. This is where Z-ORDER (clustering the table by whatever key your MERGE condition matches on) genuinely earns its keep, since it concentrates related rows into fewer files so a merge touches less. But Z-ORDER itself rewrites the entire table when you run it, so it's a periodic/occasional operation, not something to bolt onto nightly maintenance the way OPTIMIZE compaction is.

**Would nightly OPTIMIZE/VACUUM make RefreshSqlEndpoint faster?**

Yes, and this is now directly confirmed rather than inferred — the same Microsoft doc states unmanaged file growth "impacts the time it takes to sync changes to SQL analytics endpoint." Fewer, larger, well-compacted files mean less for the sync process to reconcile. VACUUM's role here is more about storage cost than sync speed specifically — the sync process should mainly care about the currently active file set, not tombstoned ones — but keeping both current is good hygiene regardless, and matters more for your incremental tables than your truncate-and-reload ones given the fragmentation pattern above.

**The bigger finding, worth flagging on its own:** Microsoft announced a new SQL Analytics Endpoint metadata sync architecture in preview (May 2026) that keeps data "available for querying within seconds of it landing in the lakehouse" — a dramatic improvement over the multi-minute lag you're dealing with today. The catch: it "applies only to new SQL analytics endpoints," not retroactively to existing ones. This is worth checking directly — if it's available for your Fabric capacity/region, it could shrink or eliminate the whole 4-minute lag problem that's been the throughline of this entire Lakehouse-vs-Warehouse discussion, independent of anything else we've talked about (maintenance, destination type, etc.). Worth a look before investing further in either OPTIMIZE/VACUUM scheduling or a full landing-architecture switch.

Sources:
- [SQL Analytics Endpoint Performance Considerations - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/sql-analytics-endpoint-performance)
- [SQL Analytics Endpoint Metadata Sync - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/sql-analytics-endpoint-metadata-sync)
- [Table Compaction - Microsoft Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/table-compaction)
- [Best practices: Delta Lake - Azure Databricks - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/databricks/delta/best-practices)
- [Delta Lake Small File Compaction with OPTIMIZE - delta.io](https://delta.io/blog/2023-01-25-delta-lake-small-file-compaction-optimize/)
- [Why MERGE Is Slower Than You Think - Medium](https://medium.com/@harsh11csb/why-merge-is-slower-than-you-think-and-how-to-design-fast-upsert-pipelines-in-delta-lake-979c45491e0c)

---

**You**

Most of our tables will be truncate and reload.  I'm not following whether Optimze and Vaccum is really helpful for these.  If it is, would it have to be run after each load?

For Warehouse, is there an equivalent set of processes we'd have to maintain, or just updating query statistics?

Are all of these processes runnable by table, or are they database-wide?

---

**Claude**

This round of research actually simplifies part of the picture a lot. Let me take each in turn.

**Is OPTIMIZE/VACUUM helpful for truncate-and-reload tables?**

**VACUUM: yes, clearly — arguably more so than for incremental tables.** A truncate-and-reload doesn't literally delete the old files; a Delta "replace"/overwrite marks the *entire previous file set* as removed and writes a fresh one. Those old files stay on disk as dead weight until VACUUM cleans them up. So an hourly-refreshed table isn't just accumulating a small changeset of dead files each cycle — it's orphaning a full copy of the previous table's files every single time. Over a day of hourly refreshes, that's a lot of dead storage sitting around. This doesn't need to run after every load, though — the default 7-day retention means dead files are safe to leave in place until a periodic sweep; nightly VACUUM cleans up everything past the retention window in one pass regardless of how many reloads happened that day.

**OPTIMIZE: genuinely less clear-cut, and worth checking before assuming you need it.** The small-file problem OPTIMIZE solves is really about files accumulating *across* writes — but a truncate-and-reload resets the active file set from scratch every time, so there's no cross-cycle pileup the way there is with incremental appends. The only way OPTIMIZE would help here is if a *single* reload's own write happens to produce a bunch of small files rather than a few well-sized ones — which depends on how Dataflow Gen2's writer partitions its output for your data volume, something I don't have specific confirmation of one way or the other. Some write paths apply "optimized writes" at write time specifically to avoid this, which if in effect would mean there's nothing for OPTIMIZE to fix at all. The concrete way to know is exactly what I suggested earlier: look at the actual file count/sizes in one of your landing tables right after a refresh. If it's already a handful of reasonably-sized files, OPTIMIZE isn't buying you anything on that table. If it's dozens of tiny files, it is.

If it does turn out to help, cadence should track your consumption pattern rather than a fixed schedule: OPTIMIZE's benefit is faster reads on the current data, so if downstream queries hit the table shortly after each reload (e.g., hourly dashboards), you'd want it run after each load — waiting for a nightly job means everything queried during the day ran against unoptimized files. If consumption is only end-of-day, nightly is fine. That's different from VACUUM, where timing relative to query load doesn't matter.

**For Warehouse — this is now confirmed, not just inferred, and it's simpler than Lakehouse.** Per Microsoft: "the Warehouse automatically optimizes data layouts, and performs garbage collection and compaction... you do not need to manually optimize Fabric Data Warehouse tables — the system handles this automatically as a background process." So there's no OPTIMIZE/VACUUM equivalent to schedule at all for Warehouse — compaction and dead-file cleanup happen transparently. The one thing that remains your responsibility, as established earlier, is statistics: Warehouse auto-creates missing stats on demand, but doesn't auto-refresh *existing* stats after a data change, so running `UPDATE STATISTICS` after a truncate-and-reload is still worth doing proactively. Net: Lakehouse gives you three things to think about (OPTIMIZE, VACUUM, stats); Warehouse gives you one (stats).

**Are these per-table or database-wide?**

Per-table, both by command and by tooling. `OPTIMIZE schema.table` / `VACUUM schema.table` are explicit single-table commands, and the Lakehouse Maintenance pipeline activity is likewise "designed to work with individual tables" — one activity instance targets one table, not the whole Lakehouse. For running maintenance across many tables at once, the documented approach is a **notebook that iterates through the Lakehouse's table list programmatically** (looping over the catalog and calling OPTIMIZE/VACUUM per table, optionally with concurrency) rather than hand-adding one pipeline activity per table. Given you have a growing number of landing tables and this repo already leans on Python scripting for everything else, that notebook-loop approach is probably the more scalable fit if OPTIMIZE/VACUUM ends up being warranted across multiple tables, rather than manually wiring up a pipeline activity per table.

Sources:
- [Smart Fabric Warehouse: Next-Gen Auto Maintenance](https://www.hubsite365.com/en-ww/crm-pages/automated-maintenance-features-in-fabric-warehouse.htm)
- [Automate Delta Tables Maintenance in a Microsoft Fabric Warehouse - MSSQLTips](https://www.mssqltips.com/sqlservertip/7950/automate-delta-tables-maintenance-microsoft-fabric-warehouse/)
- [Cross-Workload Table Maintenance and Optimization - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/fundamentals/table-maintenance-optimization)
- [Lakehouse Maintenance Activity - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-factory/lakehouse-maintenance-activity)
- [Automate Lakehouse Table Maintenance In Fabric - Kevin of Tech](https://www.kevinoftech.com/Blog/Post/2025-10-14-automate-ms-fabric-lh-maintenance)
- [Delta Lake Small File Compaction with OPTIMIZE - delta.io](https://delta.io/blog/2023-01-25-delta-lake-small-file-compaction-optimize/)
- [Control data file size - Azure Databricks - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/databricks/delta/tune-file-size)

---

**You**

Is updating query statistics per-table?

---

**Claude**

Both scopes exist, but only one is confirmed as Fabric-specific.

**Per-table (confirmed Fabric Warehouse syntax):**
```sql
UPDATE STATISTICS [schema_name.]table_name [(statistics_name)] [WITH FULLSCAN | SAMPLE number PERCENT]
```
This is the documented Fabric Warehouse form — targets one table, optionally one specific statistics object on that table (e.g., `UPDATE STATISTICS dbo.DimCustomer (DimCustomer_CustomerKey_FullScan) WITH FULLSCAN;`). One caveat specific to Fabric: `RESAMPLE` isn't supported here, unlike classic SQL Server.

**Database-wide:** `sp_updatestats` is the classic SQL Server/Synapse mechanism that runs `UPDATE STATISTICS` against every user and internal table in the database in one call — a genuine "update everything" shortcut. I don't have direct confirmation from Fabric-specific documentation that `sp_updatestats` is supported in Fabric Warehouse's T-SQL surface, as opposed to classic SQL Server or Synapse dedicated pools — Fabric Warehouse doesn't implement the full T-SQL feature set those engines have. Worth confirming directly (just try running it against a test Warehouse) before relying on it as your maintenance mechanism, rather than assuming parity.

Given that uncertainty, the safer bet if you want "update stats on everything after a load" without confirming `sp_updatestats` works is the same pattern as the Lakehouse notebook-loop approach — a script that enumerates your tables and issues `UPDATE STATISTICS` per table — which you know works since it's just the documented per-table command run repeatedly.

Sources:
- [Statistics - Microsoft Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-warehouse/statistics)
- [UPDATE STATISTICS (Transact-SQL) - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/t-sql/statements/update-statistics-transact-sql?view=sql-server-ver17)
- [sys.sp_updatestats (Transact-SQL) - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/sql/relational-databases/system-stored-procedures/sp-updatestats-transact-sql?view=sql-server-ver17)

---

**You**

If I wanted to try a comparison, loading the entire database into a Lakehouse vs. a Warehouse, it seems like I'd just have two sets of dataflows and pipelines and could run one then the other.  Would I just need to swap the destination variable in the variable library, or is there more to it?

---

**Claude**

Not just a variable swap — the underlying M code has to change too, because the destination binding uses a different connector function depending on item type. `DefaultDestination` today calls `Lakehouse.Contents(...)`; a Warehouse destination needs whatever the equivalent Warehouse-write connector function is, which — as I flagged a while back — I haven't actually verified the exact syntax for. Pointing `Lakehouse.Contents()` at a Warehouse's GUID wouldn't work; it's the wrong function for that item type entirely. So your "two sets of dataflows" instinct is right, but each set needs genuinely different `DefaultDestination` code, not the same code with a different variable value plugged in.

Given that, here's what a real comparison actually needs:

1. **Verify the Warehouse destination syntax first**, before duplicating anything. Configure one throwaway dataflow's destination as a Warehouse through the Fabric UI, then export/inspect the resulting `mashup.pq` the same way you did earlier for the `.pqt` investigation — that tells you the real connector function and destination-metadata shape to use, rather than guessing.
2. **An actual new Warehouse item** to land into — this is a real Fabric item you'd create, not a config value.
3. **A new Variable Library variable** (e.g., something like `WH_Landing`) holding that Warehouse's GUID, alongside the existing `LH_Lakehouse` — both can coexist in the same valueSet files without conflict.
4. **A genuinely separate set of dataflows** with `DefaultDestination` rewritten to use the Warehouse connector from step 1, and separate dataflow IDs — meaning a separate pipeline (or at least separate `RefreshDataflow` activities) that invoke *these* dataflow IDs rather than the Lakehouse ones, since the pipeline's job is just "refresh this dataflow ID" and doesn't care where that dataflow's own destination points.
5. **Drop the `RefreshSqlEndpoint` step** from the Warehouse-side test pipeline — it's Lakehouse-specific and either does nothing useful or references the wrong item if left in.
6. **Your existing automation won't recognize the Warehouse-pattern dataflows.** `standardize_dataflows.py` and `enforce_parameterization.py` are built around the `Lakehouse.Contents()` pattern and the `WS_Platform`/`LH_Lakehouse`/`SchemaName` variable set — running them against the test dataflows would either leave the Warehouse-pattern ones untouched (harmless) or potentially misfire if their name-matching happens to overlap. Safest to keep the test dataflows out of whatever folder/scope those scripts scan, at least for this experiment.
7. **Run the two sequentially, not concurrently**, if you're timing this — both draw on the same Fabric capacity, so running them side-by-side risks compute contention skewing whichever one goes second (or both).
8. **Scope check:** if you only care about landing/load-time comparison, you can stop there. If you want to compare the *query* side too (the actual point of this whole thread — whether native Warehouse tables beat cross-database Warehouse-over-Lakehouse queries), you'd also need a parallel set of Gold-layer views pointing at the new Warehouse-landed tables, since your existing Gold views only reference the Lakehouse today.

So: real work, but bounded — one verified destination pattern, one new item, one new variable, a duplicated dataflow/pipeline set, and awareness that your governance scripts won't touch the new pattern automatically.

---

**You**

Ok. If I tried it with one large dataflow on the write side, then testing reads with and without updating statstitics, would I be capturing most of the potential differences in performance between the two?

---

**Claude**

It would capture the two dimensions that are most directly comparable in a single run, but there's one significant gap this design can't surface at all, plus one thing to adjust in how you structure the "read" part.

**What this captures well:**
- **Raw write/load speed** — a single large write directly measures the Lakehouse-lightweight-Parquet-write vs. Warehouse-transactional-T-SQL-write difference. Solid.
- **Statistics/cold-start read behavior** — this is exactly what "with and without updating statistics" targets, and it's a real, documented dimension. One thing to get right: the "without" case means something different per engine — for Warehouse it means not running `UPDATE STATISTICS` after the load; for Lakehouse it means not having run the endpoint refresh (since that's what freshens Lakehouse's stats). Make sure you're testing the engine-appropriate maintenance action on each side, not the identical command.

**What it can't capture at all: fragmentation and the operational-maintenance burden over time.** This was actually the biggest recurring theme in this whole discussion — Lakehouse needs manual OPTIMIZE/VACUUM, Warehouse handles compaction and garbage collection automatically — and that difference only shows up *after repeated write cycles accumulate*, not from a single write. One big dataflow run gives you a pristine, freshly-loaded table on both sides; it can't reveal how a Lakehouse table degrades after 30 nights of truncate-and-reload without maintenance versus a Warehouse table that never needs it. If you want that dimension in the comparison, you'd need to either loop the same dataflow run repeatedly (simulating weeks of cycles) before measuring read performance, or treat it as already answered from earlier in this conversation rather than something this experiment needs to re-prove — Warehouse doesn't need it, Lakehouse does, full stop, independent of any timing test.

**One thing to adjust for the read test to be meaningful:** if you query the landed table directly (a plain `SELECT` against it), you'll measure raw read speed but miss the predicate-pushdown-maturity difference we found earlier — cross-warehouse-to-warehouse joins have more mature pushdown support than cross-warehouse-to-Lakehouse joins. Since your actual production pattern is a Warehouse *view* joining/filtering against the landing table (not a raw scan), the read test should mirror that — run it as a join/filter query shaped like your real Gold-layer views, not a bare table read — or you'll only be testing a query pattern you don't actually use.

One smaller consideration: your real workload is several separate, modest-sized dataflows (Orders, Product, Customer/Locations, etc.) rather than one big one — a single large dataflow tells you about per-write throughput, but not about any contention effects if multiple dataflows/views end up hitting the same target concurrently, the way your actual hourly pipeline does. Probably not worth engineering around for a first pass, but worth knowing it's out of scope for what this test tells you.

---

**You**

When you refer to a "target" are you talking about a database or a table?  We're not hitting the same table multiple times.

If we vacuum old files from a lakehouse, does that remove our ability to pull historical data if we've accidentally overwritten something we didn't want to?

---

**Claude**

**On "target" — clarifying, since you're right to push on that.** I didn't mean the same table — I meant the same Lakehouse or Warehouse *item* (or more broadly, the same underlying Fabric capacity), which can still create contention even when every dataflow writes to its own distinct table. Two reasons: (1) the SQL Analytics Endpoint's metadata sync operates at the whole-Lakehouse level, reconciling *all* tables' Delta logs together — several dataflows writing to different tables in the same Lakehouse concurrently are all feeding that one sync process; (2) everything ultimately draws from the same Fabric capacity's shared compute pool, so concurrent dataflow refreshes compete for that throughput budget regardless of which table each one touches. Since you're not hitting the same table twice, you don't have to worry about row/file-level write conflicts — but item-level and capacity-level contention are still a real, separate consideration if multiple dataflows run at the same time against the same Lakehouse/Warehouse or same capacity.

**On VACUUM removing historical recovery ability — yes, that's exactly what it does, and this is worth taking seriously.**

Delta tables keep old file versions around specifically to support time travel (`SELECT ... VERSION AS OF n` / `TIMESTAMP AS OF`) and `RESTORE` (a permanent rollback to a prior version) — but both require the underlying files to still physically exist. VACUUM's whole job is to delete files no longer referenced by the current version, once they're past the retention window (default: 7 days for data files, 30 days for the transaction log itself). Per the docs: "you must ensure that VACUUM has not deleted older data files; otherwise, restoring to those versions would be impossible." So if you accidentally overwrite something and don't catch it until after VACUUM has cleaned up the files from before the mistake, that data is genuinely gone — there's no other backstop.

Practical implication for your truncate-and-reload tables: without VACUUM running at all, you'd have *unlimited* recovery — every past version stays queryable forever, since nothing's ever cleaned up (that's exactly why VACUUM exists in the first place — unbounded storage growth otherwise). VACUUM is the deliberate trade of that unlimited safety net for reclaimed storage. Given you're specifically worried about this failure mode, it's worth choosing retention deliberately rather than defaulting to 7 days automatically — if your realistic "time to notice a bad overwrite" is longer than a week (e.g., caught during a monthly reconciliation), you'd want a longer retention window, at the cost of more storage held. Whatever OPTIMIZE/VACUUM automation you end up scheduling should use a retention period matched to that reality, not just whatever's fastest to clean up.

**One asymmetry worth knowing given you're weighing this against Warehouse:** Warehouse has its own version of this — automatic restore points, generated every 8 hours (plus user-defined ones), letting you roll the warehouse back to a prior point in time. But it's **whole-warehouse scope, not per-table** — "restore points affect the entire warehouse." So if one table gets accidentally corrupted in a Warehouse, recovering it means either rolling back *every* table in that warehouse to the same point in time, or restoring a temporary copy of the whole warehouse elsewhere and manually copying just the one recovered table back. Lakehouse's per-table VACUUM/time-travel/RESTORE is more surgical — you can recover one table without touching anything else — but it requires you to actively manage retention yourself rather than getting it automatically. That's a real, structural trade-off between the two, not just a maintenance-burden difference.

Sources:
- [A Databricks Guide to Managing Delta Table History, Data Retention and Data Recovery - Medium](https://medium.com/data-science-collective/a-data-practitioners-guide-to-managing-delta-table-history-data-retention-and-data-recovery-6d806488a835)
- [How to Rollback a Delta Lake Table to a Previous Version with Restore - delta.io](https://delta.io/blog/2022-10-03-rollback-delta-lake-restore/)
- [Query Delta tables with time travel - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/delta-lake-time-travel)
- [Restore In-Place of a Warehouse from a Restore Point - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-warehouse/restore-in-place)
- [Data retention in Fabric Data Warehouse - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-warehouse/data-retention)
- [Configurable Data Retention in Microsoft Fabric Warehouse (Preview) - Microsoft Fabric Community](https://community.fabric.microsoft.com/t5/Fabric-Updates-Blog/Configurable-Data-Retention-in-Microsoft-Fabric-Warehouse/ba-p/5181211)

---

**You**

I don't think that we can go back indefinitely to restore old table versions in Lakehouse, and it seems unlikely that Microsoft would ever store all this data by default.  14 days sounds familiar to me, though it might be 30.  Assuming that historical table restore does disappear at some point, is there any reason to think that it wouldn't have the same performance benefit as Vacuum?

---

**Claude**

Good instinct to push back — you're partly right, and it turns out the correction reveals something more important than either of us assumed initially.

**On data files specifically: my original claim holds.** A Fabric Community source is explicit: "if you don't choose to run vacuuming, then the Lakehouse tables will never be vacuumed." The actual Parquet data files really do accumulate indefinitely with no automatic deletion — that part isn't Microsoft quietly cleaning up behind the scenes.

**But you're right that something does expire automatically — it's the transaction log, not the data files, and this changes the answer meaningfully.** Delta Lake maintains two separate retention settings: log retention (default ~30 days) and data-file retention (default 7 days, which is what VACUUM enforces). The log entries — the metadata records describing which files belong to which version — get pruned automatically via checkpoint cleanup on their own schedule, regardless of whether you ever run VACUUM. I found 30 days consistently across sources, not 14 — you might be thinking of that same number, or a different Fabric-specific setting I haven't turned up; worth checking your workspace's actual `delta.logRetentionDuration` if you want the precise figure for your tables.

Here's why that matters: **time travel and RESTORE require *both* the log entry for a version and the underlying data files to still exist.** So even if you never run VACUUM, once the automatic log cleanup prunes the entry for an old version (by default, around 30 days out), you lose the ability to time-travel or restore to that version — even though the actual data files are still sitting there physically, unreferenced, taking up space.

**Now, your actual question — no, this automatic expiration would not give you the same performance benefit as VACUUM, and the reason is worth being precise about.** VACUUM's benefit comes specifically from *deleting* the physical files — reclaiming storage, and possibly reducing file-listing overhead in that storage location. Automatic log cleanup does neither of those things: it only removes the metadata pointer that makes an old version addressable. The dead files themselves are completely untouched by it. So relying on "it'll expire on its own eventually" instead of actually running VACUUM gets you the worst of both outcomes: you eventually lose your recovery safety net (because the log entry needed for time travel disappears on its own schedule), while gaining none of VACUUM's storage or performance benefit (because the orphaned files are never actually deleted — they just sit there, invisible and useless, forever, unless VACUUM removes them). That's a genuinely worse position than either "VACUUM deliberately, get the storage/perf benefit, accept the shorter recovery window" or "never run VACUUM, keep unlimited recovery, accept unbounded storage growth" — doing nothing doesn't preserve the safety you might assume it does.

Sources:
- [Solved: Delta table maintenance - is it managed? - Microsoft Fabric Community](https://community.fabric.microsoft.com/t5/Data-Engineering/Delta-table-maintenance-is-it-managed/m-p/3969634)
- [VACUUM Delta tables - Microsoft Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/delta-lake-vacuum)
- [Mastering Delta Lake Log Retention and Cleanup - Medium](https://medium.com/@senthilcegptech/delta-lake-log-retention-and-cleanup-84832c1c3a49)
- [Databricks Delta Lake Time Travel: Understanding Retention Before You Lose Your Data - Medium](https://medium.com/@vinodkumar.mani/databricks-delta-lake-time-travel-understanding-retention-before-you-lose-your-data-f4ea26ccb934)
- [Table utility commands - Delta Lake docs](https://docs.delta.io/delta-utility/)

---

**You**

For incrementally loaded tables, would I want to never run vaccum?

---

**Claude**

No — "never run VACUUM" isn't the right takeaway here either, though the reasoning differs a bit from truncate-and-reload tables.

**Incremental tables generally accumulate dead-file volume more slowly, so the pressure to VACUUM is less urgent, not zero.** A truncate-and-reload orphans a full copy of the table every single cycle — hourly reloads over a week is a lot of dead weight. An append-only incremental load only orphans whatever small changeset each cycle touches, which is usually a much smaller fraction. So incremental tables can typically tolerate a longer gap between VACUUM runs without the storage bloat becoming severe as fast — but "slower growth" still means unbounded growth eventually, just on a longer timeline. One partial exception: MERGE-based incremental loads (as opposed to pure append) create write amplification — touching one row forces rewriting its whole file — so merge-heavy tables can actually generate meaningful dead-file churn per cycle too, just distributed differently than a truncate-and-reload.

**The bigger reason "never" doesn't hold, though, is the same one from last turn: your real recoverability is capped by automatic log retention (~30 days by default) regardless of load pattern.** Skipping VACUUM doesn't get you genuine indefinite recovery — it gets you unbounded storage growth for data that becomes unreachable via time travel once the log entry for that version ages out anyway. Load pattern doesn't change that ceiling; it only changes how fast you get to "paying for storage you can no longer use."

**If the appeal of "never vacuum" is actually about preserving a real historical/audit trail of incremental changes** — rather than just an operational safety net against mistakes — that's worth calling out directly, because Delta's internal versioning is the wrong tool for that regardless of retention settings. It's designed as a short-to-medium-term recovery mechanism, not a durable change-history record, and it's capped by log retention no matter what you do. If you genuinely need to preserve how a table changed over time indefinitely, the standard approach is a dedicated history/audit table — an SCD Type 2-style pattern, or an explicit append-only change log — that stores that history as ordinary rows in a live table, independent of VACUUM or log-retention settings entirely, rather than relying on avoiding table maintenance to accidentally preserve it.

Practical takeaway: incremental tables can probably run on a less aggressive VACUUM cadence than your truncate-and-reload tables, given the slower accumulation — but they still want a deliberate retention window and a scheduled run, for the same reasons as before.

---

**You**

If I'm incrementally loading each day which creates a new parquet file with the new data, and run a vacuum operation which deletes old files, how do I avoid deleting old data?

---

**Claude**

Good question to ask, because the mental model of "VACUUM deletes old files" is easy to misread as "VACUUM might delete old *data*" — and for a pure append pattern, it structurally can't.

**The key fact: VACUUM only deletes files that are no longer referenced by the table's current version.** It never touches a file that's still part of what the table currently contains. If your incremental load is genuinely additive — each day writes a new file containing that day's new rows, and nothing ever updates, deletes, or overwrites a previous day's rows — then every file from every day remains part of the current version *forever*. There's nothing for VACUUM to clean up related to that data at all, regardless of how old it is. A file only becomes eligible for VACUUM deletion once something else has logically superseded it first.

**What actually makes an old file eligible for deletion:**
- **Running OPTIMIZE/compaction on the table** — which is likely relevant to you, since we discussed this as the right practice for incremental tables to fight small-file accumulation. Compaction takes several small daily files and consolidates them into fewer, larger ones. The old small files get marked superseded — but the *data* isn't gone, it's just been repackaged into the new compacted file, which is still part of the current version. VACUUM cleaning up the old small files afterward deletes redundant physical copies, not data.
- **An actual UPDATE, DELETE, MERGE, or overwrite that changes previously-loaded rows** — i.e., if you ever go back and correct or reprocess a past day rather than only ever adding new days. In that case, the *correction operation itself* is what logically replaces the old data (the new file becomes the current version, the old one becomes superseded) — VACUUM just physically deletes the now-orphaned old file afterward. The data was already superseded before VACUUM ever touched it.

So the practical answer to "how do I avoid deleting old data" is: **if your load is truly append-only, you don't need to do anything special — it's safe by construction, since VACUUM can't remove files that are still part of the current table.** The place retention actually matters is around compaction or corrections specifically: if you run OPTIMIZE (or reprocess a past day) and then discover a mistake in that operation, you want enough retention window left to time-travel back to the pre-compaction/pre-correction state before VACUUM deletes those superseded files for good. That's the scenario the 7-day default is protecting you against — not your routine daily appends.

Worth confirming for your own case: does your incremental process ever go back and reprocess/correct a previously-loaded day, or is it strictly "today's new rows get added, nothing before today is ever touched"? If it's the latter, VACUUM on this table is close to a non-issue for data safety — it'll mostly just be cleaning up whatever OPTIMIZE leaves behind.

---

**You**

Ok.  So incrementally loaded (append-only, etc...) tables need Optimize.  I can run Vacuum on all tables, but it'll only help with tables that aren't append-only.  Is that right?

If so, what's the command to run vacuum on the whole lakehouse?

---

**Claude**

Close, but the distinction isn't really "append-only vs. not" — it's whether anything has actually created superseded files on that table. And since you're planning to run OPTIMIZE on your append-only tables specifically (to fight the small-file fragmentation from daily appends), VACUUM absolutely will have something to do there too.

Here's the more accurate version: VACUUM only has work to do once *something* — OPTIMIZE, or an UPDATE/DELETE/MERGE/overwrite — has superseded old physical files. For your truncate-and-reload tables, that "something" is the reload itself (each one orphans the whole previous copy). For your append-only tables, that "something" is OPTIMIZE — when it compacts a bunch of small daily files into fewer larger ones, the old small files become superseded, and *that's* what VACUUM cleans up. So it's not that VACUUM skips append-only tables — it's that VACUUM on an append-only table only has debris to remove *after* OPTIMIZE has run on it. If you had a table that was purely append-only and never optimized and never touched any other way, VACUUM would genuinely be a no-op there — but that's not your actual plan, since OPTIMIZE is going on those tables too. Net: VACUUM is useful across all your tables once your maintenance plan (OPTIMIZE + VACUUM together) is actually running.

**On the command:** there isn't a native single command to VACUUM an entire lakehouse at once — same as OPTIMIZE, VACUUM is inherently per-table in Delta Lake/Spark SQL syntax. The standard approach is a notebook that enumerates the tables and loops:

```python
tables = spark.sql(f"SHOW TABLES IN `{lakehouse_name}`.`{schema_name}`").collect()

for t in tables:
    spark.sql(f"VACUUM `{schema_name}`.`{t.tableName}` RETAIN 168 HOURS")
```

For speed across many tables, you can parallelize this with `concurrent.futures`/`ThreadPoolExecutor` so the loop issues several VACUUM calls concurrently rather than strictly one at a time. There's also a third-party open-source library (DiscoverX) that wraps this into a single convenience call across many tables — worth knowing it exists, but it's an external tool, not a native Fabric/Delta feature, so I'd vet it before building production maintenance around it rather than the plain loop above.

Sources:
- [Fabric Optimize and Vacuum Your Lakehouse Tables - MSSQLTips](https://www.mssqltips.com/sqlservertip/7989/microsoft-fabric-lakehouse-optimize-and-vacuum-for-table-maintenance/)
- [Automate Lakehouse Table Maintenance In Fabric - Kevin of Tech](https://www.kevinoftech.com/Blog/Post/2025-10-14-automate-ms-fabric-lh-maintenance)
- [Maintaining Microsoft Fabric Lakehouse Tables - Ian Barker](https://www.ianbarker.net/quick-byte-automating-your-microsoft-fabric-lakehouse-maintenance-programatically/)
- [Mastering Microsoft Fabric – Vacuum command - Data Bear](https://databear.com/microsoft-fabric-vacuum/)

---

**You**

My current lakehouse doesn't have schemas.  Let's start with a script that lists all the tables.

---

**Claude**

Since there's no schema layer, tables just live in the Lakehouse's default namespace — `spark.catalog.listTables()` (or plain `SHOW TABLES`) picks them up directly once the notebook has that Lakehouse attached, no schema qualifier needed.

```python
# Requires the target Lakehouse to be attached to this notebook (left pane > Add lakehouse)

tables = spark.catalog.listTables()
table_names = [t.name for t in tables]

print(f"Found {len(table_names)} table(s):")
for name in table_names:
    print(f" - {name}")
```

Equivalent Spark SQL version if you'd rather work from SQL directly:

```python
display(spark.sql("SHOW TABLES"))
```

The `spark.catalog.listTables()` version is the one worth building on for the next step — it gives you real `Table` objects (with `.name`, `.database`, etc.) rather than a display-only result, so `table_names` is a plain Python list you can feed straight into the OPTIMIZE/VACUUM loop from earlier once you're ready to wire that up.

One thing to confirm before relying on this for maintenance: make sure `table_names` only contains the tables you actually want maintained — `listTables()` will pick up anything registered in the Lakehouse's default namespace, so if there's ever a view or a table you don't want OPTIMIZE/VACUUM run against, you'd filter it out here before looping.

---

**You**

This query has taken 5 minutes so far.  Why would it take so long to list all the tables, when opening the Lakehouse itself shows them in two seconds?

---

**Claude**

Almost certainly Spark session startup, not the actual query. The Lakehouse Explorer UI doesn't use Spark at all — it's a lightweight metadata browse straight against the Lakehouse's catalog, so it responds in seconds regardless of table count. A notebook cell, by contrast, has to provision an entire distributed Spark cluster/session before it can run *any* code — even something as trivial as listing table names pays that full startup cost first.

Per Microsoft's own community docs, typical cold-start ranges are:
- **Starter Pool, no extra config**: ~4-5 seconds — the cluster/session essentially already exists.
- **Custom pool, or added Spark properties/extra libraries, or private endpoint involved**: ~2-5 minutes cold start.

Your ~5 minutes lines up squarely with the second case. So this is very likely just session provisioning overhead, not anything wrong with your Lakehouse or the script.

**The way to confirm this:** run the exact same cell (or any trivial one, like `spark.range(10).count()`) again in the *same* notebook session, without restarting it. If this is cold-start overhead, the second run should come back in seconds. If it's still slow on a second run, something else is going on (worth checking the Fabric Notebooks troubleshooting guide at that point rather than assuming it's cold start).

One practical implication for your maintenance script going forward: this cold-start cost gets paid every time a *fresh* session spins up — including if this eventually runs as a scheduled Notebook activity in a pipeline rather than an interactive session you keep warm. That's an inherent property of Spark notebooks in Fabric, not something specific to `listTables()` — worth factoring into how you think about scheduling cadence for the OPTIMIZE/VACUUM maintenance job once you get there.

Sources:
- [Re: Starting Session takes really slow - Microsoft Fabric Community](https://community.fabric.microsoft.com/t5/Data-Engineering/Starting-Session-takes-really-slow-notebook-session-Microsoft/m-p/4011622)
- [Slow starter pool session initialization - Microsoft Fabric Community](https://community.fabric.microsoft.com/t5/Fabric-platform/Slow-starter-pool-session-initialization/m-p/3507465)
- [Solved: startup times of the Spark cluster in notebooks - Microsoft Fabric Community](https://community.fabric.microsoft.com/t5/Data-Engineering/startup-times-of-the-Spark-cluster-in-notebooks/m-p/4334329)
- [How does Fabric make Spark Notebooks Instant? - Microsoft Fabric Blog](https://blog.fabric.microsoft.com/en-US/blog/how-fabric-makes-spark-notebook-feel-instant-proactive-resource-provisioning-for-scalable-data-science-data-engineering/)
- [Fabric Notebooks troubleshooting guide - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-science/fabric-notebooks-troubleshooting-guide)

---

**You**

Ok. With that list now generated, how do I run vacuum against it?

---

**Claude**

Building on the list you already have:

**Recommended: preview first with DRY RUN**, so you can see what would be deleted before anything actually happens — worth doing the first time given everything we've discussed about retention and recoverability:

```python
for name in table_names:
    print(f"--- {name} ---")
    display(spark.sql(f"VACUUM `{name}` RETAIN 168 HOURS DRY RUN"))
```

This lists the files each table's VACUUM *would* delete, without deleting anything. Once you've eyeballed that and it looks reasonable, run the real thing:

```python
for name in table_names:
    try:
        print(f"Vacuuming {name}...")
        spark.sql(f"VACUUM `{name}` RETAIN 168 HOURS")
        print(f"Done: {name}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"FAILED: {name} — {e}")
```

A few notes on this:
- Backticks around the table name protect against any names with spaces or special characters — relevant given some of your dataflow-generated table names go through sanitization already.
- `RETAIN 168 HOURS` is the same as the default, but writing it explicitly keeps the retention policy visible in code rather than implicit — makes it a deliberate, reviewable choice rather than "whatever the default happens to be."
- The `try/except` means one table failing (permissions, a locked file, whatever) doesn't kill the run for the rest of the list — you'll get a printed failure list at the end instead of a dead script partway through.
- No schema prefix needed, consistent with your Lakehouse not using schemas.

If you want OPTIMIZE folded into the same pass (since you'll likely want both running together per the earlier discussion), it'd just be another `spark.sql(f"OPTIMIZE \`{name}\` VORDER")` call ahead of the VACUUM call in the same loop.

---

**You**

That display job is returning a bunch of tables with one column each, showing a list of parquet files.  I'm not sure what I'm supposed to get out of reviewing this, since there's no information about what each table is for (does it correspond to a Lakehouse table?), or what is contained in each of the parquet files.

---

**Claude**

That confusion is warranted — the dry-run output genuinely doesn't give you what you might expect, and it's worth being clear about what it actually is versus what it isn't.

**What `VACUUM ... DRY RUN` returns:** a single column (typically `path`) listing the full storage paths of files that would be deleted — that's the entire output format by design. It doesn't include row counts, content previews, timestamps in a friendly form, or a labeled "table" column — it's purely a flat list of file paths that Delta has already determined are no longer referenced by the current version and are past the retention window.

**Table identity isn't missing, it's just implicit in two places:**
1. Your loop prints `--- {name} ---` before each `display()` call, so the files listed right under a given header belong to that table — the association comes from the loop structure, not a column in the output itself.
2. The file paths themselves normally embed the table's folder name (Delta stores each table as its own folder), so if you look at the actual path text — something like `.../Tables/RPC_ERP_Orders_Hourly/part-00000-....parquet` — the table is identifiable from the path even without the print grouping.

**What you're not going to get, and don't actually need:** there's no way to see "what's contained in" each file from a dry run, and that's not a gap worth filling — it doesn't read file contents, only enumerates candidates. More importantly, there's nothing to protect by inspecting content here: by the time a file shows up in this list at all, Delta has already determined it's superseded and no longer part of the table's current version — that determination happened when the file was orphaned (by a reload, a merge, or an OPTIMIZE compaction), not now. The dry run isn't a safety gate against deleting live data; that protection already happened upstream. The retention window is your actual safety net (time to notice a mistake before physical deletion), not manual review of this list.

**What's actually worth checking in a dry run**, then, is really just a volume sanity check: does the *number* of files per table look roughly like what you'd expect given its refresh cadence and retention window? For example, an hourly truncate-and-reload table with 7-day retention should show something in the ballpark of a week's worth of orphaned generations — if a table you expected to have almost nothing (say, a pure append-only table you've never run OPTIMIZE on) shows up with a big list, that's a signal something unexpected happened to it (maybe it got compacted or modified in a way you didn't anticipate) and is worth investigating before running the real VACUUM — not because you'd find something wrong by reading file contents, but because an unexpected *count* tells you your assumptions about that table's history might be off.

If what you actually want is to confirm a table's *current* live data looks right (separate question from VACUUM entirely), that's a different check — query the table directly (`SELECT COUNT(*) FROM table`, spot-check recent rows) — not something the dry-run file list can tell you either way.

---

**You**

Actually, the table it's being run for is being shown above the table.

---

**Claude**

Right — that confirms it's working as expected: the `print(f"--- {name} ---")` header is what's associating each file list with its table, exactly as described. The dry-run output itself doesn't carry the table name inline; it's just relying on that print statement above it for context.

Given that's confirmed, if the file counts you saw looked reasonable for each table's refresh cadence, you're in good shape to swap in the real VACUUM loop from a couple messages back whenever you're ready.

---

**You**

It's showing exactly 1000 parquet files for each lakehouse table.  Does that imply a retention limit, or a limit on what's returned for that query?

---

**Claude**

That's the notebook UI's display cap, not anything about retention or VACUUM itself. Fabric notebooks apply a default 1,000-row limit to `display()` output to keep rendering fast — it's a visualization ceiling, not a property of the actual query result. The fact that it's *exactly* 1000 across every table, regardless of each table's different age/refresh history, is the tell — genuine eligible-file counts wouldn't coincidentally land on the same round number for multiple different tables.

To get the real count, bypass `display()` and call `.count()` directly on the underlying DataFrame:

```python
for name in table_names:
    df = spark.sql(f"VACUUM `{name}` RETAIN 168 HOURS DRY RUN")
    print(f"{name}: {df.count()} file(s) eligible for deletion")
```

That gives you the true total regardless of size. If you actually want to *see* more than 1000 rows in the notebook output rather than just the count, `display()` supports a `row_limit` parameter to raise the cap — up to a hard ceiling of 10,000 rows or 5MB, whichever hits first:

```python
display(df, row_limit=10000)
```

For your purposes (sanity-checking file counts per table before running the real VACUUM), the `.count()` version is the more useful one — you don't need to see every path, just the true number.

Sources:
- [Overcome limitation of 1000 rows for plot - Microsoft Fabric Community](https://community.fabric.microsoft.com/t5/Data-Engineering/Overcome-limitation-of-1000-rows-for-plot/m-p/4777647)
- [Notebook visualization - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/notebook-visualization)

---

**You**

Why would a query against a truncated-and-reloaded table look at any parquet file other than the latest one?

---

**Claude**

Your instinct here is exactly right, and it's worth stating plainly: a normal query against a truncate-and-reload table **only ever reads the files that make up the current version** — it doesn't scan, filter through, or even look at the superseded files from previous reload cycles at all.

The mechanism: Delta's transaction log maintains an explicit manifest of exactly which files constitute the *current* version. When you query the table normally, the engine consults that manifest directly and reads only those files — the old, orphaned files from prior reloads aren't something it has to skip past or filter out, they're simply never referenced. They're invisible to a normal query, not slow-but-present.

That means the 1,000+ dead files you're seeing per table in the VACUUM dry run are **not** slowing down your regular queries at all. Their only real cost is storage — you're paying to keep files around that nothing reads — plus possibly some minor overhead in the storage layer's own file-listing operations if a directory accumulates a very large number of objects, which is a much smaller and less certain effect than an actual query scan cost. This is worth being precise about versus what I said earlier hedging on "possible secondary file-listing performance impact" — the core query path genuinely doesn't touch these files, full stop.

The one exception: an explicit time-travel query — `SELECT * FROM table VERSION AS OF n` or `TIMESTAMP AS OF '...'` — deliberately asking for an *old* version would need to read the old files that made up that specific version. But that's a deliberate, explicit ask; it's not what happens during ordinary use of the table.

So the actual case for running VACUUM on your truncate-and-reload tables is purely storage cost reclamation (plus the retention/recoverability trade-off we covered earlier) — not query performance. That's a genuinely different justification than OPTIMIZE, which *does* directly affect query performance, since it changes the file layout of the currently-active version itself.

**You**

Also, can you give me a website reference for what vacuum does, and why it's important to run against truncated and reloaded tables?

---

**Claude**

Here are the most relevant references, prioritized for your specific angle:

**Primary — Microsoft's own Fabric documentation on VACUUM:**
[VACUUM Delta tables - Microsoft Fabric | Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/delta-lake-vacuum) — the authoritative source for syntax, default retention, and safety considerations on your actual platform.

**Most directly on-point for your truncate-and-reload question:**
[VACUUM in Delta Lake — "whenever we do Overwrite..." by Harun Raseed Basheer](https://medium.com/@harun.raseed093/vacuum-in-delta-lake-56cc374662ea) — this one is framed specifically around the overwrite scenario, which is exactly your case.

**General canonical explanation (vendor-neutral, from the Delta Lake project itself):**
[Remove old files with the Delta Lake Vacuum Command - delta.io](https://delta.io/blog/remove-files-delta-lake-vacuum-command/)

One thing this search reconfirmed worth keeping in your back pocket if you ever write this up for your team: "vacuuming can only help you save on storage costs — it doesn't improve query performance." That's a direct, independent confirmation of what we just worked through — VACUUM's value for your truncate-and-reload tables is storage reclamation (and the retention/recoverability trade-off), not query speed. If you want a source specifically for the query-performance side (OPTIMIZE's territory instead), the same Fabric docs site has a companion page: [Delta table maintenance in Microsoft Fabric - Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-engineering/delta-lake-table-maintenance), which covers OPTIMIZE and VACUUM side by side.

---

**You**

Are you saying that Fabric charges for strorage, not just memory or compute?

---

**Claude**

Yes — OneLake storage is billed as its own separate line item, distinct from capacity/compute. This directly confirms that "storage cost" as a reason to run VACUUM is a real, tangible dollar figure, not just a theoretical concern.

Fabric's billing model has two genuinely separate components:
- **Capacity (compute)** — your F-SKU (F2, F64, etc.), billed by Capacity Units consumed by Spark jobs, Warehouse queries, dataflow refreshes, pipeline activities, and so on.
- **OneLake storage** — billed separately, pay-as-you-go, at roughly **$0.023/GB/month** (about $23/TB/month), based on actual bytes sitting in OneLake — including every un-vacuumed dead Parquet file from your truncate-and-reload tables. This keeps accruing regardless of whether your capacity is even running.

One nuance I'd hold loosely rather than take at face value: there's some indication that larger capacity tiers (F64+) come with a free storage allowance, but that appears tied specifically to the Mirroring feature's storage limit rather than a blanket "your capacity size includes free general storage" rule — I don't have confident enough detail on its exact scope to say whether it'd cover your Lakehouse/Warehouse landing tables too, so I wouldn't factor that into any cost planning without verifying it directly against your specific capacity tier.

The concrete takeaway for your case: at scale, thousands of orphaned files across multiple tables sitting un-vacuumed for weeks is a real, metered cost accumulating independent of anything else — not large per table, but it's genuine money, which is a stronger practical case for actually scheduling VACUUM than "it's good hygiene."
